LCE=−1m∑i=1m∑k=1K1[yi=k]log⁡p^ik\mathcal{L$\text{CE}} = -\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^m \sum_{k=1}^K \mathbf{1}[y_i = k] \log \hat{p$ik}LCE=−m1i=1∑mk=1∑K1[yi=k]logp^ik

The equivalence is exact: minimizing cross-entropy over the training set is identical to maximizing the log-likelihood under a categorical noise model.

**What this means for neural networks.** Every classification neural network trained with cross-entropy loss — from a logistic regression to a ResNet to a transformer — is implicitly performing maximum likelihood estimation. The network's softmax outputs are probability estimates, and the training objective is to find weights that make the observed labels as probable as possible under the model's categorical distribution.

This has several important consequences. First, it explains _why_ we use cross-entropy rather than, say, MSE for classification: cross-entropy is the statistically principled loss derived from the distributional assumption. Using MSE would correspond to assuming Gaussian noise in label space, which is incoherent for categorical outputs. Second, it means that the model's output probabilities have a clear frequentist interpretation: a well-calibrated model that outputs $\hat{p} = 0.8$ for class $k$ should be correct 80% of the time on examples where it gives that confidence. Third, MLE estimates are asymptotically efficient (they achieve the Cramér-Rao lower bound) — so cross-entropy is not just convenient, it is statistically optimal as training data grows.

### Chapter Summary

This chapter built the probabilistic toolkit essential for understanding _why_ ML algorithms are designed the way they are. Key ideas:

* **Bayes' theorem** provides the principled way to update beliefs from data: $P(H|D) \propto P(D|H) \cdot P(H)$
* **Random variables and distributions** — especially Bernoulli, Gaussian, and Categorical — describe the stochastic structure of data and model outputs
* **Maximum likelihood estimation** unifies the derivation of loss functions: MSE comes from Gaussian noise; cross-entropy comes from Bernoulli/Categorical noise
* **Covariance and the covariance matrix** quantify how features co-vary, providing the foundation for PCA (Chapter 13)

In Chapter 4, we add the final mathematical tool: calculus and optimization. This will tell us _how to find_ the maximum likelihood estimates — by computing gradients and descending them.

Together, Chapters 2–4 form the mathematical foundation for the entire book. Chapter 5 is where we build our first ML model, and you'll see all three mathematical pillars — linear algebra, probability, and calculus — working together.

---

## Chapter 4: Mathematical Foundations $I$ — Calculus and Optimization

We now know how to represent data (linear algebra) and how to model uncertainty (probability). But there's one question we haven't answered: once we've decided what we want to minimize (a loss function), how do we actually _find_ the minimum?

The answer is calculus. Specifically, we need to compute _gradients_ — the multi-dimensional generalization of the derivative — and then follow them downhill toward the minimum. This process, called gradient descent, is the beating heart of how nearly every modern ML model is trained.

This is also the chapter where a single idea from high school calculus — the chain rule — turns out to be the key that makes training neural networks (billions of parameters!) computationally feasible. That connection, which we'll make explicit in Chapter 15, is called backpropagation.

### 4.1 Derivatives: The Rate of Change

#### Definition: Derivative

The **derivative** of a function f:R→Rf: \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}f:R→R at point $x$ is:

f′(x)=dfdx=lim⁡h→0f(x+h)−f(x)hf'(x) = \frac{df}{dx} = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h}f′(x)=dxdf=h→0limhf(x+h)−f(x)

In plain language, the derivative measures how much $f(x)$ changes when $x$ increases by a tiny amount. If f′(x)>0f'(x) > 0f′(x)>0, the function is increasing at $x$. If f′(x)<0f'(x) < 0f′(x)<0, it's decreasing. If f′(x)=0f'(x) = 0f′(x)=0, it's flat — a potential minimum or maximum.

Key derivatives you need to know:

* ddx$\frac{d}{dx}[c] = 0dxd$ (constant)
* ddx$\frac{d}{dx}[x^n] = nx^{n-1}dxd$ (power rule)
* ddx$\frac{d}{dx}[e^x] = e^xdxd$
* ddx[log⁡$\frac{d}{dx}[\log x] = 1/xdxd[log$
* ddx[sin⁡x]=cos⁡x\frac{d}{dx}[\sin x] = \cos xdxd[sinx]=cosx

#### The Chain Rule

The most important differentiation rule for machine learning:

ddx$\frac{d}{dx}[f(g(x))] = f'(g(x)) \cdot g'(x)dxd$

Or in Leibniz notation: if y=f(u)y = f(u)y=f(u) and u=g(x)u = g(x)u=g(x), then dydx=dydu⋅dudx\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{dy}{du} \cdot \frac{du}{dx}dxdy=dudy⋅dxdu.

In plain language: the rate of change of a composition equals the product of the rates at each step.

**Example:** Suppose f(x)=(3x+1)5f(x) = (3x + 1)^5f(x)=(3x+1)5. Let u=3x+1u = 3x + 1u=3x+1, so f=u5f = u^5f=u5. Then:

dfdx$\frac{df}{dx} = 5u^4 \cdot 3 = 15(3x+1)^4dxdf$

The chain rule becomes the chain rule of _computation_ in backpropagation: a neural network is a composition of many functions, and backpropagation systematically applies the chain rule layer by layer to compute how the loss depends on every weight.

#### Common Misconception

Students often think the chain rule applies only to "nested" functions like (3x+1)5(3x+1)^5(3x+1)5. In fact, it applies whenever you have a function of a function — including the entire forward pass of a neural network, which computes loss=L(fn(fn−1(⋯f1(x)⋯ )))\text{loss} = L(f_{n}(f_{n-1}(\cdots f_1(\mathbf{x}) \cdots)))loss=L(fn(fn−1(⋯f1(x)⋯))). The chain rule is what allows us to compute $\partial L / \partial \mathbf{w}_k$ for any layer $k$ efficiently.

### 4.2 Partial Derivatives and Gradients

Most ML loss functions depend on many parameters simultaneously. We need to differentiate with respect to each one.

#### Definition: Partial Derivative

For a function f:Rn→Rf: \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}f:Rn→R, the **partial derivative** with respect to xix_ixi is:

∂f∂xi=lim⁡h→0f(x1,…,xi+h,…,xn)−f(x1,…,xn)h\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_i} = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{$\ldots, x_i + h, \ldots, x_n) -$ \ldots, x_n)}{h}∂xi∂f=h→0limhf(x1,…,xi+h,…,xn)−f(x1,…,xn)

In plain language: hold all other variables constant and differentiate with respect to xix_ixi alone.

#### Definition: Gradient

The **gradient** of f:Rn→Rf: \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}f:Rn→R at point $\mathbf{x}$ is the vector of all partial derivatives:

∇xf=(∂f/∂x1∂f/∂x2⋮∂f/∂xn)\nabla_\mathbf{x} f = \begin{pmatrix} \partial f / \partial x_1 \\\ \partial f / \partial x_2 \\\ \vdots \\\ \partial f / \partial x_n \end{pmatrix}∇xf=∂f/∂x1∂f/∂x2⋮∂f/∂xn

In plain language, the gradient is a vector that points in the direction of _steepest ascent_ of $f$. Its magnitude tells you how steep that ascent is.

**Example:** For the MSE loss f(w)=∥y−Xw∥2=∑i=1m(yi−xi⊤w)$\mathbf{w}) = \|\mathbf{y} - X\mathbf{w}\|^2 = \sum_{i=1}^m (y_i - \mathbf{x}_i^\top \mathbf{w})^$w)=∥y−Xw∥2=∑i=1m(yi−xi⊤w)2:

$\nabla_\mathbf{w} f = -2X^\top(\mathbf{y} - X\mathbf{w})$

Setting this to zero gives the normal equations X⊤Xw=X⊤yX^\top X \mathbf{w} = X^\top \mathbf{y}X⊤Xw=X⊤y, which we'll solve in Chapter 5.

**Key gradient rules** (analogs of scalar differentiation, but now for vectors):

* $\nabla_\mathbf{x}[\mathbf{a}^\top \mathbf{x}] = \mathbf{a}$
* $\nabla_\mathbf{x}[\mathbf{x}^\top A \mathbf{x}] = (A + A^\top)\mathbf{x}$ (for symmetric $A$: 2Ax2A\mathbf{x}2Ax)
* $\nabla_\mathbf{x}\|A\mathbf{x} - \mathbf{b}\|^2 = 2A^\top(A\mathbf{x} - \mathbf{b})$

### 4.3 Gradient Descent: Following the Downhill Direction

#### Definition: Gradient Descent

**Gradient descent** iteratively updates parameters to minimize a loss function $L(\boldsymbol{\theta})$:

$\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t+1} = \boldsymbol{\theta}_t - \eta \nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}} L(\boldsymbol{\theta}_t)$

In plain language: at each step, take a small step in the _opposite_ direction of the gradient (since the gradient points uphill, we go downhill).

The scalar $\eta > 0$ is called the **learning rate** (or step size). It controls how large each step is.

**Geometric intuition:** Imagine you're standing on a hilly landscape in thick fog and want to find the lowest point. You can't see the overall landscape, but you can feel the slope under your feet. The gradient descent strategy: always step in the downhill direction. If the learning rate is too large, you might overshoot the valley and end up on the other side. If it's too small, it takes forever to get there.

**A concrete example:** Minimize f(w)=(w−3)2f(w) = (w - 3)^2f(w)=(w−3)2.

The gradient is f′(w)=2(w−3)f'(w) = 2(w-3)f′(w)=2(w−3). Starting at $w_0 = 0$ with learning rate $\eta = 0.1$:

w1$=0.6w_1 = 0 - 0.1 \cdot 2(0-3) = 0 + 0.6 = 0.6w1$=0.6 w2$=0.6+0.48=1.08w_2 = 0.6 - 0.1 \cdot 2(0.6-3) = 0.6 + 0.48 = 1.08w2$=0.6+0.48=1.08 w3$=1.08+0.384=1.464w_3 = 1.08 - 0.1 \cdot 2(1.08-3) = 1.08 + 0.384 = 1.464w3$=1.08+0.384=1.464

Each step moves closer to the minimum at w∗=3w^* = 3w∗=3. The updates get smaller as we approach the minimum (because the gradient gets smaller near a flat point).

#### Choosing the Learning Rate

The learning rate is perhaps the most important hyperparameter in practice.

* **Too large ( η\etaη too high):** Updates overshoot the minimum. The loss oscillates or even diverges ("explodes").
* **Too small ( η\etaη too low):** Training takes very long; may get stuck near the starting point.
* **Just right:** Converges smoothly to (near) the minimum.

In practice, learning rates in the range [10−4,10−1][10^{-4}, 10^{-1}][10−4,10−1] are common. Modern optimizers like Adam (Chapter 18) adapt the learning rate automatically, which greatly reduces the sensitivity.

#### Variants of Gradient Descent

**Batch gradient descent:** Compute the gradient on the _entire_ dataset before each update. Accurate but very slow for large datasets — you need to process all $m$ examples before taking even a single step.

**Stochastic gradient descent (SGD):** Compute the gradient on a _single randomly chosen example_ at each step. Very fast but noisy — each step may not go in exactly the right direction.

**Mini-batch gradient descent:** Compute the gradient on a small random subset (_mini-batch_) of $B$ examples (typically B=32,64,128,256B = 32, 64, 128, 256B=32,64,128,256). The standard in modern deep learning — balances accuracy of the gradient estimate with computational efficiency.

θt+1=θt−η⋅1B∑i∈Bt∇θLi(θt)\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t+1} = \boldsymbol{\theta}_t - \eta \cdot \frac{1}{B} \sum_{i \in \mathcal{B}_t} \nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}} L_i(\boldsymbol{\theta}_t)θt+1=θt−η⋅B1i∈Bt∑∇θLi(θt)

The noise in SGD turns out to be _beneficial_ — it acts as a form of implicit regularization and helps escape sharp local minima (more on this in Chapter 18).

#### Common Misconception

Many students assume that gradient descent always converges to the global minimum. In general, it only converges to a _local minimum_ (a point where the gradient is zero but which may not be globally optimal). For convex functions (Section 4.4), all local minima are global, so this isn't a concern. For non-convex functions like neural network loss landscapes, convergence to a local minimum is not guaranteed to be the global minimum — yet in practice, deep learning models trained with SGD find solutions that generalize very well. Why? This is one of the deepest open questions in ML theory.

### 4.4 Convexity: When Local = Global

#### Definition: Convex Function

A function f:Rn→Rf: \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}f:Rn→R is **convex** if for any two points x,y\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{y}x,y and any t∈[0,$\in [0,$∈[0,1]:

f(tx+(1−t)y)≤tf(x)+(1−t)f(y)f(t\mathbf{x} + ($\mathbf{y}) \leq t f(\mathbf{x}) + ($ f(\mathbf{y})f(tx+(1−t)y)≤tf(x)+(1−t)f(y)

In plain language: the function "curves upward." The line segment between any two points on the graph lies _above_ the graph. A convex function looks like a bowl.

For a twice-differentiable function f:R→Rf: \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}f:R→R, convexity is equivalent to f′′(x)≥0f''(x) \geq 0f′′(x)≥0 everywhere. For a multivariable function, convexity is equivalent to the Hessian matrix H=∇2fH = \nabla^2 fH=∇2f being positive semi-definite everywhere.

**Why convexity matters:** For convex functions, any local minimum is a global minimum. Gradient descent is guaranteed to converge to the global minimum (for appropriate learning rates).

**Examples in ML:**

* MSE loss for linear regression: convex in $\mathbf{w}$ ✓
* Cross-entropy loss for logistic regression: convex ✓
* Neural network loss: _not_ convex ✗ (multiple local minima exist)

The transition from Chapters 5–8 (convex optimization problems) to Chapters 14–21 (non-convex deep learning) is one of the major intellectual jumps in the course.

#### The Second-Order Condition

The **Hessian matrix** is the matrix of second-order partial derivatives:

Hij=∂2f∂xi∂xjH_{ij} = \frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial x_i \partial x_j}Hij=∂xi∂xj∂2f

At a critical point (where $\nabla f = \mathbf{0}$):

* If $H$ is positive definite: local minimum
* If $H$ is negative definite: local maximum
* If $H$ has both positive and negative eigenvalues: saddle point

For large neural networks, computing the full Hessian is computationally infeasible (a network with $n$ parameters has an n×nn \times nn×n Hessian). Modern optimization methods (Chapter 18) use approximations.

### 4.5 Optimization Landscape of Neural Networks

Since we'll spend many later chapters training neural networks, it's worth previewing what their optimization landscape looks like.

A neural network loss function L(w)L(\mathbf{w})L(w) as a function of its parameters $\mathbf{w}$ is highly non-convex. It has:

* Many local minima (some good, some bad)
* Saddle points (flat in some directions, curved in others) — research suggests these are more common than local minima
* Flat regions ("plateaus") where gradients are near zero

Despite this complexity, gradient descent (especially with mini-batch noise) tends to find solutions that generalize well. Several explanations have been proposed:

  1. In high dimensions, local minima tend to have similar function values to the global minimum
  2. The noise in SGD helps escape saddle points and sharp minima
  3. Overparameterized networks (more parameters than data) have many equivalent global minima

This is an active area of theoretical research. For now, the empirical fact is clear: gradient descent works remarkably well for deep learning.

### 4.6 Putting It All Together: Gradient Descent for Linear Regression

Let's apply everything from Chapters 2–4 to see how gradient descent trains the simplest ML model.

**Setup:** Linear regression minimizes:

L(w)=1m∑i=1m(yi−xi⊤w)2=1m∥y−Xw∥$\mathbf{w}) = \frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^m (y_i - \mathbf{x}_i^\top \mathbf{w})^2 = \frac{1}{m}\|\mathbf{y} - X\mathbf{w}\|^$w)=m1i=1∑m(yi−xi⊤w)2=m1∥y−Xw∥2

**Computing the gradient:**

∇wL=2mX⊤(Xw−y)\nabla_\mathbf{w} L = \frac{2}{m} X^\top (X\mathbf{w} - \mathbf{y})∇wL=m2X⊤(Xw−y)

This uses the matrix gradient rules from Section 4.2. Each step:

wt+1=wt−η⋅2mX⊤(Xwt−y)\mathbf{w}_{t+1} = \mathbf{w}_t - \eta \cdot \frac{2}{m} X^\top (X\mathbf{w}_t - \mathbf{y})wt+1=wt−η⋅m2X⊤(Xwt−y)

The gradient X⊤(Xw−y)X^\top(X\mathbf{w} - \mathbf{y})X⊤(Xw−y) has a beautiful interpretation:

* XwX\mathbf{w}Xw is the vector of predictions y^\hat{\mathbf{y}}y^
* Xw−yX\mathbf{w} - \mathbf{y}Xw−y is the vector of _residuals_ (errors)
* X⊤(y^−y)X^\top (\hat{\mathbf{y}} - \mathbf{y})X⊤(y^−y) is a weighted sum of feature vectors, weighted by how wrong the prediction is for each example

Large residuals (big errors) contribute more to the gradient, so the update focuses on fixing the biggest mistakes first. This is an intuitive and elegant property.

### 4.7 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** Compute the derivative of f(x)=3x4−2x2+5log⁡(x)−7f(x) = 3x^4 - 2x^2 + 5\log(x) - 7f(x)=3x4−2x2+5log(x)−7 using the standard rules.

**Answer:**

f′(x)=3⋅4x3−2⋅2x+5⋅1x−0=12x3−4x+5xf'(x) = 3 \cdot 4x^3 - 2 \cdot 2x + 5 \cdot \frac{1}{x} - 0 = 12x^3 - 4x + \frac{5}{x}f′(x)=3⋅4x3−2⋅2x+5⋅x1−0=12x3−4x+x5

**Q2.** Use the chain rule to differentiate g(x)=log⁡(σ(x))g(x) = \log(\sigma(x))g(x)=log(σ(x)) where σ(x)=11+e−x\sigma(x) = \frac{1}{1+e^{-x}}σ(x)=1+e−x1 is the sigmoid function.

Hint

First compute σ'(x), then apply the chain rule for log(σ(x)).

**Answer:** First find $\sigma'(x)$. Let u=1+e−xu = 1 + e^{-x}u=1+e−x, so $\sigma = 1/u$.

σ′(x)=−1u2⋅(−e−x)=e−x(1+e−x)2\sigma'(x) = -\frac{1}{u$\cdot (-e^{-x}) = \frac{e^{-x}}{(1+e^{-x})$σ′(x)=−u21⋅(−e−x)=(1+e−x)2e−x

This can be simplified: $\sigma'(x) = \sigma(x)(1 - \sigma(x))$. Verify: e−x(1+e−x)2=11+e−x⋅e−x1+e−x=σ(x)⋅(1−σ(x))\frac{e^{-x}}{(1+e^{-x})^2} = \frac{1$\cdot \frac{e^{-x}$ = \sigma(x) \cdot (1 - \sigma(x))(1+e−x)2e−x=1+e−x1⋅1+e−xe−x=σ(x)⋅(1−σ(x)). ✓

Now apply chain rule to g(x)=log⁡(σ(x))g(x) = \log(\sigma(x))g(x)=log(σ(x)):

g′(x)=1σ(x)⋅σ′(x)=1σ(x)⋅σ(x)(1−σ(x))=1−σ(x)g'(x) = $\cdot \sigma'(x) =$ \cdot \sigma(x)(1-\sigma(x)) = 1 - \sigma(x)g′(x)=σ(x)1⋅σ′(x)=σ(x)1⋅σ(x)(1−σ(x))=1−σ(x)

This is the gradient of the log-sigmoid function — which appears directly in the gradient of the logistic regression loss (Chapter 7). Notice how clean the result is: g′(x)=1−σ(x)g'(x) = 1 - \sigma(x)g′(x)=1−σ(x). If $x$ is large and positive (strong prediction), $\sigma(x) \approx 1$, so g′(x)≈0g'(x) \approx 0g′(x)≈0 — the gradient is near zero, meaning we don't need to update much.

**Q3.** For the function f(w1,w2)=(w1−2)2+3(w2+1)2f(w_1, w_2) = (w_1 - 2)^2 + 3(w_2 + 1)^2f(w1,w2)=(w1−2)2+3(w2+1)2, compute the gradient ∇f\nabla f∇f at the point (1,2)(1, 2)(1,2), and determine the direction gradient descent should step.

**Answer:**

∂f∂w1=2(w1−2),∂f∂w2=6(w2+1)\frac{\partial f}{\partial w_1} = 2(w_1 - 2), \quad \frac{\partial f}{\partial w_2} = 6(w_2 + 1)∂w1∂f=2(w1−2),∂w2∂f=6(w2+1)

At (1,2)(1, 2)(1,2):

$\nabla f(1, 2) = \begin{pmatrix} 2(1-2) \\\ 6(2+1) \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} -2 \\\ 18 \end{pmatrix}$

Gradient descent steps in the _negative gradient_ direction: (−(−2),−(18))=(2,−18)(-(-2), -(18)) = (2, -18)(−(−2),−(18))=(2,−18) (not normalized). So from (1,2)(1, 2)(1,2), we move toward increasing w1w_1w1 and decreasing w2w_2w2 — which makes sense, since the minimum is at (2,−1)(2, -1)(2,−1).

The minimum is where $\nabla f = \mathbf{0}$: $w_1 = 2$, w2=−1w_2 = -1w2=−1. We can verify f(2,−1)=0f(2, -1) = 0f(2,−1)=0, which is indeed the global minimum of this convex function.

**Q4.** Is f(w)=w4−4w2f(w) = w^4 - 4w^2f(w)=w4−4w2 convex? Find all local and global minima.

**Answer:** Compute f′′(w)=12w2−8f''(w) = 12w^2 - 8f′′(w)=12w2−8. At w=0w = 0w=0: f′′(0)=−8<0f''(0) = -8 < 0f′′(0)=−8<0, so $f$ is _not_ convex everywhere (it curves downward at the origin). This is a non-convex function.

Critical points: f′(w)=4w3−8w=4w(w2−2)=0f'(w) = 4w^3 - 8w = 4w(w^2 - 2) = 0f′(w)=4w3−8w=4w(w2−2)=0, so w=0,±2w = 0, \pm\sqrt{2}w=0,±2.

Second derivative at critical points:

* f′′(0)=−8<0f''(0) = -8 < 0f′′(0)=−8<0: local maximum at w=0w = 0w=0
* f′′(±2)=12(2)−8=16>0f''(\pm\sqrt{2}) = 12(2) - 8 = 16 > 0f′′(±2)=12(2)−8=16>0: local minima at w=±2w = \pm\sqrt{2}w=±2

Since $f(w) \to +\infty$ as ∣w∣→∞|w| \to \infty∣w∣→∞, the local minima at ±2\pm\sqrt{2}±2 are also global minima. $f(\pm\sqrt{2}) = 4 - 8 = -4$.

This is a simple example of a non-convex function with two symmetric global minima. Depending on starting point, gradient descent will converge to one or the other — it cannot tell which is "better" since they're equal. For neural networks with many more parameters, the situation is analogous (but far more complex).

#### Application Problems

**Q1.** You're training a linear regression model with m=10m = 10m=10 examples, n=1n = 1n=1 feature, learning rate $\eta = 0.01$. The current weight is $w_0 = 0$, and the gradient is ∂L∂w∣w=0=−12\frac{\partial L}{\partial w}\big|_{w=0} = -12∂w∂Lw=0=−12. Compute the first three gradient descent updates. Will this learning rate cause the loss to converge?

Hint

Check whether the updates are heading in a sensible direction (toward lower loss). The sign of the gradient tells you the direction; the learning rate controls the step size.

**Answer:**

w1=w0−η⋅(−12)=0+0.01×12=0.12w_1 = w_0 - \eta \cdot (-12) = 0 + 0.01 \times 12 = 0.12w1=w0−η⋅(−12)=0+0.01×12=0.12

To compute further updates, we'd need to recompute the gradient at each new $w$. But without the specific dataset, let's reason about convergence.

For the 1D quadratic L(w)=c(w−w∗)2L(w) = c(w - w^*)^2L(w)=c(w−w∗)2, the gradient is dLdw$\frac{dL}{dw} = 2c(w - w^*)dwdL$. Gradient descent gives:

wt+1=wt−η⋅2c(wt−w∗)=w∗+(1−2cη)(wt−w∗)w_{t+1} = w_t - \eta \cdot 2c(w_t - w^*) = w^* + (1 - 2c\eta)(w_t - w^*)wt+1=wt−η⋅2c(wt−w∗)=w∗+(1−2cη)(wt−w∗)

This converges if ∣1−2cη∣<1|1 - 2c\eta| < 1∣1−2cη∣<1, i.e., 0<η<1/c0 < \eta < 1/c0<η<1/c.

For general convergence: the learning rate $\eta = 0.01$ is generally safe for linear regression, but the actual threshold depends on the Lipschitz constant of the gradient (related to the largest eigenvalue of X⊤X/mX^\top X / mX⊤X/m). A learning rate that converges for one dataset might diverge for another with larger feature values — another reason to _normalize features_ (Chapter 22) before training.

**Q2.** The sigmoid function $\sigma(x) = 1/(1+e^{-x})$ appears in logistic regression. Show that its output is always in (0,1)(0, 1)(0,1) and find where its derivative is maximized. Why does this matter for training deep networks?

**Answer:** For any $x$, e−x>0e^{-x} > 0e−x>0, so 1+e−x>11 + e^{-x} > 11+e−x>1, giving $\sigma(x) < 1$. Also 1+e−x>01 + e^{-x} > 01+e−x>0 (bounded below), giving $\sigma(x) > 0$. So $\sigma(x) \in (0, 1)$ for all $x$.

From Q2 in Concept Check: $\sigma'(x) = \sigma(x)(1-\sigma(x))$.

To find the maximum: let s=σ(x)∈(0,1)s = \sigma(x) \in (0,1)s=σ(x)∈(0,1). We maximize s(1−s)s(1-s)s(1−s). By AM-GM or calculus: maximum at s=1/2s = 1/2s=1/2, giving $\sigma'(0) = 1/4$ (maximum gradient at x=0x = 0x=0).

At large ∣x∣|x|∣x∣: $\sigma(x) \approx 1$ (or ≈0\approx 0≈0), so $\sigma'(x) \approx 0$. The gradient _vanishes_ for large inputs.

**Why this matters for deep networks:** In a deep network with sigmoid activations, the gradient at each layer is multiplied by σ′\sigma'σ′ at that layer (via the chain rule). If many layers have large ∣x∣|x|∣x∣ inputs (which happens during training), the product of many near-zero σ′\sigma'σ′ values is astronomically small. Gradients in the early layers become tiny — the network can't learn. This is the **vanishing gradient problem** , and it's why sigmoid was replaced by ReLU (max⁡($\max(0, x)max($) in modern deep learning. ReLU's gradient is 1 for x>0x > 0x>0 and 0 for x<0x < 0x<0 — no saturation for positive inputs. We'll return to this in Chapters 15 and 16.

#### Think Deeper

**Q1.** Consider the loss landscape of a function with a very long, narrow valley (think of a ravine running northeast-southwest, with steep walls and a gentle slope along the bottom). Gradient descent with a fixed learning rate will "zigzag" across the ravine rather than traveling along the bottom. Why does this happen geometrically? What strategies could help?

**Discussion:** In the narrow valley, the gradient mostly points _across_ the valley (toward the steep walls) rather than _along_ it (toward the minimum). A step in the gradient direction takes you from one side of the ravine to the other — but you also make a small amount of progress toward the minimum. With a small learning rate, convergence is extremely slow (many tiny steps along the ravine). With a large learning rate, you overshoot the ravine walls entirely.

This is fundamentally a problem of **different curvatures in different directions** : the ravine has high curvature across its width and low curvature along its length. The optimal step size is large in the shallow direction and small in the steep direction — but a single learning rate can't be both simultaneously.

Strategies that help:

  1. **Momentum:** Add a "velocity" term that accumulates gradient history. The velocity builds up along the consistent ravine direction while canceling out across it.
  2. **Adaptive learning rates (Adam, RMSProp):** Give each parameter its own learning rate, scaled by the history of its gradient magnitude. Parameters in steep directions get smaller learning rates; parameters in shallow directions get larger ones.
  3. **Preconditioning (second-order methods):** Use the Hessian or an approximation to rescale the gradient. Newton's method does this exactly but is too expensive for large models.
  4. **Feature normalization:** The ravine geometry often arises from features on very different scales (e.g., "income" in hundreds of thousands vs. "age" in tens). Normalizing features to similar scales prevents this pathological geometry.

These ideas directly motivate the advanced optimizers in Chapter 18.

**Q2.** Saddle points — where the gradient is zero but the point is neither a local minimum nor a local maximum — are far more common than local minima in high-dimensional non-convex loss landscapes. Yet gradient descent in practice rarely gets stuck at them. Why? And what role does the noise in stochastic gradient descent (SGD) play in escaping saddle points?

**Discussion:** A saddle point is a critical point θ∗\boldsymbol{\theta}^*θ∗ where $\nabla L = \mathbf{0}$, but the Hessian $H$ has both positive and negative eigenvalues. In one direction the point is a local minimum; in the orthogonal direction it is a local maximum. A classic example is the origin of f(x,y)=x2−y2f(x, y) = x^2 - y^2f(x,y)=x2−y2.

**Why saddle points are ubiquitous in deep networks.** In a loss landscape with $d$ parameters, a random critical point has each principal curvature independently positive or negative. The probability that all $d$ curvatures are positive (a true local minimum) decreases exponentially with $d$. For a neural network with millions of parameters, almost all critical points are saddle points. True local minima are rare — and near the global minimum, the loss is nearly flat in many directions.

**Why gradient descent escapes them anyway.** Consider exact gradient descent initialized slightly off a saddle point. If the initialization has any component in the direction of negative curvature (the "downhill" direction away from the saddle), the gradient will pull the iterate away exponentially fast in that direction. Saddle points are unstable equilibria: they are only fixed points if you start exactly on them, which happens with probability zero from a random initialization. In this sense, gradient descent is _not_ attracted to saddle points — it only converges to them from measure-zero initializations.

**The role of SGD noise.** Exact gradient descent still slows near saddle points (the gradient is very small in a neighborhood of θ∗\boldsymbol{\theta}^*θ∗). The noise in stochastic gradient descent — arising from using a mini-batch rather than the full dataset — actively helps escape this neighborhood. Each mini-batch gradient is an unbiased estimate of the true gradient plus noise. Even when the true gradient is near zero (near a saddle), the noisy estimate has a nonzero component in the direction of negative curvature. This perturbation kicks the iterate away from the saddle, after which the true negative curvature takes over and gradient descent accelerates away.

**Practical implications.** This analysis explains several empirical observations: (1) larger batch sizes sometimes converge to "sharper" minima (less noise means less saddle-point escape, which can mean getting trapped in regions with higher curvature); (2) adding explicit noise (e.g., learning rate schedules that initially increase) can help optimization; (3) the common advice to "add a small amount of noise to initialization" has a theoretical basis in saddle-point escape. The formal study of this phenomenon is part of the field of non-convex optimization and has been active since around 2015.

### Chapter Summary

This chapter completed our mathematical toolkit with the calculus and optimization tools that underpin model training.

Key ideas:

* **Derivatives** measure rate of change; the **gradient** generalizes this to multivariate functions and points in the direction of steepest ascent
* **The chain rule** composes derivatives for composite functions — and it's the mathematical foundation of backpropagation in neural networks (Chapter 15)
* **Gradient descent** minimizes a loss function by iteratively stepping in the negative gradient direction; the learning rate controls step size
* **Convex functions** have a single global minimum — gradient descent is guaranteed to find it; non-convex functions (neural networks) require more careful treatment
* The **gradient of MSE** for linear regression points directly toward the normal equations, connecting calculus to the linear algebra result from Chapter 2

We now have all three pillars — linear algebra, probability, and calculus — needed to build and understand ML models. Chapter 5 is where we put it all together: linear regression, derived from three complementary perspectives (geometric, statistical, and optimization), applied to our first running dataset (Thread A: house price prediction).

---

## Chapter 5: Linear Regression — Learning a Line Through Data

Suppose you're a real estate agent and you need to estimate the price of a house before it goes on the market. You have data on 500 recent house sales: each sale recorded the house's square footage, number of bedrooms, age, and the actual sale price. How would you use this data to price a new house?

One approach: find the simplest relationship between the features and the price that fits the data well. Draw a line (or a plane in higher dimensions) through the data. That, in essence, is linear regression.

Linear regression is the foundation on which nearly all of machine learning is built. It is simple enough to understand completely — and complex enough that its three derivations (geometric, probabilistic, and optimization-based) each reveal something different about the structure of learning.

### 5.1 The House Price Dataset — Thread A Begins

_[Thread A: House Price Prediction — A1: Introducing the dataset and fitting linear regression]_

Throughout the rest of this book, we will track a running dataset: 500 houses in a mid-sized city, each described by:

Feature | Variable | Units  
---|---|---  
Living area | x1x_1x1 | square feet  
Number of bedrooms | x2x_2x2 | count  
House age | x3x_3x3 | years  
Garage spaces | x4x_4x4 | count  
Sale price (label) | $y$ | USD thousands  
  
A few rows from the dataset:

x1x_1x1 | x2x_2x2 | x3x_3x3 | x4x_4x4 | $y$  
---|---|---|---|---  
1,450 | 3 | 15 | 1 | 245  
2,100 | 4 | 5 | 2 | 389  
980 | 2 | 42 | 0 | 178  
3,200 | 5 | 3 | 2 | 521  
1,650 | 3 | 22 | 1 | 287  
  
Before building any model, observe the data. Larger houses (x1x_1x1) tend to cost more. Newer houses (x3x_3x3 small) tend to cost more. Bedrooms and garage spaces add value but with diminishing returns. These are not rigid laws — there are outliers — but they are patterns that a model should capture.

In this chapter, we'll build a linear model for this data, find the optimal weights, and interpret what the model has learned.

### 5.2 The Linear Model

#### Definition: Linear Regression Model

Given a feature vector $\mathbf{x} = (x_1, x_2, \ldots, x_n)$, a **linear regression model** predicts:

$\hat{y} = w_0 + w_1 x_1 + w_2 x_2 + \cdots + w_n x_n = w_0 + \mathbf{w}_{1:n}^\top \mathbf{x}$

In plain language, the prediction is a weighted sum of the features, plus a constant term w0w_0w0 called the _intercept_ (or _bias_).

The parameters $\mathbf{w} = (w_0, w_1, \ldots, w_n)$ are what we learn from data. For the house dataset:

$\hat{y} = w_0 + w_1 (\text{area}) + w_2 (\text{bedrooms}) + w_3 (\text{age}) + w_4 (\text{garage})$

**Interpretation:** If the learned weight $w_1 = 0.12$ (thousands of dollars per square foot), it means: _holding all other features constant, each additional square foot is associated with a 120 USD increase in price_.

This interpretability is one of linear regression's greatest strengths. The weights are directly meaningful as marginal effects.

**A notational trick for the intercept:** We can absorb the intercept into the weight vector by adding a constant feature $x_0 = 1$ to every data point. Then the model is simply:

$\hat{y} = \mathbf{w}^\top \mathbf{x}, \quad \mathbf{w} = (w_0, w_1, \ldots, w_n), \quad \mathbf{x} = (1, x_1, x_2, \ldots, x_n)$

This notation is cleaner and we'll use it from here on.

### 5.3 What Makes a Good Fit? The Loss Function

Given $m$ training examples (x1,y1),…,(xm,ym)(\mathbf{x}_1, y_$\ldots, (\mathbf{x}_m, y_m)(x1,y$…,(xm,ym), how do we measure how well a particular weight vector $\mathbf{w}$ fits the data?

The most natural measure is: how far off are our predictions from the true labels?

#### Definition: Mean Squared Error (MSE)

L(w)=1m∑i=1m(yi−y^i)2=1m∑i=1m(yi−w⊤xi)$\mathbf{w}) = \frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^m (y_i - \hat{y}_i)^2 = \frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^m (y_i - \mathbf{w}^\top \mathbf{x}_i)^$w)=m1i=1∑m(yi−y^i)2=m1i=1∑m(yi−w⊤xi)2

In plain language, MSE is the average squared prediction error across all training examples.

We square the errors for two reasons: (1) squaring makes all errors positive (so positive and negative errors don't cancel), and (2) squaring penalizes large errors more heavily than small ones.

As we showed in Chapter 3, minimizing MSE is equivalent to maximum likelihood estimation under Gaussian noise. The MSE loss is not an arbitrary choice — it follows from assuming our data-generating process is:

yi=w⊤xi+ϵi,ϵi∼N(0,σ2)y_i = \mathbf{w}^\top \mathbf{x}_i + \epsilon_i, \quad \epsilon_i \sim \mathcal{N}(0, \sigma^2)yi=w⊤xi+ϵi,ϵi∼N(0,σ2)

Also useful: the **Root Mean Squared Error (RMSE)** = MSE\sqrt{\text{MSE}}MSE, which has the same units as $y$.

And the **coefficient of determination** $R^2$:

R2=1−∑i(yi−y^i)2∑i(yi−yˉ)2R^2 = 1 - \frac{\sum_i (y_i - \hat{y}_i)^2}{\sum_i (y_i - \bar{y})^2}R2=1−∑i(yi−yˉ)2∑i(yi−y^i)2

R2=1R^2 = 1R2=1 means perfect fit; R2=0R^2 = 0R2=0 means the model does no better than always predicting the mean; R2<0R^2 < 0R2<0 means the model is worse than the mean predictor.

### 5.4 Solving Linear Regression: Three Perspectives

The task is to find:

w∗=arg⁡min⁡wL(w)=arg⁡min⁡w1m∥Xw−y∥2\mathbf{w}^* = $\mathbf{w} L(\mathbf{w}) =$\mathbf{w} \frac{1}{m}\|X\mathbf{w} - \mathbf{y}\|^2w∗=argwminL(w)=argwminm1∥Xw−y∥2

where X∈Rm×(n+1)X \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times (n+1)}X∈Rm×(n+1) is the data matrix (with a column of 1s for the intercept), and $\mathbf{y} \in \mathbb{R}^m$ is the vector of labels.

We present three perspectives. Each illuminates a different aspect of what regression is doing.

#### Perspective 1: Geometric (Projection)

We want to find a weight vector $\mathbf{w}$ such that XwX\mathbf{w}Xw is as close as possible to $\mathbf{y}$.

The set of all possible predictions {$\\{X\mathbf{w} : \mathbf{w} \in \mathbb{R}^{n+1}\\}{$ is exactly the _column space_ of $X$ — the span of $X$'s columns. We want the point in the column space of $X$ that is closest to $\mathbf{y}$.

From Chapter 2, this is the **orthogonal projection** of $\mathbf{y}$ onto the column space of $X$:

$\hat{\mathbf{y}} = X(X^\top X)^{-1} X^\top \mathbf{y}$

The optimal predictions are the projection, and the optimal weights are:

$\mathbf{w}^* = (X^\top X)^{-1} X^\top \mathbf{y}$

The residuals $\mathbf{e} = \mathbf{y} - X\mathbf{w}^*$ are orthogonal to every column of $X$:

X⊤e=X⊤(y−Xw∗)=X⊤y−X⊤Xw∗=0$\mathbf{e} = X^\top (\mathbf{y} - X\mathbf{w}^*) = X^\top \mathbf{y} -$ X \mathbf{w}^* = \mathbf{0}X⊤e=X⊤(y−Xw∗)=X⊤y−X⊤Xw∗=0

This orthogonality condition is exactly what makes the projection the closest point.

#### Perspective 2: Algebraic (Normal Equations)

Take the gradient of the MSE loss and set it to zero:

∇wL=2mX⊤(Xw−y)=0\nabla_\mathbf{w} L = \frac{2}{m} X^\top (X\mathbf{w} - \mathbf{y}) = \mathbf{0}∇wL=m2X⊤(Xw−y)=0

Multiplying through by m/2m/2m/2:

X⊤Xw=X⊤yX^\top X \mathbf{w} = X^\top \mathbf{y}X⊤Xw=X⊤y

#### Definition: Normal Equations

$\mathbf{w}^* = (X^\top X)^{-1} X^\top \mathbf{y}$

(assuming X⊤XX^\top XX⊤X is invertible)

These are called the _normal equations_ because they encode the condition that the residuals are _normal_ (orthogonal) to the column space — connecting back to the geometric perspective.

The normal equations give the exact solution in closed form. For small datasets, this is efficient. For datasets with millions of examples or thousands of features, inverting X⊤XX^\top XX⊤X (an O(n3)O(n^3)O(n3) operation) becomes impractical, and we use gradient descent instead.

#### Perspective 3: Probabilistic (Maximum Likelihood)

Under the Gaussian noise model yi=w⊤xi+ϵiy_i = \mathbf{w}^\top \mathbf{x}_i + \epsilon_iyi=w⊤xi+ϵi with $\epsilon_i \sim \mathcal{N}(0, \sigma^2)$, we showed in Chapter 3 that the MLE is:

w^MLE=arg⁡min⁡w∑i=1m(yi−w⊤xi)2\hat{\mathbf{w}}_{\text{MLE}} = \arg\min_\mathbf{w} \sum_{i=1}^m (y_i - \mathbf{w}^\top \mathbf{x}_i)^2w^MLE=argwmini=1∑m(yi−w⊤xi)2

which is exactly the same as the MSE minimizer. All three perspectives give the same answer — which is deeply reassuring.

#### Common Misconception

Many students think linear regression can only model linear relationships between features and target. But "linear" refers to linearity in the _parameters_ $\mathbf{w}$, not in the original features. We can add polynomial features: use x1,x12,x13x_1, x_1^2, x_1^3x1,x12,x13 as features. We can add interaction terms: use x1⋅x2x_1 \cdot x_2x1⋅x2. The model is still linear in these extended features, so all the same theory applies — while capturing non-linear patterns in the original input space.

### 5.5 Interpreting the Fitted Model

After fitting the house price model on our 500-house dataset, suppose we get:

$\hat{y} = -28.3 + 0.124 \cdot x_1 + 15.7 \cdot x_2 - 1.9 \cdot x_3 + 22.4 \cdot x_4$

(prices in thousands of dollars, area in square feet)

**Interpretation:**

* **w 0=−28.3w_0 = -28.3w0=−28.3**: The intercept has no direct physical meaning here (a house with zero area, zero bedrooms, etc., is not a real house). It absorbs overall level effects.
* **w 1=0.124w_1 = 0.124w1=0.124**: Each additional square foot adds 124 USD to the predicted price, holding other features constant.
* **w 2=15.7w_2 = 15.7w2=15.7**: Each additional bedroom adds 15,700 USD, holding area constant. (Notice this captures the _marginal_ value of a bedroom in a house of a given size.)
* **w 3=−1.9w_3 = -1.9w3=−1.9**: Each year of age _reduces_ price by 1,900 USD. Older houses are cheaper, holding other features constant.
* **w 4=22.4w_4 = 22.4w4=22.4**: Each garage space adds 22,400 USD. This is a large effect, possibly reflecting that garages are associated with larger lots in this market.

How good is this fit? Suppose R2=0.73R^2 = 0.73R2=0.73. The model explains 73% of the variance in sale prices — reasonable for four simple features, but there's clearly more structure in the data (neighborhood quality, school district, recent renovations) that the model is missing.

In Chapter 9, we'll apply a decision tree to the same dataset and see whether a non-linear model can do better.

### 5.6 Gradient Descent for Linear Regression

For large datasets, the normal equations are too expensive. We use gradient descent instead.

From Chapter 4, the gradient of the MSE loss is:

∇wL=2mX⊤(Xw−y)=2m∑i=1m(yi−w⊤xi)(−xi)\nabla_\mathbf{w} L = \frac{2}{m} X^\top (X\mathbf{w} - \mathbf{y}) = \frac{2}{m} \sum_{i=1}^m (y_i - \mathbf{w}^\top \mathbf{x}_i)(-\mathbf{x}_i)∇wL=m2X⊤(Xw−y)=m2i=1∑m(yi−w⊤xi)(−xi)

The mini-batch update rule (using batch of size $B$):

wt+1=wt+2ηB∑i∈Bt(yi−wt⊤xi)xi\mathbf{w}_{t+1} = \mathbf{w}_t + \frac{2\eta}{B} \sum_{i \in \mathcal{B}_t} (y_i - \mathbf{w}_t^\top \mathbf{x}_i) \mathbf{x}_iwt+1=wt+B2ηi∈Bt∑(yi−wt⊤xi)xi

Notice: when the prediction $\mathbf{w}_t^\top \mathbf{x}_i$ underestimates yiy_iyi, the residual (yi−y^i)(y_i - \hat{y}_i)(yi−y^i) is positive, so the update adds xi\mathbf{x}_ixi to $\mathbf{w}$ — increasing the prediction for similar examples. This is exactly the intuitive "correct toward the true value" update.

**Feature scaling matters for gradient descent.** If x1x_1x1 is in square feet (range 500–5000) and x2x_2x2 is number of bedrooms (range 1–6), the loss function has very different curvature in the w1w_1w1 and w2w_2w2 directions. This creates the "ravine" geometry we discussed in Chapter 4, making gradient descent slow and oscillatory.

Solution: **standardize features** before training. For each feature $j$, compute the training mean μj\mu_jμj and standard deviation σj\sigma_jσj, and replace xijx_{ij}xij with (xij−μj)/σj(x_{ij} - \mu_j) / \sigma_j(xij−μj)/σj. After standardization, all features have mean 0 and standard deviation 1, making the loss landscape more spherical and gradient descent much more efficient.

#### Common Misconception

The normal equations and gradient descent both find the _same_ w∗\mathbf{w}^*w∗ (when gradient descent converges, and when X⊤XX^\top XX⊤X is invertible). They are not different models — they are different _computational methods_ to find the same parameters. Gradient descent is preferred when $m$ or $n$ is large; normal equations when $m$ and $n$ are small and exact solutions are needed.

#### Concept Check

Write out the data matrix $X$ (with the bias column), the label vector $\mathbf{y}$, and compute X⊤XX^\top XX⊤X and X⊤yX^\top \mathbf{y}X⊤y.

X=(111213),y=(235)X = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 \\\ 1 & 2 \\\ 1 & 3 \end{pmatrix}, \quad \mathbf{y} = \begin{pmatrix} 2 \\\ 3 \\\ 5 \end{pmatrix}X=111123,y=235 X⊤X=(111123)(111213)=(36614)X^\top X = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 & 1 \\\ 1 & 2 & 3 \end{pmatrix} \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 \\\ 1 & 2 \\\ 1 & 3 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 3 & 6 \\\ 6 & 14 \end{pmatrix}X⊤X=(111213)111123=(36614) X⊤y=(111123)(235)=(1023)X^\top \mathbf{y} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 & 1 \\\ 1 & 2 & 3 \end{pmatrix} \begin{pmatrix} 2 \\\ 3 \\\ 5 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 10 \\\ 23 \end{pmatrix}X⊤y=(111213)235=(1023)

For a 2×22 \times 22×2 matrix (abcd)\begin{pmatrix} a & b \\\ c & d \end{pmatrix}(acbd), the inverse is 1ad−bc(d−b−ca)\frac{1}{ad-bc}\begin{pmatrix} d & -b \\\ -c & a \end{pmatrix}ad−bc1(d−c−ba).

X⊤X=(36614),det⁡=3⋅14−6⋅6=42−36=6X^\top X = \begin{pmatrix} 3 & 6 \\\ 6 & 14 \end{pmatrix}, \quad \det = 3 \cdot 14 - 6 \cdot 6 = 42 - 36 = 6X⊤X=(36614),det=3⋅14−6⋅6=42−36=6 (X⊤X)−1=16(14−6−63)(X^\top X)^{-1} = \frac{1}{6}\begin{pmatrix} 14 & -6 \\\ -6 & 3 \end{pmatrix}(X⊤X)−1=61(14−6−63) w∗=16(14−6−63)(1023)=16(140−138−60+69)=16(29)=(1/33/2)\mathbf{w}^* = $\begin{pmatrix} 14 & -6 \\\ -6 & 3 \end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix} 10 \\\ 23 \end{pmatrix} = \frac{1}{6}\begin{pmatrix} 140 - 138 \\\ -60 + 69 \end{pmatrix} =$\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\\ 9 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 1/3 \\\ 3/2 \end{pmatrix}w∗=61(14−6−63)(1023)=61(140−138−60+69)=61(29)=(1/33/2)

So $\hat{y} \approx 0.333 + 1.5 x$.

Verify: predictions are 1/3+1.5=1.8331/3 + 1.5 = 1.8331/3+1.5=1.833 (actual 2), 1/3+3=3.3331/3 + 3 = 3.3331/3+3=3.333 (actual 3), 1/3+4.5=4.8331/3 + 4.5 = 4.8331/3+4.5=4.833 (actual 5). The residuals are [0.167,−0.333,0.167][0.167, -0.333, 0.167][0.167,−0.333,0.167], which sum to zero (as expected for OLS with an intercept). MSE =(0.1672+0.3332+0.1672)/3≈0.056= (0.167^2 + 0.333^2 + 0.167^$\approx 0.056=(0.1672+0.3332+0.167$≈0.056.

**Q3.** What is $R^2$ for the fit in Q2?

**Answer:** Mean of $y$: $\bar{y} = (2+3+5)/3 = 10/3 \approx 3.333$.

Total sum of squares: (2−3.333)2+(3−3.333)2+(5−3.333)2=1.778+0.111+2.778=4.667(2-3.333)^2 + (3-3.333)^2 + (5-3.333)^2 = 1.778 + 0.111 + 2.778 = 4.667(2−3.333)2+(3−3.333)2+(5−3.333)2=1.778+0.111+2.778=4.667

Residual sum of squares: (0.167)2+(−0.333)2+(0.167)2=0.028+0.111+0.028=0.167(0.167)^2 + (-0.333)^2 + (0.167)^2 = 0.028 + 0.111 + 0.028 = 0.167(0.167)2+(−0.333)2+(0.167)2=0.028+0.111+0.028=0.167

R2=1−0.167/4.667=1−0.036=0.964R^2 = 1 - 0.167/4.667 = 1 - 0.036 = 0.964R2=1−0.167/4.667=1−0.036=0.964

The model explains 96.4% of the variance in this tiny dataset — a very good fit for three data points and one feature.

**Q4.** Why can linear regression fail if X⊤XX^\top XX⊤X is singular? What does singularity mean about the features in the dataset?

**Answer:** If X⊤XX^\top XX⊤X is singular (non-invertible), the normal equations have infinitely many solutions — there is no unique w∗\mathbf{w}^*w∗. Gradient descent may also fail to converge (the loss landscape has a flat direction with no minimum).

Singularity of X⊤XX^\top XX⊤X occurs when the columns of $X$ are linearly dependent — when one feature is a linear combination of others. In the house price context, this could happen if: (1) you include both "total rooms" and "bedrooms + bathrooms" (the first is a sum of the second two), (2) you include "price in dollars" and "price in cents" (one is 100× the other), or (3) you have more features than examples (n>mn > mn>m).

This is called _perfect multicollinearity_. The practical solution is to detect and remove redundant features, or to use _regularization_ (Chapter 6), which adds a term to X⊤XX^\top XX⊤X that makes it invertible.

#### Application Problems

**Q1.** You're fitting a linear regression model to predict exam scores from study hours. After fitting, you compute the residuals and notice they increase as the predicted score increases (larger residuals for students with higher predicted scores). What problem does this suggest, and how might you address it?

Hint

This pattern — residuals that grow with the predicted value — violates a key assumption of ordinary least squares. Think about what the Gaussian noise model assumes.

**Answer:** This pattern is called **heteroskedasticity** — the variance of the residuals is not constant (as the Gaussian noise model assumes), but grows with the predicted value. Intuitively: students predicted to score near 100 have "ceiling effects" — a score of 100 is the maximum, so high-performing students' scores are "compressed" even if their true ability varies widely. Linear OLS is still unbiased under heteroskedasticity, but is no longer the minimum-variance estimator, and confidence intervals are incorrect.

Solutions include: (1) a log transformation of the target $y$ (predict log⁡(score)\log(\text{score})log(score) instead of score), which often stabilizes variance; (2) _weighted least squares_ , which gives less weight to observations with higher expected variance; (3) using a generalized linear model with a more appropriate error distribution; (4) robust standard errors. The key insight is that the choice of loss function should match the assumed data-generating process.

**Q2.** A colleague claims that adding more features to a linear regression model always improves (or at least doesn't hurt) performance, because the model can always set a feature's weight to zero if it's unhelpful. Is this true for training error? For test error? Explain.

Hint

Think about the bias-variance tradeoff from Chapter 1. What happens to variance as you add more parameters?

**Answer:** For **training error** : yes, adding features can never increase training error. The optimal solution in the higher-dimensional space always achieves at least as low a training loss as in the lower-dimensional space (it can set the extra weight to zero to match the original performance, or use the extra feature to reduce error).

For **test error** : no. Adding features increases model complexity and variance. Even if a feature has no true relationship to the label, the model will fit its random correlation with the label in the training set — introducing variance without reducing bias. This is called _variance inflation from noise features_.

In practice, with $m$ examples and $n$ features, a rule of thumb is to need m≫nm \gg nm≫n for OLS to generalize well. When $n$ is comparable to or larger than $m$, unregularized regression overfits severely. This motivates regularization (Chapter 6): penalizing large weights to discourage the model from fitting noise.

**Q3.** The house price model we described uses raw square footage x1x_1x1. A colleague suggests using log⁡($\log(x_1)log($ instead. Under what empirical circumstances would the log transformation improve the model, and what would it imply about how price scales with house size?

**Answer:** Using log⁡($\log(x_1)log($ implies a _logarithmic_ relationship between area and price:

y^=w0+w1log⁡(x1)+other features\hat{y} = w_0 + w_1 \log(x_1) + \text{other features}y^=w0+w1log(x1)+other features

This means each doubling of area adds a constant w1log⁡(2)w_1 \log(2)w1log(2) to the predicted price — diminishing absolute returns to scale. A house twice as large doesn't necessarily cost twice as much; the first 500 extra square feet (going from 1,000 to 1,500) may add more value than the next 500 (going from 3,000 to 3,500).

The log transformation is appropriate when:

  1. The distribution of x1x_1x1 is right-skewed (a few very large houses) — the log compresses the range
  2. The effect of x1x_1x1 on $y$ appears sublinear in a scatter plot
  3. The residuals from the raw model show increasing variance with x1x_1x1 (heteroskedasticity)

In the context of house prices, this is often realistic: price scales roughly with the log of area rather than area itself, because larger homes have increasing marginal construction costs but also decreasing marginal utility for buyers.

#### Think Deeper

**Q1.** Linear regression minimizes SSE (sum of squared errors), which is equivalent to MLE under Gaussian noise. But what if the noise is not Gaussian — for instance, what if we expect occasional catastrophic measurement errors? Propose an alternative loss function, explain its derivation from a probabilistic model, and discuss the practical trade-offs.

**Discussion:** If we expect heavy-tailed noise — occasional large errors from measurement mistakes, data entry errors, or extreme events — the Gaussian assumption is inappropriate. Two alternatives:

**ℓ 1\ell_1ℓ1 loss (MAE):** Corresponding to Laplace noise (as derived in Chapter 3), this minimizes $\sum_i |y_i - \hat{y}_i|$. The MAE loss penalizes large errors _linearly_ rather than quadratically, so outliers have less influence. The downside: MAE is not differentiable at zero, which complicates gradient computation (though the _subgradient_ can be used). The solution is the sample median rather than the mean — robust but less efficient when the Gaussian assumption actually holds.

**Huber loss:** A hybrid that behaves like MSE for small errors (smooth, efficient) and like MAE for large errors (robust to outliers):

Lδ(r)={12r2if ∣r∣≤δδ(∣r∣−δ2)otherwiseL_\delta(r) = \begin{cases} \frac{1}{2}r^2 & \text{if } |r| \leq \delta \\\ \delta(|r| - \frac{\delta}{2}) & \text{otherwise} \end{cases}Lδ(r)={21r2δ(∣r∣−2δ)if ∣r∣≤δotherwise

The threshold δ\deltaδ is a hyperparameter: errors smaller than δ\deltaδ are treated as Gaussian, larger ones as Laplace. This is differentiable everywhere (the derivative is continuous at r=±δr = \pm\deltar=±δ) and is widely used in robust regression and reinforcement learning (the Huber loss appears in deep Q-networks).

Trade-off: MSE is optimal when the Gaussian assumption holds (efficient, easy to compute). When outliers are present, MAE or Huber loss are more robust. Always ask: does the problem naturally produce symmetric Gaussian noise, or are there systematic reasons for heavy-tailed errors?

### Chapter Summary

Linear regression is the simplest supervised learning model, and the one from which all others are understood by comparison. We've seen it from three angles:

* **Geometrically** : fitting a line (or hyperplane) is finding the _orthogonal projection_ of the label vector onto the column space of the data matrix
* **Algebraically** : the optimal weights satisfy the _normal equations_ $\mathbf{w}^* = (X^\top X)^{-1} X^\top \mathbf{y}$
* **Probabilistically** : minimizing MSE is equivalent to _maximum likelihood estimation_ under Gaussian noise

**Thread A checkpoint (A1):** We've introduced the house price dataset with four features (area, bedrooms, age, garage). The linear regression model gives us interpretable coefficients — 124 USD per square foot, -1,900 USD per year of age — and explains 73% of price variance. This is our baseline. Every future method will be benchmarked against it.

In Chapter 6, we'll address the key weakness of unregularized regression: overfitting when features are many or correlated. In Chapter 8, we'll establish the proper methodology for measuring model performance. And in Chapter 9, we'll return to the house dataset with a fundamentally different model — the decision tree — to see what the linear model was missing.

---

## Chapter 6: Regularization — Controlling Complexity

In Chapter 5, we fit a linear regression model to predict house prices and got R2=0.73R^2 = 0.73R2=0.73 on the training data. But there's something we haven't checked: how well does the model do on _new_ houses it hasn't seen during training?

Imagine adding more features to the model: school rating, proximity to park, number of windows, wall color, last renovation year, tax history... With enough features, a linear model can fit the 500 training houses perfectly (R2=1.0R^2 = 1.0R2=1.0 on training data). But such a model will perform terribly on new houses — it has memorized the training data rather than learned the underlying patterns.

This is overfitting. And regularization is the set of techniques for preventing it.

### 6.1 The Overfitting Problem

Let's make overfitting concrete. Suppose we have 500 training houses but add 600 features — more features than examples. The data matrix X∈R500×600X \in \mathbb{R}^{500 \times 600}X∈R500×600 has more columns than rows, so the system Xw=yX\mathbf{w} = \mathbf{y}Xw=y is underdetermined: there are infinitely many exact solutions. The least-squares solution will have zero training error — but it will wildly overfit, essentially memorizing which house maps to which price rather than learning anything generalizable.

Even with fewer features than examples, if the features are too numerous or noisy, the model starts fitting random patterns. The model's weights grow large in magnitude, "compensating" for each other. For example, a weight of +50 on one feature and -49 on a highly correlated feature achieves a small _net_ effect but introduces enormous variance.

The solution is **regularization** : add a penalty on the size of the weights to the loss function. Large weights cost extra. This forces the model to only use features that are genuinely predictive, with a penalty for complexity.

### 6.2 Ridge Regression (L2 Regularization)

#### Definition: Ridge Regression

**Ridge regression** adds an ℓ2\ell_2ℓ2 penalty on the weights to the MSE loss:

Lridge(w)=1m∥Xw−y∥2+λ∥w∥2=1m∑i=1m(yi−w⊤xi)2+λ∑j=1nwj2L_{\text{ridge}}(\mathbf{w}) = $\|X\mathbf{w} - \mathbf{y}\|^2 + \lambda \|\mathbf{w}\|^2 =$\sum_{i=1}^m (y_i - \mathbf{w}^\top \mathbf{x}_i)^2 + \lambda \sum_{j=1}^n w_j^2Lridge(w)=m1∥Xw−y∥2+λ∥w∥2=m1i=1∑m(yi−w⊤xi)2+λj=1∑nwj2

where $\lambda \geq 0$ is the **regularization strength** (also called the penalty parameter).

In plain language, we're minimizing prediction error _plus_ the sum of squared weights. Large weights are penalized — the model must "justify" a large weight with a proportionally large reduction in prediction error.

The intercept w0w_0w0 is typically _not_ penalized (since it affects the overall scale of predictions, not complexity).

Setting $\nabla_\mathbf{w} L_{\text{ridge}} = 0$ gives the closed-form solution:

$\mathbf{w}^*_{\text{ridge}} = (X^\top X + \lambda m I)^{-1} X^\top \mathbf{y}$

Notice the key improvement: even when X⊤XX^\top XX⊤X is singular (Chapter 5 — this happens when features are linearly dependent), adding $\lambda m I$ to the diagonal ensures the matrix is invertible. Ridge regression regularizes _and_ stabilizes the solution.

#### The Effect of λ\lambdaλ

* **λ =0\lambda = 0λ=0**: Recovers ordinary least squares (OLS). No regularization.
* **λ →∞\lambda \to \inftyλ→∞**: The penalty dominates, forcing all weights toward zero. The model reduces to always predicting the mean.
* **λ \lambdaλ intermediate**: Weights are "shrunk" toward zero but not set to zero. The degree of shrinkage increases with λ\lambdaλ.

The path of solutions {$\\{\mathbf{w}^*_{\text{ridge}}(\lambda) : \lambda \geq 0\\}{$ is called the **regularization path**. Plotting how each weight changes as λ\lambdaλ varies shows which features are "used" at different complexity levels.

#### Bayesian Interpretation of Ridge

There's an elegant probabilistic explanation for Ridge regression. Suppose we place a Gaussian prior on the weights:

P(w)∝exp⁡ ⁣(−λ2σ2∥w∥2)=N(0,σ2λI)P(\mathbf{w}) \propto \exp\\!\left(-\frac{\lambda}{2$\|\mathbf{w}\|^2\right) = \mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0}, \frac{${\lambda} I)P(w)∝exp(−2σ2λ∥w∥2)=N(0,λσ2I)

This prior says: we believe weights are likely close to zero before seeing any data. Combining this prior with the Gaussian likelihood from Chapter 5 via Bayes' theorem, the maximum a posteriori (MAP) estimate is:

wMAP∗=arg⁡max⁡wP(w∣data)=arg⁡min⁡w[1σ2∥Xw−y∥2+λσ2∥w∥2]\mathbf{w}^*_{\text{MAP}} = \arg\max_\mathbf{w} P(\mathbf{w}|\text{data}) = \arg\min_\mathbf{w} \left[ \frac{1$\|X\mathbf{w} - \mathbf{y}\|^2 + \frac{\lambda$\|\mathbf{w}\|^2 \right]wMAP∗=argwmaxP(w∣data)=argwmin[σ21∥Xw−y∥2+σ2λ∥w∥2]

which is exactly Ridge regression. The regularization strength λ\lambdaλ encodes our prior belief about how large weights should be. A large λ\lambdaλ means a strong prior belief that weights are near zero.

**This connection runs throughout ML:** nearly every regularizer has a Bayesian interpretation as a prior on the parameters. Understanding this makes regularization feel principled, not ad hoc.

#### Common Misconception

Ridge regression shrinks all weights toward zero but _never sets any weight exactly to zero_. This is the key difference from Lasso (next section). Ridge keeps all features, but reduces the influence of less important ones. If you need a model that uses only a subset of features, Ridge is not the right tool.

### 6.3 Lasso Regression (L1 Regularization)

#### Definition: Lasso Regression

**Lasso (Least Absolute Shrinkage and Selection Operator)** uses an ℓ1\ell_1ℓ1 penalty:

Llasso(w)=1m∥Xw−y∥2+λ∥w∥1=1m∑i=1m(yi−w⊤xi)2+λ∑j=1n∣wj∣L_{\text{lasso}}(\mathbf{w}) = $\|X\mathbf{w} - \mathbf{y}\|^2 + \lambda \|\mathbf{w}\|_1 =$\sum_{i=1}^m (y_i - \mathbf{w}^\top \mathbf{x}_i)^2 + \lambda \sum_{j=1}^n |w_j|Llasso(w)=m1∥Xw−y∥2+λ∥w∥1=m1i=1∑m(yi−w⊤xi)2+λj=1∑n∣wj∣

In plain language, instead of penalizing the sum of _squared_ weights, we penalize the sum of their absolute values.

This seemingly small change has a dramatic consequence: **Lasso drives many weights exactly to zero** , producing sparse solutions.

#### Why Lasso Produces Sparsity: Geometric Intuition

To see why, consider the constrained form of each problem:

**Ridge:** Minimize MSE subject to $\|\mathbf{w}\|^2 \leq t$ (stay within an ℓ2\ell_2ℓ2 ball)

**Lasso:** Minimize MSE subject to $\|\mathbf{w}\|_1 \leq t$ (stay within an ℓ1\ell_1ℓ1 ball, a "diamond" shape)

The ℓ2\ell_2ℓ2 ball is a smooth sphere. The ℓ1\ell_1ℓ1 ball is a diamond with _corners_ located at the coordinate axes (points like (t,0,0,…)(t, 0, 0, \ldots)(t,0,0,…), (0,t,0,…)(0, t, 0, \ldots)(0,t,0,…), etc.). When the elliptical contours of the MSE function (centered at the unregularized OLS solution) "collide" with the constraint region, they are much more likely to first touch the diamond at a corner than at an arbitrary point on its surface. Corners correspond to sparse solutions (most weights are zero).

In contrast, the ℓ2\ell_2ℓ2 ball has no corners — so the collision can happen anywhere on the sphere, and exact zero weights are not preferred.

**Practical implication:** Lasso performs automatic **feature selection** — it identifies which features are truly predictive and sets the rest to zero. This is invaluable when you have hundreds or thousands of features and want an interpretable model.

#### Thread A — Regularization in Action (A2)

_[Thread A: A2 — Adding regularization to the house price model; Ridge and Lasso select features automatically]_

Suppose we've expanded the house price model to include 25 features: the original 4 plus additional neighborhood statistics, nearby amenity distances, and historical tax records. We fit Ridge and Lasso with cross-validated λ\lambdaλ.

**Ridge result:** All 25 features remain, but weights for the weaker features (e.g., distance to nearest restaurant: w=0.003w = 0.003w=0.003) are shrunk nearly to zero. The model is still "using" all features, just very lightly for the weak ones.

**Lasso result:** 17 of 25 features are set exactly to zero. The 8 surviving features include: living area, house age, garage count, school district rating, distance to downtown, and three neighborhood statistics. RMSE on validation data is slightly better than Ridge because the model is focusing on genuinely predictive features.

This is the "automatic feature selection" property of Lasso at work. We didn't have to manually decide which features to include — the Lasso figured it out.

### 6.4 Elastic Net: The Best of Both

#### Definition: Elastic Net

**Elastic Net** combines both penalties:

Lelastic(w)=1m∥Xw−y∥2+λ1∥w∥1+λ2∥w∥2L_{\text{elastic}}(\mathbf{w}) = \frac{1}{m}\|X\mathbf{w} - \mathbf{y}\|^2 + \lambda_1 \|\mathbf{w}\|_1 + \lambda_2 \|\mathbf{w}\|^2Lelastic(w)=m1∥Xw−y∥2+λ1∥w∥1+λ2∥w∥2

In plain language: both shrink weights toward zero (Ridge property) and encourage sparsity (Lasso property). When features are correlated, Elastic Net handles them better than pure Lasso (which may arbitrarily pick one of a group of correlated features).

The ratio $\alpha = \lambda_1 / (\lambda_1 + \lambda_2) \in [0, 1]$ interpolates between pure Ridge ($\alpha = 0$) and pure Lasso ($\alpha = 1$).

### 6.5 Choosing λ\lambdaλ: Cross-Validation

The regularization strength λ\lambdaλ is a _hyperparameter_ — a parameter we must set before training, not one learned from data. Choosing λ\lambdaλ too small gives too little regularization (overfitting); too large gives too much regularization (underfitting).

The standard approach is **k kk-fold cross-validation**:

  1. Split the training data into $k$ equal folds (typically k=5k = 5k=5 or k=10k = 10k=10)
  2. For each candidate value of λ\lambdaλ: 
     * For i=1,…,ki = 1, \ldots, ki=1,…,k: train on folds $1, \ldots, i-1, i+1, \ldots, k$; evaluate on fold $i$
     * Average the $k$ validation errors
  3. Choose the λ\lambdaλ with the lowest average validation error
  4. Retrain on _all_ training data with the chosen λ\lambdaλ

This gives an unbiased estimate of how each λ\lambdaλ value will perform on unseen data, without "touching" the test set.

A practical shortcut: fit the regularization path (all values of λ\lambdaλ simultaneously) using the **LARS algorithm** for Lasso or simple matrix operations for Ridge. This is more efficient than fitting separately for each λ\lambdaλ.

#### Common Misconception

The validation error used to select λ\lambdaλ cannot be used as an estimate of _final_ generalization error. Once you've chosen λ\lambdaλ based on validation performance, you've "used up" the validation set for model selection. Final performance should be reported on a _test set_ that was set aside before any modeling decisions were made. We cover this properly in Chapter 8.

### 6.6 Regularization Beyond Linear Models

The ideas of L1 and L2 regularization are not specific to linear regression — they apply to any ML model:

* **Logistic regression** (Chapter 7): Ridge/Lasso regularization on the classification weights
* **Neural networks** (Chapter 15): **Weight decay** adds $\lambda \|\mathbf{w}\|^2$ to the loss — equivalent to L2 regularization
* **Support vector machines** (Chapter 11): The parameter C=1/λC = 1/\lambdaC=1/λ controls the margin vs. misclassification tradeoff — Ridge-like
* **Decision trees** (Chapter 9): Regularized via depth limits and minimum leaf size rather than weight penalties

The same intuition applies everywhere: regularization restricts model complexity to prevent overfitting, and the regularization strength must be tuned to balance bias and variance.

### 6.7 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** Write down the Ridge regression objective for a model with weights $\mathbf{w} = (w_1, w_2)$ and $\lambda = 0.5$. If the OLS solution is $\mathbf{w}_{\text{OLS}} = (3, 4)$, explain qualitatively whether the Ridge solution will have larger or smaller weights.

**Answer:** The Ridge objective is:

Lridge(w1,w2)=1m∥Xw−y∥2+0.5(w12+w22)L_{\text{ridge}}(w_1, w_2) = \frac{1}{m}\|X\mathbf{w} - \mathbf{y}\|^2 + 0.5(w_1^2 + w_2^2)Lridge(w1,w2)=m1∥Xw−y∥2+0.5(w12+w22)

The Ridge solution will have **smaller** (in absolute value) weights than the OLS solution. The ℓ2\ell_2ℓ2 penalty adds a cost for large weights, so the optimizer trades off a little extra MSE to reduce w12+w22w_1^2 + w_2^2w12+w22. The Ridge solution shrinks $\mathbf{w}_{\text{OLS}} = (3, 4)$ toward (0,0)(0, 0)(0,0), reaching some intermediate point like (2.1,2.8)(2.1, 2.8)(2.1,2.8) depending on λ\lambdaλ and the data.

**Q2.** For Lasso with λ\lambdaλ very large, what happens to the predictions? What does this model predict for every input?

**Answer:** As $\lambda \to \infty$, the ℓ1\ell_1ℓ1 penalty dominates the objective, forcing all weights wj→0w_j \to 0wj→0 (for j≥1j \geq 1j≥1). The model reduces to $\hat{y} = w_0$ (just the intercept), predicting a constant value for every input. Typically, the optimal intercept under MSE loss with all other weights zero is the training mean yˉ\bar{y}yˉ — the model predicts the same value for everyone.

This is the _maximum bias_ end of the bias-variance tradeoff: the model underfits completely. Its training error equals the variance of $y$ around the mean, and its test error is similarly poor.

**Q3.** If you have two features that are nearly perfectly correlated ($\rho \approx 0.99$), which would perform better: Ridge or Lasso? Why?

**Answer:** **Ridge** performs better for nearly perfectly correlated features.

When two features are highly correlated, the Lasso tends to arbitrarily pick one and set the other to zero (which one depends sensitively on the data and random initialization). This is unstable and can produce high variance across different training sets. Ridge, on the other hand, shrinks both correlated features equally — it spreads the predictive credit across all correlated features proportionally. The Ridge solution is more stable and typically has lower variance when features are correlated.

This is the motivation for Elastic Net: use the Lasso's sparsity-inducing property while Ridge's stability for correlated features.

**Q4.** The Bayesian interpretation of Ridge uses a Gaussian prior. What prior distribution on weights corresponds to Lasso?

**Answer:** The Lasso corresponds to a **Laplace prior** (also called double-exponential):

P(wj)∝exp⁡(−λ∣wj∣)P(w_j) \propto \exp(-\lambda|w_j|)P(wj)∝exp(−λ∣wj∣)

The Laplace distribution has a sharp peak at zero and heavier tails than the Gaussian. The sharp peak at zero is what induces sparsity: the prior assigns very high probability to weights near zero, and the MAP estimate is often pushed _exactly_ to zero (especially when the data provides little evidence for a non-zero weight).

This is another example of the principle from Chapter 3: loss functions and regularizers have probabilistic interpretations. The Gaussian prior → L2 penalty, the Laplace prior → L1 penalty.

#### Application Problems

**Q1.** You're building a spam classifier with 10,000 word features. You expect only about 50–100 words are actually useful for distinguishing spam from non-spam. Which regularizer should you use and why? What would you expect the regularization path to look like as λ\lambdaλ increases from 0?

Hint

Think about the feature selection property of Lasso vs. Ridge. How many non-zero weights do you expect in the final model?

**Answer:** Use **Lasso**. With 10,000 features but only ~50–100 genuinely predictive, you need a model that explicitly sets the 9,900 non-predictive weights to zero. Ridge would keep all 10,000 features with small but non-zero weights, making the model uninterpretable and potentially overfitting. Lasso's sparsity property makes it natural for this kind of "needle in a haystack" feature selection problem.

Regularization path: at $\lambda = 0$ (OLS), all 10,000 features have non-zero weights. As λ\lambdaλ increases from 0, the least predictive features are first to be set to zero. As λ\lambdaλ continues to increase, progressively more features are zeroed out. At the end, only the ~50–100 genuinely predictive spam-indicating words (e.g., "WINNER", "FREE", "click here", "unsubscribe") remain. At very large λ\lambdaλ, even these are pushed to zero and the model always predicts "not spam."

A practical note: this is exactly how Naive Bayes and Lasso logistic regression are both used for text classification. Thread B (Chapter 7) will apply logistic regression to sentiment analysis, and regularization will be essential for the high-dimensional bag-of-words features.

**Q2.** A linear regression model with n=5n = 5n=5 features and m=1000m = 1000m=1000 examples is fit with OLS. Then the same model is fit with Ridge ($\lambda = 0.01$) and Lasso ($\lambda = 0.01$). Rank the three models by expected training error and explain. Then rank by expected test error, assuming the true model only uses 2 of the 5 features.

**Answer:**

**Training error ranking (lowest to highest):** OLS < Lasso ≈ Ridge

OLS has the lowest training error because it minimizes training MSE with no other constraints. Ridge and Lasso have higher training error because they're also minimizing the regularization term, trading off some training fit for regularization.

**Test error ranking (lowest to highest):** Lasso < Ridge < OLS

Since the true model uses only 2 features, the other 3 are noise. OLS fits these noise features, increasing variance. Ridge shrinks but doesn't eliminate them. Lasso sets them to zero (given appropriate λ\lambdaλ), recovering something close to the true 2-feature model — lowest bias and no wasted variance on irrelevant features.

This example perfectly illustrates the bias-variance tradeoff: OLS has the lowest bias on training data but highest variance; Lasso has somewhat higher bias but much lower variance when the true model is sparse.

#### Think Deeper

**Q1.** Consider a setting where n=2000n = 2000n=2000 features (gene expression measurements) and m=100m = 100m=100 patients, and you want to predict disease outcome. In this "small $n$, large $p$" setting (statistics calls features "predictors" or "p"), ordinary least squares doesn't even have a unique solution. Describe a complete analysis strategy and justify each choice.

**Discussion:** This is a classic genomics problem. A complete strategy:

**Step 1 — Preprocessing:** Standardize all 2,000 features. In genomics, different genes have wildly different expression ranges; standardization prevents high-variance genes from dominating regardless of their predictive power.

**Step 2 — Model selection:** Use Lasso (or Elastic Net). With m=100m = 100m=100 and n=2,000n = 2,000n=2,000, we expect only a handful of genes (perhaps 5–50) to be truly associated with disease outcome. Lasso will automatically select these and set the rest to zero. OLS fails entirely (non-unique solution). Ridge would keep all 2,000 genes, which is biologically implausible and statistically unstable.

**Step 3 — λ\lambdaλ tuning:** Use leave-one-out cross-validation (LOO-CV) rather than 5-fold CV. With only m=100m = 100m=100 patients, 5-fold CV leaves only 80 patients for training — risky with 2,000 features. LOO-CV trains on 99 patients, giving the most data to each training fold.

**Step 4 — Stability analysis:** With small $m$, the specific features Lasso selects will vary somewhat across different training subsets. Run Lasso on many bootstrap samples and report the _selection frequency_ of each gene. Features selected in >80% of bootstrap runs are more robustly associated with the outcome.

**Step 5 — Validation:** Reserve 20 patients completely out of all analysis for final evaluation. Report performance only on these. With m=100m = 100m=100, this leaves m=80m = 80m=80 for training/cross-validation — small, but better than reporting training performance.

This strategy respects the fundamental challenge of high-dimensional, small-sample problems: statistical inference is fragile when n≪mn \ll mn≪m (using statistics notation here where $n$ = patients and $p$ = features), and the risk of false discovery is high.

**Q2.** Show that Ridge regression corresponds to placing a Gaussian prior on the weights, and Lasso corresponds to a Laplace prior. From this Bayesian perspective, what does "choosing the regularization parameter λ\lambdaλ" actually mean?

**Discussion:** The connection between regularization and Bayesian priors comes from MAP (Maximum A Posteriori) estimation: instead of maximizing the likelihood alone, we maximize the posterior $P(w)P(\mathbf{w} \mid \mathbf{X}, \mathbf{y}) \propto P(\mathbf{y} \mid \mathbf{X}, \mathbf{w}) \cdot P(\mathbf{w})$P(w).

**Ridge regression as a Gaussian prior.** Assume the likelihood is Gaussian (i.e., yi=w⊤xi+ϵiy_i = \mathbf{w}^\top \mathbf{x}_i + \epsilon_iyi=w⊤xi+ϵi with $\epsilon_i \sim \mathcal{N}(0, \sigma^2)$), and place an independent Gaussian prior on each weight: wj∼N(0,τ2)w_j \sim \mathcal{N}(0, \tau^2)wj∼N(0,τ2). The log-posterior is:

log⁡P(w∣data)=−12σ2∑i(yi−w⊤xi)2−12τ2∥w∥2+const\log P(\mathbf{w} \mid \text{data}) = -$\sigma^2}\sum_i (y_i - \mathbf{w}^\top \mathbf{x}_i)^2 -$\tau^2}\|\mathbf{w}\|^2 + \text{const}logP(w∣data)=−2σ21i∑(yi−w⊤xi)2−2τ21∥w∥2+const

Maximizing this is equivalent to minimizing ∑i(yi−w⊤xi)2+σ2τ2∥w∥2\sum_i (y_i - \mathbf{w}^\top \mathbf{x}_i)^2 + \frac{\sigma^2}{\tau^2}\|\mathbf{w}\|^2∑i(yi−w⊤xi)2+τ2σ2∥w∥2, which is exactly Ridge regression with $\lambda = \sigma^2 / \tau^2$.

**Lasso regression as a Laplace prior.** Now place an independent Laplace prior on each weight: wj∼Laplace(0,b)w_j \sim \text{Laplace}(0, b)wj∼Laplace(0,b), with PDF p(wj)=12bexp⁡(−∣wj∣/b)p(w_j) = \frac{1}{2b}\exp(-|w_j|/b)p(wj)=2b1exp(−∣wj∣/b). The log-posterior becomes:

log⁡P(w∣data)=−12σ2∑i(yi−w⊤xi)2−1b∥w∥1+const\log P(\mathbf{w} \mid \text{data}) = -\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}\sum_i (y_i - \mathbf{w}^\top \mathbf{x}_i)^2 - \frac{1}{b}\|\mathbf{w}\|_1 + \text{const}logP(w∣data)=−2σ21i∑(yi−w⊤xi)2−b1∥w∥1+const

Maximizing this is equivalent to minimizing ∑i(yi−w⊤xi)2+2σ2b∥w∥1\sum_i (y_i - \mathbf{w}^\top \mathbf{x}_i)^2 + \frac{2\sigma^2}{b}\|\mathbf{w}\|_1∑i(yi−w⊤xi)2+b2σ2∥w∥1, which is Lasso with $\lambda = 2\sigma^2 / b$. The Laplace distribution has a sharp peak at zero, which explains geometrically why Lasso promotes exact sparsity: the prior strongly concentrates mass at wj=0w_j = 0wj=0.

**What choosing λ\lambdaλ means from a Bayesian perspective.** The ratio $\lambda = \sigma^2 / \tau^2$ (for Ridge) or $\lambda = 2\sigma^2 / b$ (for Lasso) encodes the _relative belief_ about noise magnitude versus prior scale. A large λ\lambdaλ says: the prior variance τ2\tau^2τ2 is small relative to the noise, meaning we strongly believe weights are near zero. A small λ\lambdaλ says: the prior is diffuse and we trust the data. Cross-validation for λ\lambdaλ is then an empirical procedure for estimating this ratio from data — a frequentist approximation to what a full Bayesian would compute by marginalizing over λ\lambdaλ using a hyperprior. The Bayesian perspective thus reveals that regularization is never "arbitrary" — it always encodes a belief about the magnitude of the true weights.

### Chapter Summary

Regularization prevents overfitting by penalizing model complexity. The two fundamental approaches are:

* **Ridge (L2)** : Adds $\lambda\|\mathbf{w}\|^2$ to the loss; shrinks all weights toward zero but never exactly to zero; equivalent to a Gaussian prior; has a closed-form solution $(X^\top X + \lambda m I)^{-1} X^\top \mathbf{y}$; preferred when all features are relevant
* **Lasso (L1)** : Adds $\lambda\|\mathbf{w}\|_1$ to the loss; drives many weights to exactly zero; performs automatic feature selection; equivalent to a Laplace prior; preferred when features are sparse

**Thread A checkpoint (A2):** Applying Lasso to the expanded 25-feature house price model, 17 features are eliminated automatically. The 8 surviving features are more interpretable and generalize better on validation data. This is regularization at work: not just preventing overfitting, but discovering what actually matters.

The right λ\lambdaλ is chosen by cross-validation (properly introduced in Chapter 8). In Chapter 7, we extend linear models to classification, and regularization applies there too — in fact, logistic regression without regularization is almost never used in practice.

---

## Chapter 7: Classification — Logistic Regression and Softmax

Linear regression predicts a continuous number — house prices, temperature, stock returns. But many of the most important ML problems are not about predicting numbers. Is this email spam or legitimate? Will this loan default? Is this tumor malignant or benign? Is this movie review positive or negative?

These are **classification** problems: the output is a _category_ , not a number.

In this chapter, we build two fundamental classification models — logistic regression for binary classification and softmax regression for multi-class problems — and apply them to the first appearance of our second running thread: sentiment analysis.

### 7.1 From Regression to Classification

At first, it seems like you could just use linear regression: predict $y \in \{0, 1\}$ directly with a linear model. Let's think about why this fails.

Suppose you train a linear model to predict spam (1) or not-spam (0). The model produces outputs like $\hat{y} = 0.73$ or $\hat{y} = 1.2$ or $\hat{y} = -0.4$. The values outside [0,1][0, 1][0,1] don't have obvious probabilistic meaning. Worse, the predictions are sensitive to outliers: a single obvious spam email far from the decision boundary will pull the entire line toward it, distorting predictions for borderline cases.

What we want is a model that outputs a _probability_ $\hat{p} = P(y=1 | \mathbf{x})$. From this probability, we can make a classification decision: predict class 1 if $\hat{p} > 0.5$ (or any threshold appropriate for the task).

The key design question is: how do we map a linear function $\mathbf{w}^\top \mathbf{x}$ (which can be any real number) to a probability in [0,1][0, 1][0,1]?

### 7.2 The Logistic Function

#### Definition: Logistic (Sigmoid) Function

σ(z)=11+e−z\sigma(z) = \frac{1}{1 + e^{-z}}σ(z)=1+e−z1

In plain language, the logistic function maps any real number $z$ to the interval (0,1)(0, 1)(0,1):

* As z→+∞z \to +\inftyz→+∞: $\sigma(z) \to 1$
* As z→−∞z \to -\inftyz→−∞: $\sigma(z) \to 0$
* At z=0z = 0z=0: $\sigma(0) = 0.5$

The function is S-shaped (sigmoidal): it changes slowly for extreme values of $z$ and transitions sharply near zero.

#### Definition: Logistic Regression Model

p^=P(y=1∣x)=σ(w⊤x)=11+e−w⊤x\hat{p} = P(y=1|\mathbf{x}) = \sigma($\mathbf{x}) = \frac{1}{1 + e^{-$ \mathbf{x}}}p^=P(y=1∣x)=σ(w⊤x)=1+e−w⊤x1

In plain language, compute the linear score z=w⊤xz = \mathbf{w}^\top \mathbf{x}z=w⊤x, then squeeze it through the sigmoid to get a probability.
