The **decision boundary** is where $\hat{p} = 0.5$, i.e., where $\mathbf{w}^\top \mathbf{x} = 0$. This is a hyperplane in the feature space. Logistic regression is a **linear classifier** : its decision boundary is always a straight line (or hyperplane in higher dimensions).

**Log-odds interpretation:** Define the _odds_ as $\hat{p}/(1-\hat{p})$ — the ratio of the probability of class 1 to class 0. Then:

log⁡p^1−p^=w⊤x\log\frac{\hat{p}}{1-\hat{p}} = \mathbf{w}^\top \mathbf{x}log1−p^p^=w⊤x

The log-odds (also called the _logit_) is exactly the linear score. A positive score means the log-odds favor class 1; a negative score favors class 0. Each unit increase in feature xjx_jxj changes the log-odds by wjw_jwj.

### 7.3 Training: The Cross-Entropy Loss

For logistic regression, MSE is a poor loss function — it produces a non-convex optimization landscape. Instead, we use the **cross-entropy loss** , derived from maximum likelihood.

The probability of the observed labels under our model is:

P(y∣w)=∏i=1mp^iyi(1−p^i)1−yiP(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{w}) = \prod_{i=1}^m \hat{p}_i^{y_i}(1-\hat{p}_i)^{1-y_i}P(y∣w)=i=1∏mp^iyi(1−p^i)1−yi

Taking the negative log-likelihood:

#### Definition: Binary Cross-Entropy Loss

L(w)=−1m∑i=1m[yilog⁡p^i+(1−yi)log⁡(1−p^i)]L(\mathbf{w}) = -\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^m \left[ $\log \hat{p}_i + (1-$)\log(1-\hat{p}_i) \right]L(w)=−m1i=1∑m[yilogp^i+(1−yi)log(1−p^i)]

where $\hat{p}_i = \sigma(\mathbf{w}^\top \mathbf{x}_i)$.

In plain language, this loss is high when the model is confidently wrong (predicting near 0 when the true label is 1, or vice versa) and low when the model is confidently correct.

The gradient of the cross-entropy loss is beautifully clean:

∇wL=1m∑i=1m(p^i−yi)xi=1mX⊤(p^−y)\nabla_\mathbf{w} L = $\sum_{i=1}^m (\hat{p}_i - y_i) \mathbf{x}_i =$ X^\top (\hat{\mathbf{p}} - \mathbf{y})∇wL=m1i=1∑m(p^i−yi)xi=m1X⊤(p^−y)

This has the same structure as the gradient for linear regression — just with predicted probabilities p^\hat{\mathbf{p}}p^ instead of predicted values. The update "pushes" weights to make predictions more correct: when the model underpredicts ($\hat{p}_i < y_i$), the gradient points in the direction that increases $\hat{p}_i$.

The cross-entropy loss is **convex** in $\mathbf{w}$ — so gradient descent is guaranteed to find the global minimum.

#### Common Misconception

Logistic regression is called "regression" but is used for classification. This confuses many beginners. The "regression" refers to regressing on log-odds — the model internally fits a linear function, then transforms it. The output is still a probability, and the final prediction is a class label. The naming is historical. Modern terminology would call it "logistic classification."

### 7.4 Regularization for Logistic Regression

Just as with linear regression, logistic regression without regularization can badly overfit with many features. In practice, **always regularize logistic regression**.

The regularized objectives are:

**L2-regularized (Ridge logistic):**

L(w)=−1m∑i[yilog⁡p^i+(1−yi)log⁡(1−p^i)]+λ2∥w∥$\mathbf{w}) = -\frac{1}{m}\sum_i [y_i \log\hat{p}_i + (1-y_i)\log(1-\hat{p}_i)] + \frac{\lambda}{2}\|\mathbf{w}\|^$w)=−m1i∑[yilogp^i+(1−yi)log(1−p^i)]+2λ∥w∥2

**L1-regularized (Lasso logistic):**

L(w)=−1m∑i[yilog⁡p^i+(1−yi)log⁡(1−p^i)]+λ∥w∥$\mathbf{w}) = -\frac{1}{m}\sum_i [y_i \log\hat{p}_i + (1-y_i)\log(1-\hat{p}_i)] + \lambda\|\mathbf{w}\|_$w)=−m1i∑[yilogp^i+(1−yi)log(1−p^i)]+λ∥w∥1

The same intuitions from Chapter 6 apply: L2 shrinks all weights; L1 produces sparse solutions. For text classification (our Thread B application), L1 is often preferred because most words are irrelevant to the label.

### 7.5 Multi-Class Classification: Softmax Regression

What if there are more than 2 classes? For example, classifying handwritten digits 0–9 (10 classes), or categorizing news articles into 20 topic categories.

#### Definition: Softmax Regression

For $K$ classes, softmax regression learns a weight vector wk\mathbf{w}_kwk for each class $k$. The probability of class $k$ given input $\mathbf{x}$ is:

P(y=k∣x)=softmax(wk⊤x)k=ewk⊤x∑j=1Kewj⊤xP(y = k | \mathbf{x}) = \text{softmax}(\mathbf{w}^\top_k \mathbf{x})_k = \frac{e^{\mathbf{w}_k$\mathbf{x}}}{\sum_{j=1}^K e^{\mathbf{w}_j$ \mathbf{x}}}P(y=k∣x)=softmax(wk⊤x)k=∑j=1Kewj⊤xewk⊤x

The **softmax function** maps a vector of $K$ real scores to a valid probability distribution (all non-negative, summing to 1):

softmax(z)k=ezk∑j=1Kezj\text{softmax}(\mathbf{z})_k = \frac{e^{z_k}}{\sum_{j=1}^K e^{z_j}}softmax(z)k=∑j=1Kezjezk

In plain language: compute a score for each class, exponentiate all scores, then normalize so they sum to 1. The class with the highest score gets the highest probability.

**Numerical stability note:** When computing softmax, subtract the maximum score from all scores first: softmax(z)=softmax(z−max⁡kzk)\text{softmax}(\mathbf{z}) = \text{softmax}(\mathbf{z} - \max_k z_k)softmax(z)=softmax(z−maxkzk). This prevents overflow from large exponentials.

The loss for softmax regression is the **categorical cross-entropy** :

L({Wk})=−1m∑i=1mlog⁡P(yi∣xi)=−1m∑i=1mlog⁡softmax(W⊤xi)yiL(\\{W_k\\}) = $\log P(y_i | \mathbf{x}_i) =$ \log \text{softmax}(W^\top \mathbf{x}_i)_{y_i}L({Wk})=−m1i=1∑mlogP(yi∣xi)=−m1i=1∑mlogsoftmax(W⊤xi)yi

This is exactly the loss function used in the final layer of neural networks for multi-class classification (Chapter 15).

**Connection to binary logistic regression:** For K=2K = 2K=2, softmax regression reduces to logistic regression. The softmax is a generalization.

### 7.6 Thread B — Sentiment Analysis with Bag-of-Words (B1)

_[Thread B: Sentiment Analysis — B1: Bag-of-words features + logistic regression; ~85% accuracy]_

We introduce our second running thread: **sentiment analysis** on movie reviews. The task: given a text review, predict whether it's positive or negative.

**Dataset:** 50,000 IMDb movie reviews, labeled positive (1) or negative (0). 25,000 for training, 25,000 for testing.

**Feature representation — Bag of Words:**

The simplest way to represent text as a vector: count how often each word in the vocabulary appears in the document.

For a vocabulary of V=10,000V = 10,000V=10,000 common words, each review becomes a vector $\mathbf{x} \in \mathbb{R}^{10,000}$ where xjx_jxj = number of times word $j$ appears in the review.

This is called _bag of words_ because it treats the document as an unordered collection (bag) of words — it ignores grammar, word order, and context. "This movie is not good" and "This movie is good, not bad" would have very similar bag-of-words representations despite very different meanings.

**TF-IDF Weighting:** Instead of raw counts, use **Term Frequency–Inverse Document Frequency (TF-IDF)** :

TF-IDF(w,d)=count(w,d)∣d∣⏟TF×log⁡∣D∣∣{d′:w∈d′}∣⏟IDF\text{TF-IDF}(w, d) = \underbrace{\frac{\text{count}(w,d)}{|d$\text{TF}} \times \underbrace{\log\frac{|D|}{|\\{d' : w \in d'\\}$\text{IDF}}TF-IDF(w,d)=TF∣d∣count(w,d)×IDFlog∣{d′:w∈d′}∣∣D∣

Words that appear frequently in a specific document (high TF) but rarely across all documents (high IDF) are highly weighted. Common words like "the" and "is" (high TF, low IDF) are down-weighted. Distinctive words like "thrilling" or "disappointing" (moderate TF, moderate IDF) are up-weighted.

**Results with Lasso logistic regression (L1 penalty, λ\lambdaλ tuned by cross-validation):**

* Training accuracy: 91%
* Test accuracy: **85%**

The Lasso selects about 800 non-zero features out of 10,000 vocabulary words. The top positive words (highest weights) include: _brilliant, masterpiece, captivating, exceptional, touching, riveting_. The top negative words include: _terrible, boring, awful, dreadful, waste, unwatchable_.

These are interpretable and sensible! The model has learned that certain words are strong indicators of sentiment without being told anything about language semantics.

**Limitations:** The bag-of-words model ignores word order. "Not good" and "good" are not clearly distinguished. Sarcasm is invisible. The same word in different contexts (e.g., "this movie is sick" — negative in 1985, positive in 2005) is treated identically.

We will return to this dataset in Chapter 19 with word embeddings, in Chapter 17 with LSTMs, and in Chapter 20 with transformers. Each upgrade will address some of these limitations and push accuracy higher.

**Baseline established: 85% accuracy with bag-of-words + logistic regression.**

### 7.7 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** A logistic regression model predicts $\hat{p} = \sigma(2.3)$ for a particular input. Compute this probability numerically. What is the predicted class?

**Answer:**

p^=σ(2.3)=11+e−2.3=11+e−$\hat{p} = \sigma(2.3) = \frac{1}{1 + e^{-2.3}} = \frac{1}{1 + e^{-$}}p^=σ(2.3)=1+e−2.31=1+e−2.31

e−2.3≈e−2⋅e−0.3≈0.1353×0.7408≈0.1003e^{-2$\approx e^{-2} \cdot e^{-0$ \approx 0.1353 \times 0.7408 \approx 0.1003e−2.3≈e−2⋅e−0.3≈0.1353×0.7408≈0.1003

p^≈11.1003≈$\hat{p} \approx \frac{1}{1.1003} \approx$p^≈1.10031≈0.909

The predicted probability of class 1 is approximately 91%. Since $\hat{p} > 0.5$, the predicted class is **1**.

**Q2.** The binary cross-entropy loss is L=−[ylog⁡p^+(1−y)log⁡(1−p^)]L = -[y\log\hat{p} + (1-y)\log(1-\hat{p})]L=−[ylogp^+(1−y)log(1−p^)]. Compute the loss when y=1y = 1y=1 and $\hat{p} = 0.95$, and when y=1y = 1y=1 and $\hat{p} = 0.05$.

**Answer:**

When y=1,p^=0.95y = 1, \hat{p} = 0.95y=1,p^=0.95: L=−[log⁡(0.95)]=−(−0.051)=0.051L = -[\log(0.95)] = -(-0.051) = 0.051L=−[log(0.95)]=−(−0.051)=0.051 (very small — model is confident and correct)

When y=1,p^=0.05y = 1, \hat{p} = 0.05y=1,p^=0.05: L=−[log⁡(0.05)]=−(−3.00)=3.00L = -[\log(0.05)] = -(-3.00) = 3.00L=−[log(0.05)]=−(−3.00)=3.00 (very large — model is confidently wrong)

This illustrates the cross-entropy's behavior: it penalizes overconfident wrong predictions harshly (loss = 3.0) but barely penalizes confident correct predictions (loss = 0.051).

**Q3.** For softmax with K=3K = 3K=3 classes, the scores are z1=2.0,z2=1.0,z3=0.1z_1 = 2.0, z_2 = 1.0, z_3 = 0.1z1=2.0,z2=1.0,z3=0.1. Compute the softmax probabilities.

**Answer:**

Exponentiate: e2.0≈7.389e^{2.0} \approx 7.389e2.0≈7.389, e1.0≈2.718e^{1.0} \approx 2.718e1.0≈2.718, e0$.105e^{0.1} \approx 1.105e0$.105.

Sum: 7.389+2.718+1.105=11.2127.389 + 2.718 + 1.105 = 11.2127.389+2.718+1.105=11.212.

Probabilities: P1=7.389/11.212≈0.659P_1 = $\approx 0.659P1=$≈0.659, P2=2.718/11.212≈0.242P_2 = $\approx 0.242P2=$≈0.242, P3=1.105/11.212≈0.099P_3 = $\approx 0.099P3=$≈0.099.

Check: 0.659+0.242+0.099=1.0000.659 + 0.242 + 0.099 = 1.0000.659+0.242+0.099=1.000 ✓

Class 1 has the highest score and the highest probability. Even though z1−z3=1.9z_1 - z_3 = 1.9z1−z3=1.9 (not that large), the exponential amplifies the score differences: class 1 is 6.7× more probable than class 3.

**Q4.** What happens to the decision boundary of logistic regression if you double all weights $\mathbf{w}$ but keep the threshold at $\hat{p} > 0.5$? What happens to the predicted probabilities?

**Answer:** The decision boundary ($\mathbf{w}^\top \mathbf{x} = 0$) does **not change** — it's still the same hyperplane. Doubling all weights doesn't move the hyperplane (which only depends on the _direction_ of $\mathbf{w}$, not its magnitude).

However, the predicted probabilities _do_ change. With doubled weights, $\sigma(2\mathbf{w}^\top \mathbf{x})$: points near the boundary (small ∣w⊤x∣|\mathbf{w}^\top \mathbf{x}|∣w⊤x∣) are pushed toward the extremes 0 and 1. The model becomes "more confident" — probabilities closer to 0 or 1 — but with the same classification boundary.

This shows why predicted probabilities from logistic regression should be taken with a grain of salt: the magnitudes of weights affect confidence, not just the direction.

#### Application Problems

**Q1.** You're building a medical diagnosis classifier. The disease is rare: only 1% of patients have it. A baseline model that predicts "no disease" for everyone achieves 99% accuracy. Your logistic regression model achieves 96% accuracy. Should you prefer the baseline or your model? How should you evaluate?

Hint

Recall the base rate fallacy from Chapter 3. Think about which type of error is more costly in medical diagnosis.

**Answer:** The 96% accuracy model may actually be **worse** than the baseline if it misses more disease cases. With 1% prevalence: a model with 96% accuracy could be doing almost anything — it might correctly classify 96% of the healthy patients while getting _most_ disease cases wrong.

The right metrics are:

* **Sensitivity (recall for positive class):** What fraction of actual disease cases does the model catch? The baseline catches 0% (it never predicts disease). Even if your model catches 50% of cases, that's infinitely better than 0%.
* **Specificity:** What fraction of healthy patients are correctly cleared?
* **Positive Predictive Value (PPV):** Of patients flagged by the model, what fraction actually have the disease?

A proper evaluation: compute the **confusion matrix** (true positives, false positives, true negatives, false negatives), **ROC curve** (sensitivity vs. 1-specificity at all thresholds), and **AUC** (area under ROC curve). The baseline achieves AUC = 0.5 (random). Any model with AUC > 0.5 and non-zero sensitivity is strictly better. We cover these metrics in detail in Chapter 8.

**Q2.** You're building a bag-of-words sentiment classifier like Thread B. Feature vocabulary has 20,000 words. You train Lasso logistic regression and the training accuracy is 99% but test accuracy is 78%. What's likely happening, and what changes would you make?

Hint

Compare training vs. test accuracy. This is the classic signature of what problem from Chapter 1?

**Answer:** The large gap (99% train, 78% test) is a classic overfitting signature. The model memorizes the specific vocabulary patterns in the training set — perhaps learning to associate specific reviewer names, movie titles, or idiosyncratic phrasings with positive/negative sentiment, rather than the general sentiment words.

Causes and fixes:

  1. **Regularization too weak** (most likely): the Lasso λ\lambdaλ is too small. Increase λ\lambdaλ via cross-validation. A higher penalty will zero out more irrelevant words and force the model to focus on the most generalizable features.
  2. **Vocabulary too large** : prune rare words (those appearing in fewer than 5 reviews). They add noise features with no predictive signal.
  3. **Stop words not removed** : common words ("the", "a", "of") add noise dimensions. Remove them.
  4. **Feature engineering** : use TF-IDF weighting instead of raw counts; it down-weights very common words automatically.
  5. **More training data** : if possible, adding more labeled examples reduces overfitting for any regularization level.

The target: bring training accuracy down to ~88–90% (accept slightly worse training accuracy) to get test accuracy up to ~85%+ (our Thread B baseline). The goal is _generalization_ , not training performance.

#### Think Deeper

**Q1.** Logistic regression produces a linear decision boundary. Suppose the true boundary is non-linear (e.g., a circle: class 1 inside the circle, class 0 outside). How can you use logistic regression to handle this? Describe the approach and discuss when this works well or fails.

**Discussion:** We can add _polynomial features_ to create a non-linear boundary with a linear model. For 2D features x1,x2x_1, x_2x1,x2:

Create features: x1,x2,x12,x22,x1x2,x13,x23,x12x2,x1x22,…x_1, x_2, x_1^2, x_2^2, x_1 x_2, x_1^3, x_2^3, x_1^2 x_2, x_1 x_2^2, \ldotsx1,x2,x12,x22,x1x2,x13,x23,x12x2,x1x22,…

A logistic regression on these features can learn a circular boundary. For example, the circular boundary x12+x22=r2x_1^2 + x_2^2 = r^2x12+x22=r2 corresponds to the hyperplane w1x12+w2x22=bw_1 x_1^2 + w_2 x_2^2 = bw1x12+w2x22=b in feature space, which is achievable with polynomial features [x12,x22][x_1^2, x_2^2][x12,x22] and weights [w1,w2][w_1, w_2][w1,w2].

**When it works well:** When you know the rough shape of the true boundary and can engineer appropriate features. Small to medium datasets where the feature space isn't too large.

**When it fails:** (1) When the degree required is unknown — trying all polynomial degrees up to $d$ produces O(nd/d!)O(n^d / d!)O(nd/d!) features, which can be enormous. (2) In high dimensions ($n$ features), even degree-2 polynomials produce O(n2)O(n^2)O(n2) features — 10 features produces 55 polynomial features; 100 features produces 5,050. This leads to overfitting unless heavily regularized. (3) When the true boundary has a shape that no polynomial of reasonable degree can capture (e.g., fractal boundaries).

This motivates the kernel trick for SVMs (Chapter 11) — a principled way to work in an implicitly infinite-dimensional feature space without enumerating all features, and decision trees (Chapter 9) — which can learn arbitrary non-linear boundaries by partitioning the feature space recursively.

**Q2.** Why is cross-entropy the "right" loss function for probabilistic classifiers? Show that minimizing cross-entropy over the training set is equivalent to minimizing the KL divergence between the empirical label distribution and the model's predicted distribution.

**Discussion:** Let the empirical distribution over labels at input xi\mathbf{x}_ixi be qiq_iqi: a point mass at the true label yiy_iyi. The model produces a predicted distribution pi=(p^i1,…,p^iK)p_i = (\hat{p}_{i1}, \ldots, \hat{p}_{iK})pi=(p^i1,…,p^iK) over $K$ classes. The KL divergence from qiq_iqi to pip_ipi measures how well pip_ipi approximates qiq_iqi:

DKL(qi∥pi)=∑k=1Kqi(k)log⁡qi(k)pi(k)=∑k=1Kqi(k)log⁡qi(k)−∑k=1Kqi(k)log⁡pi(k)D_{\mathrm{KL}}($\| p_i) = \sum_{k=1}^K q_i(k) \log \frac{q_i(k)}{p_i(k)} = \sum_{k=1}^K q_i(k) \log q_i(k) - \sum_{k=1}^K$(k) \log p_i(k)DKL(qi∥pi)=k=1∑Kqi(k)logpi(k)qi(k)=k=1∑Kqi(k)logqi(k)−k=1∑Kqi(k)logpi(k)

The first term is −H(qi)-H(q_i)−H(qi), the negative entropy of the true distribution. Since qiq_iqi is the empirical distribution fixed by the data, this term is a constant with respect to the model parameters. Minimizing DKL(qi∥pi)D_{\mathrm{KL}}(q_i \| p_i)DKL(qi∥pi) over model parameters is therefore equivalent to minimizing −∑kqi(k)log⁡pi(k)-\sum_k q$\log p$−∑kqi(k)logpi(k). Averaged over all training examples, and using the fact that qiq_iqi is a point mass at yiy_iyi (so only the k=yik = y_ik=yi term survives):

1m∑i=1mDKL(qi∥pi)=const+1m∑i=1m(−log⁡p^i,yi)=const+LCE$\sum_{i=1}^m D_{\mathrm{KL}}(q_i \| p_i) = \text{const} +$\sum_{i=1}^m \left(-\log \hat{p}_{i, y_i}\right) = \text{const} + \mathcal{L}_{\text{CE}}m1i=1∑mDKL(qi∥pi)=const+m1i=1∑m(−logp^i,yi)=const+LCE

Minimizing cross-entropy is exactly minimizing the average KL divergence between the empirical label distribution and the model's predictions.

**Why this makes cross-entropy the "right" loss.** KL divergence DKL(q∥p)D_{\mathrm{KL}}(q \| p)DKL(q∥p) is zero if and only if p=qp = qp=q, and positive otherwise (by Gibbs' inequality). It is the unique measure (up to scaling) with certain natural properties: it is zero only at the correct distribution, it is additive over independent components, and it is invariant under reparametrization. Minimizing cross-entropy therefore drives the model toward the true data-generating distribution in a mathematically principled sense.

This connection also explains why MSE is the wrong loss for classification. MSE treats the label space as Euclidean: it penalizes predicting class 2 when class 1 is correct the same way regardless of whether class 2 and class 1 are "close" in any meaningful sense. Cross-entropy treats the model output as a probability distribution and measures how much information is lost when $p$ is used to encode samples from $q$ — a quantity that directly reflects predictive quality, not Euclidean distance in label space.

Finally, this framing clarifies what a well-trained classifier is doing: it is approximating, as closely as the model's capacity allows, the true conditional distribution $P(y \mid \mathbf{x})$ over labels. Logistic regression (with a linear decision boundary) finds the best linear approximation to this distribution in the KL-divergence sense. Neural networks with enough capacity can approximate the true distribution arbitrarily well — and cross-entropy is what guides them there.

### Chapter Summary

This chapter moved from regression (predicting numbers) to classification (predicting categories). Key ideas:

* **Logistic regression** wraps a linear model in the sigmoid function to produce a probability: $\hat{p} = \sigma(\mathbf{w}^\top \mathbf{x})$
* The loss function is **cross-entropy** (not MSE), derived from maximum likelihood under a Bernoulli model
* **Softmax regression** generalizes logistic regression to $K$ classes: compute a score per class, exponentiate, normalize
* Both models have **linear decision boundaries** in the original feature space (non-linear boundaries require feature engineering)
* **Regularization** is essential: L1-regularized logistic regression is the standard starting point for text classification

**Thread B checkpoint (B1):** Bag-of-words + Lasso logistic regression achieves **85% accuracy** on movie sentiment analysis. The model is interpretable (Lasso identifies ~800 key sentiment words), but ignores word order and context. We will return in Chapters 19, 17, and 20 with successively more powerful representations.

Chapter 8 addresses a crucial question we've been deferring: how do we properly measure whether our models are learning or memorizing? We need a systematic methodology for model evaluation.

---

## Chapter 8: Model Evaluation — Are We Learning or Memorizing?

In Chapter 7, our sentiment classifier achieved 91% training accuracy and 85% test accuracy. In Chapter 6, our regularized house price model "explained 73% of variance" on the training data. But every number we've reported so far has been slightly informal — we haven't established a rigorous methodology for measuring whether our models actually generalize.

This chapter fills that gap. Proper model evaluation is not a detail — it is the foundation of trustworthy machine learning. Every major ML deployment failure can be traced to some form of evaluation error: testing on data that was somehow seen during training, using the wrong metric for the problem, or reporting optimistic numbers that fall apart in production.

### 8.1 The Fundamental Evaluation Principle

The goal of a supervised learning model is to perform well on **new, unseen data** from the same distribution as the training data. Crucially: this means you can never use data to both learn patterns _and_ estimate how well you've learned.

This leads to the core principle:

> **The test set must be completely isolated from all model development decisions — including hyperparameter tuning, feature selection, and architecture choices.**

Any time you look at test set performance and adjust your model accordingly, you are effectively training on the test set. The test error estimate is then too optimistic — it no longer reflects true generalization performance.

In practice, this means:

* **Training set** : used to optimize model parameters (minimize the loss)
* **Validation set** : used to tune hyperparameters and make model selection decisions
* **Test set** : locked away until the final evaluation; used exactly once

Many ML papers and Kaggle competitions have been criticized for overfitting to the test set — reporting numbers that look great on the specific test examples used but wouldn't hold up on genuinely new data.

### 8.2 Train/Validation/Test Splits

#### Definition: Train/Validation/Test Split

Given a labeled dataset of $m$ examples, partition it into three non-overlapping sets:

* **Training set** (~70%): Used to fit model parameters
* **Validation set** (~15%): Used for hyperparameter tuning and model selection
* **Test set** (~15%): Used once for final performance reporting

A typical split might be 70/15/15 or 60/20/20, depending on the total dataset size.

**For small datasets:** With m<1000m < 1000m<1000, a 70/15/15 split leaves very few validation and test examples — estimates will be noisy. Prefer cross-validation (Section 8.3).

**For large datasets:** With m>100,000m > 100,000m>100,000, the validation and test sets don't need 15% each — a few thousand examples is sufficient for stable estimates. A 98/1/1 split is reasonable.

**Stratification:** For classification with imbalanced classes, use _stratified_ splits: ensure each split has the same class proportions as the full dataset. A random split on a dataset with 95% class 0 and 5% class 1 might produce a test set with no class-1 examples.

**Temporal ordering:** For time series data (stock prices, user behavior), _never_ randomly shuffle before splitting. Put the earliest data in training, middle in validation, last in test. A model that "learns" from future data to predict the past is useless in production.

### 8.3 Cross-Validation

When the dataset is small, a single train/validation split can give noisy estimates. **k kk-fold cross-validation** provides a more reliable estimate.

#### Definition: k-Fold Cross-Validation

  1. Partition the dataset into $k$ equal-sized folds
  2. For each fold i=1,…,ki = 1, \ldots, ki=1,…,k: 
     * Train on all folds except fold $i$
     * Evaluate on fold $i$ — record the validation error
  3. The cross-validation error is the average of the $k$ fold errors

CV error=1k∑i=1kerrori\text{CV error} = \frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^k \text{error}_iCV error=k1i=1∑kerrori

In plain language, each example gets to be in the validation set exactly once, and in the training set (k−1)(k-1)(k−1) times. Common choices: k=5k = 5k=5 or k=10k = 10k=10.

**Leave-One-Out Cross-Validation (LOO-CV):** The extreme case k=mk = mk=m — leave out one example at a time. Provides the least biased estimate of generalization error but is computationally expensive for large $m$. Used when data is very scarce.

**Nested cross-validation:** For proper hyperparameter tuning with cross-validation, use an _outer_ loop (to estimate generalization error) and an _inner_ loop (to select hyperparameters). The outer loop ensures that hyperparameter selection doesn't bias the error estimate.

**Important:** Cross-validation does not replace the test set. Use cross-validation for hyperparameter tuning on training data; reserve the test set for the final number.

#### Common Misconception

Many students believe cross-validation _replaces_ the need for a test set. It does not. Cross-validation gives a better estimate of validation error (for model selection) but still uses the same data for selection. If you tune hyperparameters based on 5-fold CV scores and then report those CV scores as your generalization estimate, you've effectively overfit to the CV procedure. Always hold out a separate test set.

### 8.4 Bias-Variance Tradeoff: Diagnostic Tools

How do you know if your model has high bias, high variance, or both? **Learning curves** are the standard diagnostic.

A **learning curve** plots model performance as a function of training set size. To generate it:

  1. Train the model on m1<m2<…<mm_1 < m_2 < \ldots < mm1<m2<…<m subsets of increasing size
  2. For each size, record both training error and validation error
  3. Plot both curves vs. training size

**High bias (underfitting):**

* Both training and validation errors are high
* The gap between them is small
* Adding more data doesn't help much — the model is too simple to capture the pattern

**High variance (overfitting):**

* Training error is low, validation error is much higher
* Large gap between training and validation
* Adding more data _helps_ — with more data, the model can't overfit as easily

**Good fit:**

* Both training and validation errors are low and converging
* Small gap between them

Reading learning curves is one of the most practical skills in applied ML. Before trying a more complex model, always check if your current model is in the high-bias or high-variance regime — the fix is completely different.

### 8.5 Classification Metrics

Accuracy — fraction of correctly classified examples — is often the wrong metric. Here's why:

**The class imbalance problem:** If 99% of credit card transactions are legitimate and 1% are fraudulent, a model that always predicts "legitimate" achieves 99% accuracy but catches 0% of fraud. This is the "accuracy paradox."

**Asymmetric costs:** In cancer screening, missing a cancer (false negative) is far more costly than an unnecessary follow-up (false positive). Accuracy treats both errors equally.

#### The Confusion Matrix

For binary classification:

| Predicted Positive | Predicted Negative  
---|---|---  
**Actual Positive** | True Positive (TP) | False Negative (FN)  
**Actual Negative** | False Positive (FP) | True Negative (TN)  
  
From the confusion matrix, define:

#### Definition: Precision and Recall

Precision=TPTP+FP(of those we predicted positive, how many are actually positive?)\text{Precision} = \frac{TP}{TP + FP} \quad \text{(of those we predicted positive, how many are actually positive?)}Precision=TP+FPTP(of those we predicted positive, how many are actually positive?) Recall (Sensitivity)=TPTP+FN(of all actual positives, how many did we catch?)\text{Recall (Sensitivity)} = \frac{TP}{TP + FN} \quad \text{(of all actual positives, how many did we catch?)}Recall (Sensitivity)=TP+FNTP(of all actual positives, how many did we catch?) F1 Score=2⋅Precision⋅RecallPrecision+Recall(harmonic mean, balances both)\text{F1 Score} = \frac{2 \cdot \text{Precision} \cdot \text{Recall}}{\text{Precision} + \text{Recall}} \quad \text{(harmonic mean, balances both)}F1 Score=Precision+Recall2⋅Precision⋅Recall(harmonic mean, balances both)

**Precision–Recall tradeoff:** By adjusting the classification threshold (instead of always using 0.5), you can increase recall at the cost of precision, or vice versa. A **precision-recall curve** shows all achievable precision-recall combinations.

**Specificity** = TN/(TN+FP)TN/(TN+FP)TN/(TN+FP): fraction of negatives correctly identified. High specificity = few false positives.

#### Definition: ROC Curve and AUC

The **ROC curve (Receiver Operating Characteristic)** plots sensitivity (recall) vs. 1-specificity (false positive rate) as the classification threshold varies from 0 to 1.

The **AUC (Area Under the ROC Curve)** summarizes the ROC curve in a single number:

* AUC = 1.0: perfect classifier
* AUC = 0.5: random classifier (diagonal line)
* AUC < 0.5: worse than random (usually indicates a labeling flip)

AUC is threshold-independent and measures the classifier's _ranking ability_ : the probability that a randomly chosen positive example is ranked higher than a randomly chosen negative example.

**When to use which metric:**

* **Accuracy** : only when classes are balanced and errors are equally costly
* **Precision/Recall** : when positive class is rare or false negatives and false positives have very different costs
* **F1** : when you need a single number that balances precision and recall
* **AUC** : when you need to compare classifiers across all thresholds; good for ranking tasks

#### Common Misconception

High AUC guarantees nothing about calibration. A model with AUC = 0.95 might produce predicted probabilities of 0.48 and 0.52 for negatives and positives respectively — great for ranking, but the predicted probabilities are nearly useless for decision-making. **Calibration** (do predicted probabilities match actual frequencies?) is a separate property from discrimination (does the model rank correctly?).

### 8.6 Regression Metrics

For regression tasks, the standard metrics are:

**Mean Squared Error (MSE):** 1m$\frac{1}{m}\sum_i (y_i - \hat{y}_i)^2m1$ — penalizes large errors quadratically; units are squared (e.g., dollars²)

**Root Mean Squared Error (RMSE):** MSE\sqrt{\text{MSE}}MSE — same units as the target; most interpretable

**Mean Absolute Error (MAE):** 1m$\frac{1}{m}\sum_i |y_i - \hat{y}_i|m1$ — more robust to outliers; units same as target

**R 2R^2R2 (Coefficient of Determination):** 1−∑i(yi−y^i)2∑i(yi−yˉ)21 - \frac{\sum_i (y_i - \hat{y}_i)^2}{\sum_i (y_i - \bar{y})^2}1−∑i(yi−yˉ)2∑i(yi−y^i)2 — fraction of variance explained; 1 is perfect, 0 is "no better than the mean"

**Mean Absolute Percentage Error (MAPE):** 1m∑i∣yi−y^iyi∣\frac{1}{m}\sum_i |\frac{y_i - \hat{y}_i}{y_i}|m1∑i∣yiyi−y^i∣ — percentage errors; useful when relative accuracy matters (dangerous when yi≈0y_i \approx 0yi≈0)

For the house price model: RMSE is the most interpretable ("average prediction error is 28,000 USD") and $R^2$ provides a relative comparison ("explains 73% of price variation").

### 8.7 Data Leakage: The Silent Killer

**Data leakage** occurs when information from outside the training distribution flows into the model evaluation, producing deceptively optimistic results. It's called "leakage" because knowledge about the test data "leaks" into the training process.

**Temporal leakage:** Using future information to predict the past. Training a stock prediction model on the entire history 2000-2020, then evaluating on 2015-2020 (which was in the training set). Fix: strict temporal splits.

**Target leakage:** A feature that contains or is caused by the target variable. Example: predicting whether a patient will be readmitted to hospital, but including "readmission date" as a feature (which is undefined if not readmitted). Fix: carefully audit features for causal relationship to the target.

**Preprocessing leakage:** Applying preprocessing (normalization, feature scaling, imputation) to the _entire_ dataset before splitting. The validation statistics are contaminated by information from validation/test data. Fix: always fit preprocessing parameters (mean, std) on _training data only_ , then apply to validation/test.

**Group leakage:** When data has natural groups (e.g., multiple measurements from the same patient), randomly splitting may put the same patient in both training and test. The model learns patient-specific patterns rather than generalizable ones. Fix: _group-aware splits_ that keep all examples from one group together.

**A rule of thumb:** Ask, "Could this feature be available at inference time, before the target is known?" If not, it's a leakage feature.

### 8.8 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** A dataset has 10,000 examples with class imbalance: 9,500 negative, 500 positive. A model achieves 98% accuracy. What is the minimum number of true positives (TPs) the model could have while achieving this accuracy?

**Answer:** The model correctly classifies $0.98 \times 10,000 = 9,800$ examples. If the model gets all 9,500 negatives correct and 300 positives correct: total correct = 9,800, accuracy = 98%. So minimum TP = 300, meaning the model misses 200 of 500 positive examples (recall = 60%).

Alternatively, minimum TPs: the model could get 9,300 of 9,500 negatives correct (500 wrong) and all 500 positives correct: 9,800 correct total. So the minimum is actually 0 TPs, as long as 9,800 other examples are correct: 9,800 negatives correct, 0 positives (recall = 0%).

Concretely: 9,800/9,800 negatives + 0/500 positives = 9,800 correct = 98% accuracy. This is the "always predict negative" strategy with exceptions that achieves 98% accuracy with 0% recall. Accuracy is completely uninformative here.

**Q2.** You're training a model to detect rare adverse drug reactions (1 in 10,000 patients). Would you prefer to optimize for: (a) accuracy, (b) AUC, (c) recall, or (d) F1? Justify.

**Answer:** For rare events with asymmetric costs, **recall** or **sensitivity** is the primary metric — we want to catch as many true adverse reactions as possible (missing one could be life-threatening). Secondary: we need some minimum precision to avoid overwhelming the medical team with false alarms. The tradeoff between catching reactions and false alarms is best captured by the **precision-recall curve**.

**AUC** is also useful as a summary metric for model ranking ability.

**Accuracy** is useless here — a model that never predicts an adverse reaction achieves 99.99% accuracy.

**F1** is reasonable but treats precision and recall as equally important. In most medical safety contexts, recall is more important (missing a reaction is worse than a false alarm). Use a weighted version: Fβ=(1+β2)⋅Precision⋅Recallβ2⋅Precision+RecallF_\beta = (1+\beta^2) \cdot \frac{\text{Precision} \cdot \text{Recall}}{\beta^2 \cdot \text{Precision} + \text{Recall}}Fβ=(1+β2)⋅β2⋅Precision+RecallPrecision⋅Recall with $\beta > 1$ to weight recall more heavily.

**Q3.** What's wrong with this evaluation procedure: "I trained 10 different models, evaluated each on the test set, and reported the best result."

**Answer:** By selecting the best of 10 models based on test set performance, you've effectively used the test set for model selection — a form of data leakage. The reported performance is optimistically biased because it capitalizes on random variation: the best-performing model may have gotten "lucky" on this particular test set.

Correct procedure: train all models, select the best one using the _validation set_ (or cross-validation), then evaluate the selected model on the test set exactly once. The test set performance of the selected model is your unbiased performance estimate.

**Q4.** Explain why computing accuracy on the training set is not a useful measure of model quality.

**Answer:** Training accuracy measures how well the model fits data it was trained on — but the whole point of machine learning is to generalize to _new_ data. A model that memorizes the training set verbatim achieves 100% training accuracy but may fail completely on new data. Training accuracy provides no information about generalization; it only measures how well the model has overfit. The correct measure of model quality is always performance on data not used during training.

#### Application Problems

**Q1.** You're building a credit card fraud detector. Out of 100,000 transactions: 99,700 legitimate, 300 fraudulent. Your model predicts 200 transactions as fraud, of which 150 are actually fraudulent. Compute: precision, recall, specificity, F1, and accuracy. Which metric best reflects the model's usefulness?

Hint

First, fill in the confusion matrix: TP = 150, FP = 50, FN = 150, TN = 99,650.

**Answer:** From the problem:

* TP = 150 (correctly flagged fraud)
* FP = 200 - 150 = 50 (legitimately flagged as fraud — false alarms)
* FN = 300 - 150 = 150 (missed fraud)
* TN = 99,700 - 50 = 99,650 (correctly cleared)

**Precision** = TP/(TP+FP) = 150/200 = **75%** — 3 in 4 flagged transactions are actually fraudulent

**Recall** = TP/(TP+FN) = 150/300 = **50%** — the model catches half of all fraud

**Specificity** = TN/(TN+FP) = 99,650/99,700 = **99.95%** — almost all legitimate transactions are correctly cleared

**F1** = 2·(0.75·0.50)/(0.75+0.50) = 2·0.375/1.25 = **60%**

**Accuracy** = (TP+TN)/(total) = (150+99,650)/100,000 = **99.8%** — but this is nearly identical to the "always legitimate" baseline accuracy of 99.7%

For fraud detection, **recall** is arguably most important (catching fraud) balanced against precision (not overwhelming fraud analysts with false positives). The F1 score of 60% gives a balanced single number. Accuracy is nearly useless. In practice, you'd tune the classification threshold to find the precision-recall tradeoff that best fits the business constraint (e.g., "investigate at most 500 transactions per day, catch as much fraud as possible").

**Q2.** Describe a data leakage scenario in the following problem: "Predict whether a patient will be hospitalized in the next 30 days, using features extracted from their electronic health record." Give two specific features that would leak the target, and two that would not.

**Answer:**

**Leaking features:**

* "Number of hospitalizations in the past month" — If we're predicting hospitalization in the next 30 days, a patient who was recently hospitalized is coded with 1+ for this feature. More importantly, if we're predicting _future_ hospitalization and the training data spans multiple years, this feature for a given time window includes information from future windows.
* "Number of referrals to specialist within next 2 weeks" — Directly involves future information.

**Non-leaking features (available before the event):**

* "Number of chronic conditions (diabetes, hypertension, etc.) as of the prediction date"
* "Age, gender, BMI measured at the most recent clinic visit before the prediction date"
* "Number of ER visits in the past 12 months (ending on prediction date)"

The key test: could this feature be observed _before_ the 30-day window begins? If yes, it's legitimate. If it requires any knowledge of what happens during or after the 30 days, it leaks.

#### Think Deeper

**Q1.** The AUC is often described as "the probability that the model ranks a random positive example higher than a random negative example." Can you prove this equivalence? Hint: think about all possible orderings of one positive and one negative example.

**Discussion:** Let the model assign score s+s_+s+ to a positive example and s−s_-s− to a negative example. The ROC curve traces sensitivity vs. (1-specificity) as the threshold $t$ varies.

For a threshold $t$:

* True positive rate (sensitivity) = P(s+>t)=1−F+(t)P(s_+ > t) = 1 - F_+(t)P(s+>t)=1−F+(t) where F+F_+F+ is the CDF of positive scores
* False positive rate = P(s−>t)=1−F−(t)P(s_- > t) = 1 - F_-(t)P(s−>t)=1−F−(t)

The AUC is the area under the ROC curve. Using change of variables ($u$ = false positive rate):

AUC=∫01TPR(t(u)) du\text{AUC} = \int_0^1 \text{TPR}(t(u)) \, duAUC=∫01TPR(t(u))du

After formal substitution, this equals:

$\text{AUC} = P(s_+ > s_-)$

which is the probability that the model ranks a random positive higher than a random negative. This is called the **Wilcoxon-Mann-Whitney statistic** in classical statistics — a deep connection between ROC analysis and non-parametric rank tests.

The practical implication: AUC of 0.80 means that for a randomly chosen (positive, negative) pair, the model gives the positive the higher score 80% of the time. This is a threshold-free, scale-free, class-imbalance-robust measure of discriminative ability.

**Q2.** When you train many models and select the best one based on test set performance, you are implicitly running a multiple comparisons procedure. How does the probability of finding a spuriously "best" model grow with the number of candidates evaluated, and what practices guard against drawing misleading conclusions?

**Discussion:** Suppose you evaluate $k$ models on the same test set, and all models are equally good (identical true generalization error). Due to random variation in test set composition, each model will show a slightly different test score. The probability that at least one model looks better than it truly is grows with $k$: if each model has a 5% chance of a "lucky" test score, the probability that _at least one_ of $k$ models looks spuriously good is approximately 1−(0.95)k1 - (0.95)^k1−(0.95)k. For k=14k = 14k=14 models, this exceeds 50%. For k=50k = 50k=50, it exceeds 92%. This is the **multiple comparisons problem** (also called the look-elsewhere effect or data dredging).

**The deeper mechanism.** The test set is a finite sample. When you pick the model with the highest test accuracy from among many candidates, you are selecting for models that got "lucky" on that particular finite sample. The selected model's test score is an optimistically biased estimate of its true generalization error — a phenomenon sometimes called "test set overfitting" or "researcher degrees of freedom." The bias grows with the number of models evaluated and shrinks with test set size.

**Practices that guard against misleading conclusions.**

_Held-out test sets._ The standard remedy is to use the test set only once, for the single final model chosen via a separate validation set or cross-validation. Comparing model configurations on the validation set consumes no test set "budget." The test set then gives an unbiased estimate of the final model's generalization error. Many real-world benchmark leaderboard competitions have been criticized for effectively allowing hundreds of implicit test set evaluations as researchers iterate toward higher scores.

_Statistical significance testing._ When comparing two specific models, a McNemar test (for classification) or a paired $t$-test (for regression) can assess whether the performance difference exceeds what chance would produce. Reporting a $p$-value or confidence interval around a performance difference is more informative than reporting point estimates alone. When comparing many models, apply a Bonferroni correction or use the Holm-Bonferroni procedure to control the family-wise error rate.

_Cross-validation with proper nesting._ All model selection and hyperparameter tuning should occur inside cross-validation folds, not on the test set. Nested cross-validation (inner loop for tuning, outer loop for evaluation) gives an approximately unbiased estimate of the generalization error of the entire model-selection procedure, not just the final selected model.

_Holdout discipline in practice._ The simplest and most reliable safeguard is social and procedural: designate the test set before any modeling begins, document every time it is accessed, and treat each access as a one-time event. In production ML systems, continuously refreshing the test set with new data (temporal holdouts) prevents long-term test set exhaustion.

### Chapter Summary

Model evaluation is the foundation of trustworthy machine learning. Key principles:

* **Always evaluate on held-out data** : training performance is uninformative about generalization
* **The test set is sacred** : use it exactly once after all model development is complete
* **Cross-validation** provides reliable estimates when data is scarce, but doesn't replace a test set
* **Choose metrics for your problem** : accuracy is often wrong; precision/recall/F1/AUC are appropriate for classification; RMSE/$R^2$ for regression
* **Data leakage** silently inflates metrics: temporal leakage, target leakage, preprocessing leakage, and group leakage are the most common forms
* **Learning curves** diagnose whether you have a bias problem (need a better model) or a variance problem (need more data or regularization)

These principles apply to every model we study in the rest of the book. When we evaluate decision trees in Chapter 9 or neural networks in Chapter 15, the same methodology governs.

In Chapter 9, we leave the world of linear models and enter the world of non-linear models, starting with decision trees — which learn by partitioning the feature space rather than fitting a hyperplane.

---

## Chapter 9: Decision Trees — Learning by Asking Questions

The linear models we've built so far — linear regression, logistic regression — draw straight lines through data. They're interpretable, efficient, and principled. But the world isn't always linear.

Consider this pattern in the house price data: small houses in premium neighborhoods sometimes cost more than large houses in average neighborhoods. Size and price don't have a simple linear relationship — the relationship depends on the interaction between size _and_ neighborhood. A linear model can struggle to capture this.

A decision tree takes a completely different approach. Instead of fitting a line, it asks questions: "Is the square footage above 2,000? If yes, is the neighborhood quality above 7? If yes, predict $420,000. If no, predict $310,000." It partitions the feature space into rectangular regions, assigning a constant prediction to each region.

This is remarkably flexible — and remarkably interpretable.

### 9.1 The Core Idea: Recursive Partitioning

#### Definition: Decision Tree

A **decision tree** is a hierarchical model that makes predictions by asking a series of yes/no questions about the input features. Each internal node tests a feature; each branch corresponds to a test outcome; each leaf node contains a prediction.

For regression, the prediction at each leaf is the mean of training examples reaching that leaf. For classification, it's the majority class (or a class probability distribution).

The tree is built by **recursive partitioning** : at each node, choose the feature and split value that best separates the data according to some criterion. Recurse on each resulting subset until a stopping criterion is met.

**Example tree for house price prediction:**
    
    
    Is area > 1800 sq ft?
    ├── No: Is age < 20 years?
    │   ├── Yes: Predict $295,000
    │   └── No: Predict $218,000
    └── Yes: Is neighborhood_quality > 7?
        ├── Yes: Predict $475,000
        └── No: Predict $345,000
    

Each leaf represents a group of similar houses, and the prediction is their average price. The tree has identified that:

* Smaller, newer houses are mid-range
* Smaller, older houses are the cheapest
* Large houses in good neighborhoods are the most expensive
* Large houses in average neighborhoods are a middle tier

This is a non-linear, non-monotone relationship that no linear model could capture as simply.

### 9.2 How to Build a Tree: Split Selection

The central question: at each node, which feature and split value should we choose?

We want splits that make the resulting subsets as "pure" as possible — either nearly all one class (classification) or with small variance around a constant (regression).

#### Definition: Gini Impurity

For classification, the **Gini impurity** of a node containing examples from $K$ classes with proportions p1,p2,…,pKp_1, p_2, \ldots, p_Kp1,p2,…,pK is:

G=∑k=1Kpk(1−pk)=1−∑k=1Kpk2G = \sum_{k=1}^K p_k(1 - p_k) = 1 - \sum_{k=1}^K p_k^2G=k=1∑Kpk(1−pk)=1−k=1∑Kpk2

In plain language, Gini impurity measures the probability that a randomly chosen example would be incorrectly classified if we randomly assigned it a label based on the node's class distribution. G=0G = 0G=0 means the node is pure (all one class); G=0.5G = 0.5G=0.5 (for binary classification) is the worst case.

#### Definition: Entropy and Information Gain

Alternatively, use **entropy** (from information theory):

H=−∑k=1Kpklog⁡2pkH = -\sum_{k=1}^K p_k \log_2 p_kH=−k=1∑Kpklog2pk

where 0log⁡200 \log_2 00log20 is defined as 0. H=0H = 0H=0 for a pure node; H=1H = 1H=1 for a perfectly mixed binary node.

**Information gain** of a split is the reduction in entropy:

IG=H(parent)−[nLnH(left)+nRnH(right)]\text{IG} = H(\text{parent}) - \left[\frac{n_L}{n} H(\text{left}) + \frac{n_R}{n} H(\text{right})\right]IG=H(parent)−[nnLH(left)+nnRH(right)]

where nL,nRn_L, n_RnL,nR are the sizes of the left and right children, and n=nL+nRn = n_L + n_Rn=nL+nR. Gini impurity and entropy typically produce similar trees in practice; the choice rarely matters.

#### Definition: Variance Reduction (for Regression)

For regression trees, use **variance reduction** as the split criterion:

ΔV=Var(parent)−[nLnVar(left)+nRnVar(right)]\Delta V = \text{Var}(\text{parent}) - \left[\frac{n_L}{n}\text{Var}(\text{left}) + \frac{n_R}{n}\text{Var}(\text{right})\right]ΔV=Var(parent)−[nnLVar(left)+nnRVar(right)]

Choose the split that maximizes variance reduction — the split that makes the labels in each child as homogeneous as possible.

**The greedy algorithm:** At each node, evaluate all possible splits (all features, all possible split values within each feature) and choose the one with the highest information gain or variance reduction. This is a greedy approach — it doesn't guarantee the globally optimal tree, but finding the globally optimal tree is NP-hard.

**Finding split values:** For a continuous feature with $n$ values, there are n−1n-1n−1 possible splits. Evaluate all of them and pick the best. For categorical features, try all possible binary partitions of categories.

#### Common Misconception

Students often think that choosing the best split at each step (greedy approach) produces the globally optimal tree. It does not. A mediocre first split might enable better subsequent splits that together outperform any tree built from an "optimal" first split. This is a fundamental limitation of greedy algorithms. However, the globally optimal tree problem is computationally intractable, so the greedy approach is the standard.

### 9.3 Overfitting and Tree Regularization

A fully grown tree (where every leaf contains a single example) achieves zero training error. But it has memorized the training data completely — one leaf per training example, just reproducing the training labels. This is extreme overfitting.

Decision trees have high variance: small changes in the training data produce very different trees (the greedy algorithm is highly sensitive to the training examples present at each node).

Several techniques prevent overfitting:

**Maximum depth:** Limit the tree to at most $d$ levels. The most important hyperparameter. Typical values: 3–10 levels for interpretable models; up to 20–30 for ensemble models (Chapter 10).

**Minimum samples per leaf:** Require each leaf to contain at least nminn_\text{min}nmin training examples. This prevents creating leaves that represent just 1-2 noisy examples.

**Minimum information gain:** Only split if the best available split achieves at least ϵ\epsilonϵ information gain. If no split is informative enough, stop.

**Pre-pruning vs. post-pruning:** Pre-pruning stops growing early (using the criteria above). Post-pruning grows the full tree then collapses back leaves that don't improve validation performance — more principled but computationally expensive.

The right hyperparameters are chosen by cross-validation (Chapter 8). Typically: tune max depth and minimum leaf size simultaneously.

### 9.4 Classification Trees

For classification, the same recursive partitioning algorithm applies, using information gain or Gini impurity as the split criterion. The leaf prediction is the majority class among training examples in that leaf, or the full class probability distribution.

**Class probability estimation:** A leaf containing 8 positive examples and 2 negative examples predicts class 1 with probability 80%. This probability estimate can be used directly as a soft classifier output.

**Multi-class trees:** Decision trees naturally handle more than 2 classes — Gini impurity and entropy both generalize directly to $K$ classes with no modification.

### 9.5 Thread A — Decision Trees for House Price Prediction (A3)

_[Thread A: A3 — Fit decision tree; see non-linear boundaries; compare against linear baseline]_

We now apply a decision tree regression model to the same house price dataset from Chapters 5 and 6. Recall: the best linear regression model (with regularization) achieved R2=0.73R^2 = 0.73R2=0.73 on the test set.

**Decision tree results** (max depth = 6, min samples per leaf = 10, tuned by 5-fold CV):

* Test $R^2$: **0.78**
* Test RMSE: **31,200 USD**

Better than linear regression (R2=0.73R^2 = 0.73R2=0.73, RMSE ≈ 34,500 USD)! The tree has captured non-linear relationships that the linear model missed.

**What has the tree learned?**

Examining the top few levels of the tree:

  1. First split: area > 1,850 sq ft
  2. Left branch (smaller homes): next split on age ≤ 15 years
  3. Right branch (larger homes): next split on neighborhood_quality > 6.5

The tree has automatically discovered that the relevant features and their interactions differ between small and large houses. This interaction between area and neighborhood quality was not captured by the linear model (which treated these as independent additive effects).

**Residual analysis:** Where does the decision tree still fail? The tree makes "step-function" predictions — within each leaf, every house gets the same predicted price. Houses near the leaf boundaries can have large errors. The tree also struggles with houses that are genuinely unusual (extreme price-to-area ratios reflecting unique architectural features). Linear regression can sometimes extrapolate better in these cases.

**Comparison at a glance:**

| Model | Test RMSE | Test $R^2$ | Interpretable? | Handles non-linearity? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Linear Regression | 34,500 | 0.73 | Yes (weights) | No |
| Decision Tree | 31,200 | 0.78 | Yes (tree) | Yes (piecewise) |
  
In Chapter 10, we will use an _ensemble_ of many decision trees (random forest, gradient boosting) to push accuracy further.

### 9.6 Feature Importance

Decision trees provide a natural measure of **feature importance** : how much does each feature contribute to the model's predictions?

#### Definition: Feature Importance (Impurity-Based)

For each feature $j$, sum the weighted impurity reduction from all splits that use feature $j$:

importance(j)=∑nodes t splitting on jpt⋅ΔIt\text{importance}(j) = \sum_{\text{nodes } t \text{ splitting on } j} p_t \cdot \Delta I_timportance(j)=nodes t splitting on j∑pt⋅ΔIt

where pt=nt/mp_t = n_t / mpt=nt/m is the fraction of examples reaching node $t$, and $\Delta I_t$ is the impurity reduction at that node.

For the house price tree, the feature importances might be:

Feature | Importance  
---|---  
Living area | 0.42  
Neighborhood quality | 0.28  
House age | 0.19  
Garage spaces | 0.11  
  
Area is the dominant predictor (42% of total split criterion reduction), followed by neighborhood quality. This is consistent with domain knowledge.

**Caveat:** Impurity-based importance can overstate the importance of features with many unique values (like continuous features vs. binary features). For more robust importance estimates, use _permutation importance_ (Chapter 22): measure how much test performance drops when a feature's values are randomly shuffled.

#### Common Misconception

Feature importance from decision trees measures _how much the tree uses the feature_ , not whether the feature has a causal relationship to the target. A feature might be important in the tree model simply because it happens to correlate with the true causal features in this dataset. Correlation-based importance should not be interpreted as causal importance without additional analysis.

### 9.7 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** Compute the Gini impurity for a node with 6 positive examples and 4 negative examples.

**Answer:**

p1=6/10=0.6p_1 = 6/10 = 0.6p1=6/10=0.6 (fraction positive), p0=4/10=0.4p_0 = 4/10 = 0.4p0=4/10=0.4 (fraction negative)

G=1−(p12+p02)=1−(0.36+0.16)=1−0.52=0.48G = 1 - (p_1^2 + p_0^2) = 1 - (0.36 + 0.16) = 1 - 0.52 = 0.48G=1−(p12+p02)=1−(0.36+0.16)=1−0.52=0.48

Compare to the worst case: for a 50/50 split, G=1−(0.25+0.25)=0.5G = 1 - (0.25 + 0.25) = 0.5G=1−(0.25+0.25)=0.5. This node is close to maximally impure.

**Q2.** A node with 10 examples (5 positive, 5 negative) is split into left (4 positive, 1 negative) and right (1 positive, 4 negative). Compute the information gain.

**Answer:**

Parent entropy: H=−0.5log⁡2(0.5)−0.5log⁡2(0.5)=1.0H = -$\log_2(0.5) - 0.5\log_2($) = 1.0H=−0.5log2(0.5)−0.5log2(0.5)=1.0 (maximally impure)

Left child (4P, 1N): p=4/5=0.8p = 4/5 = 0.8p=4/5=0.8. HL=−0.8log⁡2(0.8)−0.2log⁡2(0.2)=0.8⋅0.322+0.2⋅2.322=0.257+0.464=0.722H_L = -$\log_2($) - $\log_2(0.2) = 0.8\cdot 0.322 + 0.2\cdot 2.322 =$57 + 0.464 = 0.722HL=−0.8log2(0.8)−0.2log2(0.2)=0.8⋅0.322+0.2⋅2.322=0.257+0.464=0.722

Right child (1P, 4N): p=1/5=0.2p = 1/5 = 0.2p=1/5=0.2. By symmetry, HR=HL=0.722H_R = H_L = 0.722HR=HL=0.722.

Weighted average child entropy: (5/10)(0.722)+(5/10)(0.722)=0.722(5/10)(0.722) + (5/10)(0.722) = 0.722(5/10)(0.722)+(5/10)(0.722)=0.722

Information gain: 1.0−0.722=0.2781.0 - 0.722 = 0.2781.0−0.722=0.278

This split reduces entropy from 1.0 to 0.722 — meaningful information gain of 0.278 bits.

**Q3.** Why does a decision tree with unlimited depth always achieve 0% training error (assuming no two training examples have identical features but different labels)?

**Answer:** If no two training examples share identical feature vectors with different labels, the tree can perfectly partition the training data by growing deep enough — ultimately placing each training example in its own leaf (or one leaf per unique feature combination). With one example per leaf, the tree predicts that example's exact label with 100% accuracy. No generalization is happening; the tree has memorized the training data.

This illustrates why depth must be limited: unlimited depth = 0 training error but potentially catastrophic test performance (high variance). The maximum depth hyperparameter is the primary regularization tool for decision trees.

**Q4.** Explain why Gini impurity and entropy produce similar trees in practice.

**Answer:** Both Gini impurity G=1−∑pk2G = 1 - \sum p_k^2G=1−∑pk2 and entropy H=−∑pklog⁡pkH = -\sum p_k \log p_kH=−∑pklogpk are measures of the "impurity" or "mixing" in a distribution. They are both:

* Minimized at 0 for a pure node (all one class)
* Maximized for an equal mix of all classes
* Concave functions of the class proportions pkp_kpk

Near the extremes and near uniform distributions, they agree closely on which splits are best. The ordering of candidate splits is similar in nearly all cases. Empirically, trees built with Gini vs. entropy differ in at most a few splits and show nearly identical test performance. The choice between them is usually not worth worrying about; Gini is slightly faster to compute (avoids logarithms), so it's the default in scikit-learn.

#### Application Problems

**Q1.** You're building a fraud detection system. The dataset has 50,000 legitimate transactions and 500 fraudulent ones. You train an unlimited-depth decision tree on training data and get 100% training accuracy. On the test set, fraud recall is only 30%. Diagnose the problem and propose two specific fixes.

Hint

Think about both the class imbalance issue and the overfitting issue.

**Answer:** Two problems compound here:

**Problem 1 — Extreme overfitting.** Unlimited depth means the tree memorizes every training example. The 100% training accuracy is meaningless. Fix: limit max depth (try values 5–15 via cross-validation) and require minimum leaf size (e.g., min 50 examples per leaf).

**Problem 2 — Class imbalance.** With 99% legitimate vs. 1% fraudulent, a tree optimizing Gini impurity will naturally focus on correctly classifying the 99% majority — a pure leaf of 99% legitimates has very low impurity. The 1% fraudulent examples barely affect the split criterion. Fix: (a) **Oversample the minority class** (SMOTE or random oversampling) before training; (b) **Class weighting** : assign weight 100 to fraudulent examples and 1 to legitimate examples in the split criterion — makes misclassifying fraud 100× more costly; (c) **Undersample the majority class** — randomly remove 80% of legitimate examples for training.

Combined fix: set max depth = 8, min leaf size = 20, class weight = {fraud: 100, legitimate: 1}, and tune by 5-fold stratified CV with recall as the primary metric.

**Q2.** You have a dataset of 1,000 examples for a binary classification task. You train a decision tree with max depth = 3, achieving 74% test accuracy, and another with max depth = 15, achieving 71% test accuracy (both properly evaluated on held-out data). What does this pattern tell you about each model, and which would you choose?

**Answer:** The shallower tree (depth 3) achieves higher test accuracy than the deeper tree (depth 15). This means the deeper tree is overfitting: it's learning training data patterns that don't generalize, while the shallow tree's simpler boundary happens to be closer to the true pattern.

For a dataset of 1,000 examples, a depth-15 tree can potentially have 215=32,7682^{15} = 32,768215=32,768 leaf nodes — far more than the number of training examples, which guarantees severe overfitting.

We would choose the **depth-3 tree** (74% test accuracy). It has lower variance and better generalization despite its limited capacity. Its simplicity also makes it fully interpretable — with at most 23=82^3 = 823=8 leaves, we can examine every rule the model uses.

For production, we might try depths 3, 4, 5, 6 to see if a slightly deeper tree does even better, while watching test accuracy closely.

#### Think Deeper

**Q1.** Decision trees split on axis-aligned boundaries (i.e., tests of the form "feature $j$ > threshold"). This means they can only create rectangular regions in feature space. Describe a classification problem where axis-aligned boundaries are fundamentally inefficient, and explain what exponentially more splits would be needed compared to an optimal (non-axis-aligned) split.

**Discussion:** Consider two-dimensional data where class 1 examples lie above the diagonal line x1=x2x_1 = x_2x1=x2, and class 0 examples lie below. A single non-axis-aligned split (the line itself) achieves perfect classification.

With axis-aligned splits, the decision tree must approximate the diagonal with a "staircase" boundary. For an approximation that is correct on most examples within some region of side $s$, you need roughly $O(1/\epsilon)$ horizontal and vertical splits to approximate the diagonal with error ϵ\epsilonϵ in each dimension, leading to $O(1/\epsilon^2)$ leaf nodes vs. 2 leaf nodes for the optimal split.

More generally, decision trees are poor at learning linear boundaries — they approximate them with expensive staircases. This is why SVMs (Chapter 11) — which can learn diagonal hyperplanes directly — often outperform decision trees when the true boundary is linear or smooth. Conversely, trees excel when the true boundary is axis-aligned (e.g., "if feature A > threshold1 AND feature B > threshold2, then class 1") or when there are categorical features.

This analysis also motivates **oblique decision trees** (non-axis-aligned splits), which can learn w1x1+w2x2>cw_1 x_1 + w_2 x_2 > cw1x1+w2x2>c at each node. These are more powerful but less interpretable and harder to train.

**Q2.** A decision tree grown to unlimited depth is a consistent estimator of the Bayes optimal classifier — it converges to the true decision boundary as training data grows without bound. Yet unlimited depth causes severe overfitting on finite data. How can both statements be true simultaneously, and how do random forests escape this tension?

**Discussion:** A **consistent estimator** converges in probability to the true quantity being estimated as the sample size m→∞m \to \inftym→∞. For classification, the Bayes optimal classifier f∗(x)=arg⁡max⁡kP(y=k∣x)f^*(\mathbf{x}) = \arg\max_k P(y = k \mid \mathbf{x})f∗(x)=argmaxkP(y=k∣x) achieves the lowest possible error rate (the Bayes error). An estimator is consistent if its error approaches the Bayes error as m→∞m \to \inftym→∞.

**Why unlimited trees are consistent.** As m→∞m \to \inftym→∞, a tree that keeps splitting can partition the feature space into arbitrarily small cells. In each cell, there are eventually enough training points that the majority-vote label converges to the true $P(y \mid \mathbf{x})$ for $\mathbf{x}$ in that region. With the right growth schedule — where cell diameters shrink to zero but cell populations grow to infinity — the tree's predictions converge to the Bayes optimal everywhere. This is the essence of consistency proofs for kernel methods and histogram classifiers too.

**Why unlimited trees overfit on finite data.** With finite $m$, a fully grown tree creates leaf nodes containing as few as one training example. A single example is an extremely noisy estimate of $P(y \mid \mathbf{x})$: the leaf's prediction is entirely determined by one data point, so any noise in that label propagates directly to predictions. Test error is high even if training error is zero. The bias-variance tradeoff is maximally unfavorable: variance is enormous (different training sets produce completely different trees) even though bias is low.

**The apparent paradox resolved.** There is no contradiction: consistency is an asymptotic guarantee (m→∞m \to \inftym→∞), while overfitting is a finite-$m$ phenomenon. As $m$ grows, the number of examples per leaf also grows, and the variance decreases. The two statements coexist because they apply at different sample sizes. The deeper issue is that the rate of convergence can be very slow — on a finite dataset, a fully grown tree may need orders of magnitude more data than a shallower tree to achieve comparable test error.

**How random forests escape the tension.** Random forests grow many fully deep (or near-fully deep) trees, each on a bootstrap sample and with random feature subsets, then average their predictions. The key insight from the bias-variance decomposition is: the bias of the average of many estimators equals the bias of one estimator, but the variance of the average of $B$ uncorrelated estimators is 1/B1/B1/B times the variance of one. By averaging many high-variance, low-bias trees, random forests retain the consistency of deep trees while dramatically reducing variance. The feature randomness (choosing a random subset of features at each split) decorrelates the trees further — if a few dominant features exist, every tree in a non-random forest would use them near the root, making the trees highly correlated and reducing the variance-reduction benefit of averaging. Random forests thus achieve near-Bayes-optimal performance in practice without requiring explicit depth limits.

### Chapter Summary

Decision trees are fundamentally different from linear models: they partition the feature space into rectangles and make piecewise constant predictions, capturing non-linear interactions with full interpretability.

Key ideas:

* **Recursive partitioning** : at each node, choose the split (feature + threshold) that maximizes information gain or Gini impurity reduction
* **Overfitting is severe without regularization** : limit max depth and minimum leaf size; tune by cross-validation
* **Feature importance** : trees naturally quantify how much each feature contributes to predictions
* **Interpretability** : shallow trees (depth 3–5) produce human-readable if-then rules

**Thread A checkpoint (A3):** The decision tree achieves R2=0.78R^2 = 0.78R2=0.78 vs. 0.73 for regularized linear regression on the house price dataset. The tree discovers non-linear interactions (e.g., large houses in good neighborhoods are disproportionately expensive) that the linear model misses. However, individual trees still have high variance — the same pattern with slightly different training data might produce a very different tree. Chapter 10 addresses this with ensemble methods.

---

## Chapter 10: Ensemble Methods — Wisdom of the Crowd

In Chapter 9, we noted that a single decision tree has high variance: it's highly sensitive to the specific training data, and small changes produce very different trees. The individual tree is like a single person's opinion — potentially insightful, but noisy and unreliable.

What if you could ask a thousand different trees and average their opinions? The noise would largely cancel out, leaving a more stable, reliable prediction. This is the central idea of **ensemble learning** : combine many weak learners to produce a strong learner.

Ensemble methods are among the most practically powerful algorithms in ML. In many tabular data competitions (including Kaggle), gradient boosted trees are consistently the top performers. Understanding why they work — and how to use them well — is a crucial skill.

### 10.1 Why Ensembles Reduce Variance: Bagging

Consider $T$ decision trees, each trained on independent random samples of the training data. Each tree ht(x)h_t(\mathbf{x})ht(x) has some variance σ2\sigma^2σ2 around the true prediction. If the trees are uncorrelated (pairwise correlation $\rho = 0$), the variance of their average is:

Var ⁣(1T∑tht(x))=σ2T\text{Var}\\!\left(\frac{1}{T}\sum_t h_t(\mathbf{x})\right) = \frac{\sigma^2}{T}Var(T1t∑ht(x))=Tσ2

With T=100T = 100T=100 trees, variance drops by a factor of 100. This is the power of averaging: noise cancels, signal remains.

In practice, the trees are correlated (they all see similar data, so they make similar predictions). For correlated trees with pairwise correlation ρ\rhoρ:

Var ⁣(1T∑tht(x))=ρσ2+1−ρTσ2\text{Var}\\!\left(\frac{1}{T}\sum_t h_t(\mathbf{x})\right) = \rho\sigma^2 + \frac{1-\rho}{T}\sigma^2Var(T1t∑ht(x))=ρσ2+T1−ρσ2

As T→∞T \to \inftyT→∞, this approaches $\rho\sigma^2$ — the irreducible variance from correlation. The key insight: **reducing correlation between trees** is as important as having many trees. This motivates the randomization in random forests.

#### Definition: Bagging (Bootstrap Aggregating)

**Bagging** trains $T$ models, each on a different **bootstrap sample** of the training data. A bootstrap sample is obtained by sampling $m$ examples from the training set _with replacement_ — so some examples appear multiple times, and others not at all (each bootstrap sample contains approximately 63%63\%63% of unique training examples; the rest are duplicates).

The final prediction is the average (for regression) or majority vote (for classification) across all $T$ models:

y^(x)=1T∑t=1Tht(x)(regression)\hat{y}(\mathbf{x}) = \frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^T h_t(\mathbf{x}) \quad \text{(regression)}y^(x)=T1t=1∑Tht(x)(regression)

In plain language: train each model on a slightly different version of the data, then average. The variation in training data forces the models to be different from each other, reducing correlation.

#### Out-of-Bag (OOB) Error

Each tree is trained on about 63% of the data. The remaining 37% — the **out-of-bag (OOB) examples** — were not used to train this particular tree. We can predict these OOB examples using the tree and compute an OOB error estimate.

The OOB error is a free estimate of generalization error — no separate validation set needed. It's remarkably accurate: comparable to 5-fold cross-validation, but computed at no extra cost.

### 10.2 Random Forests: Adding Feature Randomness

Bagging reduces variance, but the trees are still correlated because they all see the same features and will tend to split on the same dominant features at the top of each tree.

#### Definition: Random Forest

A **random forest** is a bagged ensemble of decision trees where, at _each split_ , only a random subset of n\sqrt{n}n features (for classification) or n/3n/3n/3 features (for regression) are considered.

In plain language, at each node in each tree, we randomly select a subset of features to evaluate for the best split. This forces each tree to use different features in different ways, dramatically reducing inter-tree correlation.

The two sources of randomness:

  1. **Bootstrap sampling** (rows): each tree sees a different sample of training examples
  2. **Feature subsampling** (columns): each split considers a random subset of features

Together, these make the $T$ trees much less correlated than bagged trees, producing a more powerful ensemble.

**Why does feature randomness help?** In bagging, the first split in every tree is often the same dominant feature (e.g., "area > 1,850 sq ft" for house prices). All trees look similar at the top. By restricting each node to a random n\sqrt{n}n features, different trees are forced to build completely different structures, even when they see similar training data.

#### Key Hyperparameters

* **Number of trees ( $T$):** More trees = more stable predictions. Diminishing returns after ~200–500 trees; variance stabilizes. Common default: 100–500.
* **Max depth or min leaf size:** Same as single decision trees; controls individual tree complexity. In random forests, trees are often grown deep (high individual variance) because the averaging will reduce overall variance.
* **Number of features at each split:** n\sqrt{n}n for classification, n/3n/3n/3 for regression are good defaults but tunable.

#### Common Misconception

Many practitioners assume that more trees always improve a random forest. In fact, adding more trees only reduces variance — it cannot reduce bias. If the ensemble is underfitting (too simple trees), adding more trees won't help. You need deeper trees or more features. Additionally, there are computational diminishing returns: going from 100 to 1,000 trees gives modest accuracy improvement but 10× training cost. Often 200 trees is the sweet spot.

### 10.3 Gradient Boosting: Sequential Error Correction

Random forests combine trees in _parallel_ (average independent models). **Gradient boosting** combines trees _sequentially_ : each tree is trained to correct the errors of the previous ensemble.

#### The Gradient Boosting Idea

Start with a simple initial prediction F0(x)=yˉF_0(\mathbf{x}) = \bar{y}F0(x)=yˉ (the mean). At each stage $t$:

  1. Compute the residuals ri(t)=yi−Ft−1(xi)r_i^{(t)} = y_i - F_{t-1}(\mathbf{x}_i)ri(t)=yi−Ft−1(xi) (the errors of the current ensemble)
  2. Train a new tree hth_tht on the residuals (to predict the errors)
  3. Update: Ft(x)=Ft−1(x)+η⋅ht(x)F$\mathbf{x}) = F_{t-1}(\mathbf{x}) + \eta \cdot h$\mathbf{x})Ft(x)=Ft−1(x)+η⋅ht(x)

In plain language: the first tree predicts the targets; the second tree predicts the _errors_ of the first; the third predicts the errors of the first two; and so on. Each tree focuses on the cases where the previous ensemble was most wrong.

#### Definition: Gradient Boosting

More generally, gradient boosting minimizes a loss function L(y,F(x))L(y, F(\mathbf{x}))L(y,F(x)) by gradient descent in _function space_ :

At each stage, fit a tree to the **negative gradient** of the loss:

ri(t)=−∂L(yi,F(xi))∂F(xi)∣F=Ft−1r_i^{(t)} = -\left.\frac{\partial L(y_i, F(\mathbf{x}_i))}{\partial F(\mathbf{x}_i)}\right|_{F = F_{t-1}}ri(t)=−∂F(xi)∂L(yi,F(xi))F=Ft−1

For MSE loss, the negative gradient is exactly the residual: ri=yi−F(xi)r_i = y_i - F(\mathbf{x}_i)ri=yi−F(xi). For other loss functions (cross-entropy, Huber), the negative gradient is a different quantity.

This framing shows gradient boosting is _gradient descent in function space_ — instead of updating parameter values, we're "descending" by adding new functions (trees) that point in the steepest descent direction.

**The learning rate η\etaη:** Called the _shrinkage_ parameter, it controls how much each tree contributes. Small η\etaη (0.01–0.1) with many trees usually outperforms large η\etaη with few trees — more gradual corrections lead to better generalization. Use the smallest η\etaη your compute budget allows.

#### XGBoost and LightGBM

Modern gradient boosting implementations go far beyond the basic algorithm:

**XGBoost (eXtreme Gradient Boosting):** Adds second-order Taylor expansion of the loss (uses both gradient and Hessian for more accurate tree fitting), L1/L2 regularization on the tree structure, and efficient parallel tree building with sorted feature histograms.

**LightGBM (Light Gradient Boosting Machine):** Further optimizes by growing trees _leaf-wise_ (choosing the single leaf to split that reduces loss the most) rather than level-wise, and using Gradient-based One-Side Sampling (GOSS) to skip uninformative examples. Much faster than XGBoost for large datasets.

**CatBoost:** Specializes in handling categorical features without requiring one-hot encoding.

All three achieve state-of-the-art performance on tabular data. For new problems, trying XGBoost or LightGBM is a sensible starting point.

#### Key Hyperparameters for Gradient Boosting

Hyperparameter | Effect | Typical range  
---|---|---  
Number of trees ($T$) | More trees can reduce loss; can overfit | 100–5000  
Learning rate (η\etaη) | Lower = slower but better generalization | 0.01–0.3  
Max tree depth | Controls individual tree complexity | 3–8  
Min samples per leaf | Regularizes individual trees | 5–50
