Subsample | Fraction of data per tree (like bagging) | 0.5–1.0  
Colsample | Fraction of features per tree | 0.5–1.0  
  
Early stopping: monitor validation error during boosting. Stop when it starts increasing — this automatically finds the right $T$.

### 10.4 Thread A — Ensembles for House Price Prediction (A4)

_[Thread A: A4 — Random forest and gradient boosting on house price dataset; compare against all previous methods]_

We now apply random forest and gradient boosting to the house price prediction dataset. Recall:

* Linear regression (regularized): R2=0.73R^2 = 0.73R2=0.73, RMSE = 34,500 USD
* Decision tree (depth 6): R2=0.78R^2 = 0.78R2=0.78, RMSE = 31,200 USD

**Random forest** (500 trees, max depth = 12, n\sqrt{n}n features per split):

* Test $R^2$: **0.83**
* Test RMSE: **27,100 USD**

**Gradient boosting (XGBoost)** (T=800T = 800T=800 trees, $\eta = 0.05$, max depth = 5):

* Test $R^2$: **0.87**
* Test RMSE: **23,800 USD**

**Complete comparison:**

| Model | Test RMSE | Test $R^2$ | Interpretable? | Handles non-linearity? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Linear Regression | 34,500 USD | 0.73 | High |
| Decision Tree (depth 6) | 31,200 USD | 0.78 | Medium |
| Random Forest | 27,100 USD | 0.83 | Low |
| Gradient Boosting | 23,800 USD | 0.87 | Very Low |
  
XGBoost achieves the best accuracy — 10,700 USD better RMSE than linear regression. But this comes at a cost: interpretability decreases dramatically. A random forest of 500 trees cannot be read directly; a gradient boosted ensemble of 800 trees even less so.

**When does simplicity win?** For houses very similar to those in the training set, ensemble methods win convincingly. But: (1) for houses outside the training range (very large mansions, unusual configurations), the ensemble may extrapolate poorly while a linear model extrapolates more gracefully; (2) for explaining a specific prediction to a client ("why is this house priced at 420,000 USD?"), the decision tree's explicit rules are much more useful.

The lesson: **there is no universally best model**. The right model depends on the performance-interpretability-robustness tradeoffs for the specific problem.

In Chapter 15, we'll apply a neural network to this same data and examine whether deep learning can do better.

### 10.5 Ensemble Methods for Classification

Everything above extends directly to classification:

**Random forest for classification:** Majority vote across all trees (or average of class probabilities). Feature importance is computed the same way as for regression trees.

**Gradient boosting for classification:** Uses cross-entropy loss (binary or multi-class). The negative gradient is (yi−p^i)(y_i - \hat{p}_i)(yi−p^i) for binary classification.

**Stacking (Stacked Generalization):** A meta-learning ensemble technique: train multiple base models (e.g., random forest, XGBoost, logistic regression), then train a _meta-model_ on their predictions. The meta-model learns which base model to trust for which kinds of inputs. Often the best-performing approach in competitions, at the cost of complexity.

### 10.6 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** A random forest of 10 trees makes the following predictions for a regression problem: [24,27,22,31,26,25,29,23,28,25][24, 27, 22, 31, 26, 25, 29, 23, 28, 25][24,27,22,31,26,25,29,23,28,25]. What is the final prediction?

**Answer:** The final prediction is the average of all 10 tree predictions:

y^=24+27+22+31+26+25+29+23+28+2510=2601$\hat{y} = \frac{24 + 27 + 22 + 31 + 26 + 25 + 29 + 23 + 28 + 25}{10} = \frac{260}{10} = 26.0y^=1024+27+22+31+26+25+29+23+28+25=1026$

Note: some trees are much higher (31) or lower (22) than the average. The ensemble smooths these out. A single tree predicting 31 would be very different from a single tree predicting 22 — the ensemble's 26.0 is more reliable than either extreme.

**Q2.** Explain in plain language why a random forest typically performs better than a single decision tree on new data, using the concept of bias and variance.

**Answer:** A single deep decision tree has low bias (it can fit complex patterns) but very high variance (slight changes in training data produce very different trees). It's like asking one noisy expert — they might be right, but they might also be wildly wrong.

A random forest keeps the low bias (each tree is still deep and complex) but dramatically reduces variance by averaging many trees. The averaging works because the trees make somewhat independent errors — when one tree is wrong in one direction, another is likely wrong in a different direction, so the errors partially cancel. Crucially, signal (the true pattern) is consistent across trees and doesn't cancel.

The result: the ensemble has similar bias to a single tree but much lower variance — better test performance without sacrificing model capacity.

**Q3.** In gradient boosting, the learning rate η\etaη is a hyperparameter. If $\eta = 1.0$, each tree directly predicts the current residual with full weight. What is the risk, and how does $\eta < 1$ address it?

**Answer:** With $\eta = 1.0$, each tree attempts to perfectly fit the current residuals. But tree fitting is imperfect (especially with limited depth), and each tree itself has variance. If the tree overfits the residuals (memorizing noise), subsequent trees will compound these errors — correcting for noise rather than signal. The boosting procedure can become unstable.

With $\eta < 1$ (say $\eta = 0.05$), each tree contributes only 5% of its correction. This forces the ensemble to make small, conservative corrections at each step, needing more trees but generalizing better. The model "approaches the optimum slowly but steadily" rather than overcommitting to any single tree's noisy correction. Small η\etaη combined with early stopping (stop when validation error increases) is the standard recipe for well-calibrated gradient boosting.

**Q4.** What is an out-of-bag (OOB) error estimate, and why is it a useful property of random forests?

**Answer:** Each tree in a random forest is trained on a bootstrap sample containing approximately 63% of the training examples. The remaining 37% — the out-of-bag examples — were not used to train that particular tree. For each training example, we can find all trees that did _not_ train on it, use those trees to predict its label, and measure the error. The average OOB error across all training examples is the OOB error estimate.

This is useful because: (1) it provides an estimate of generalization error without requiring a separate validation set — valuable when data is scarce; (2) it's computed "for free" during training (no extra computation beyond what's already done); (3) it's comparable in accuracy to 5-fold cross-validation for typical datasets. The OOB error can also be used to compute per-feature importance and to stop adding trees (when OOB error stops improving).

#### Application Problems

**Q1.** You're using gradient boosting for a classification task. You train for 1,000 iterations with learning rate 0.1. Training loss decreases steadily to nearly 0, but validation loss starts increasing after 300 iterations. What is happening, and how would you fix it?

Hint

This is the classic signature of a specific type of failure in iterative methods. What does the validation loss curve tell you about model complexity?

**Answer:** After 300 iterations, the model begins **overfitting** — it's fitting noise in the training data that doesn't generalize. The training loss keeps dropping (the model gets better at memorizing training data) while validation loss rises (generalization worsens). The optimal model is at the minimum of the validation loss curve — iteration 300.

Fixes:

  1. **Early stopping** : the cleanest fix. Monitor validation loss at each iteration; stop when it has not improved for $K$ consecutive rounds (typically K=50K = 50K=50). Use the model at the best validation checkpoint.
  2. **Reduce learning rate + increase trees** : switch to $\eta = 0.01$ and train for 10,000 iterations. More gradual corrections tend to overfit less; early stopping still applies.
  3. **Reduce tree complexity** : lower max depth (from e.g. 6 to 4) or increase min leaf size. Simpler individual trees have lower variance.
  4. **Subsample** : add subsampling (e.g., use 80% of data per tree, 80% of features per split) to add randomness similar to random forests.

**Q2.** Compare random forests and gradient boosting on the following dimensions: training time, sensitivity to hyperparameters, handling of outliers, and performance on noisy data. Which would you choose for a quick baseline on a new tabular dataset?

**Answer:**

Dimension | Random Forest | Gradient Boosting  
---|---|---  
Training time | Easily parallelizable; fast | Sequential; slower per tree, but efficient with histograms  
Hyperparameter sensitivity | Relatively insensitive; default n\sqrt{n}n features, 100–500 trees work well | More sensitive; learning rate, depth, and subsample all interact  
Handling of outliers | More robust (averaging dilutes impact of any single misprediction) | More sensitive (each tree focuses on residuals, which can be dominated by outliers)  
Noisy data | Better (lower variance, doesn't overfit as aggressively) | Worse (can fit noise if η\etaη too large or $T$ too many)  
  
**For a quick baseline:** Random forest. It requires less hyperparameter tuning (default parameters work well), is robust to outliers, and trains fast in parallel. Just set `n_estimators=200`, `max_depth=None`, and run — you'll get a strong baseline in minutes. Then tune gradient boosting if you need the extra accuracy.

#### Think Deeper

**Q1.** Gradient boosting and gradient descent both use "gradients" to make progress. Can you describe the precise analogy? What plays the role of "parameters" in gradient boosting, and what plays the role of the "gradient update"?

**Discussion:** The analogy is mathematically precise. In standard gradient descent, we minimize $L(\boldsymbol{\theta})$ over a finite-dimensional parameter space by updating $\boldsymbol{\theta} \to \boldsymbol{\theta} - \eta \nabla L$.

In gradient boosting, we minimize $\sum_i L(y_i, F(\mathbf{x}_i))$ over the _space of functions_ $F$ (not a finite-dimensional parameter space). The "parameter" is the entire function $F$.

The "gradient" of the loss in function space at each training point xi\mathbf{x}_ixi is $\partial L(y_i, F(\mathbf{x}_i)) / \partial F(\mathbf{x}_i)$ — the sensitivity of the loss to changes in the prediction at xi\mathbf{x}_ixi. The vector of these values across all training points is the functional gradient.

The "gradient descent step" is: fit a tree hth_tht to these negative gradients (approximate the gradient descent direction in function space using a tree), then add $\eta \cdot h_t$ to $F$. This is exactly steepest descent in function space, restricted to the space of shallow decision trees.

This perspective explains why gradient boosting can minimize arbitrary differentiable loss functions — you just need the gradient of the loss at each training point, then fit a tree to those gradients. This is exactly how XGBoost uses the second-order (Hessian) information too: analogous to Newton's method in function space.

**Q2.** The connection between gradient boosting and gradient descent is often described informally. Make it precise: in what sense does fitting each new tree to the negative gradient correspond to a steepest descent step in the space of functions? Use the squared loss case to make the analogy fully concrete.

**Discussion:** Standard gradient descent operates in a finite-dimensional parameter space Rd\mathbb{R}^dRd. At each step we move in the direction −∇θL-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}} L−∇θL because this is the direction of steepest decrease of $L$ in Rd\mathbb{R}^dRd.

**The function-space view.** In gradient boosting, the "parameter" being optimized is not a weight vector but a function F:X→RF: \mathcal{X} \to \mathbb{R}F:X→R. The loss is $\mathcal{L}[F] = \sum_{i=1}^m L(y_i, F(\mathbf{x}_i))$. We can think of $F$ as an infinite-dimensional vector indexed by all possible inputs $\mathbf{x}$, but on a finite training set it is effectively the vector of predictions $\mathbf{F} = (F(\mathbf{x}_1), \ldots, F(\mathbf{x}_m)) \in \mathbb{R}^m$.

The functional gradient at training point xi\mathbf{x}_ixi is:

gi=∂L∂F(xi)=∂L(yi,F(xi))∂F(xi)g_i = \frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial F(\mathbf{x}_i)} = \frac{\partial L(y_i, F(\mathbf{x}_i))}{\partial F(\mathbf{x}_i)}gi=∂F(xi)∂L=∂F(xi)∂L(yi,F(xi))

The vector $\mathbf{g} = (g_1, \ldots, g_m)$ is the gradient of the loss with respect to the current predictions. The steepest descent step in function space is to update $\mathbf{F} \leftarrow \mathbf{F} - \eta \mathbf{g}$.

**The role of the tree.** We cannot take an unconstrained step −ηg-\eta \mathbf{g}−ηg in function space because this would just memorize the negative gradient values at training points and generalize nowhere. Instead, we fit a shallow tree hth_tht to minimize $\sum_i (h_t(\mathbf{x}_i) - (-g_i))^2$ — finding the best approximation to the negative gradient _within the restricted space of decision trees_. Adding $\eta \cdot h_t$ to $F$ is thus a projected gradient descent step: steepest descent in function space, projected onto the subspace of functions representable by a single tree.

**The squared loss case: residuals as negative gradients.** For L(yi,F(xi))=12(yi−F(xi))2L(y_i, F(\mathbf{x}_i)) = \frac{1}{2}(y_i - F(\mathbf{x}_i))^2L(yi,F(xi))=21(yi−F(xi))2, the functional gradient is:

gi=∂∂F(xi)12(yi−F(xi))2=−(yi−F(xi))=F(xi)−yig_i = \frac{\partial}{\partial F(\mathbf{x}_i)} \frac{1}{2}(y_i - F(\mathbf{x}_i))^2 = -(y_i - F(\mathbf{x}_i)) = F(\mathbf{x}_i) - y_igi=∂F(xi)∂21(yi−F(xi))2=−(yi−F(xi))=F(xi)−yi

So the negative gradient is −gi=yi−F(xi)-g_i = y_i - F(\mathbf{x}_i)−gi=yi−F(xi), which is exactly the **residual** of the current ensemble. Fitting a new tree to the residuals (as in the classical boosting description for squared loss) is precisely a steepest descent step in function space. The general gradient boosting algorithm replaces this with the negative gradient of an arbitrary differentiable loss — making gradient boosting a direct generalization of residual fitting to non-MSE objectives such as log-loss, Huber loss, or quantile loss.

### Chapter Summary

Ensemble methods combine many weak learners to produce a strong learner, dramatically reducing variance while maintaining or improving bias.

Key ideas:

* **Bagging** trains models on bootstrap samples and averages; reduces variance proportionally to the number of uncorrelated models
* **Random forests** add feature subsampling to further decorrelate trees; robust, fast, requires minimal tuning
* **Gradient boosting** trains trees sequentially, each correcting the previous ensemble's errors; state-of-the-art on tabular data but requires careful regularization
* **OOB error** provides a free generalization estimate for random forests
* **Early stopping** is essential for gradient boosting to avoid overfitting

**Thread A checkpoint (A4):** Gradient boosting (XGBoost) achieves R2=0.87R^2 = 0.87R2=0.87, RMSE = 23,800 USD on the house price dataset — the best result yet, beating linear regression by 10,700 USD RMSE. Random forest achieves a strong intermediate result (R2=0.83R^2 = 0.83R2=0.83). The clear lesson: ensemble methods substantially outperform single models on this dataset. But interpretability decreases: the XGBoost model is an ensemble of 800 trees and cannot be directly inspected.

In Chapter 11, we explore Support Vector Machines — a fundamentally different approach based on finding the maximum-margin boundary.

---

## Chapter 11: Support Vector Machines — Maximum Margin Classifiers

Consider a binary classification problem where two classes are perfectly separable by a straight line. There are infinitely many lines that separate them — so which one should we choose?

Logistic regression might choose a line that barely separates the classes, passing close to some training examples. A small perturbation in the data could move those examples to the wrong side. Intuitively, we want a line that is as _far as possible_ from both classes — a boundary that has the most "room" before misclassifying anything.

This is the central idea of Support Vector Machines: find the maximum-margin classifier. The resulting algorithm turns out to be elegant, theoretically well-founded, and — through a remarkable trick called the kernel — extendable to non-linear boundaries in infinite-dimensional feature spaces.

### 11.1 The Maximum Margin Classifier

Imagine class +1 points and class -1 points separated by a hyperplane $\mathbf{w}^\top \mathbf{x} + b = 0$. The **margin** is the width of the "street" between the two classes — twice the distance from the separating hyperplane to the nearest point.

#### Definition: Hard-Margin SVM

For linearly separable data with labels yi∈{−1,+1}y_i \in \\{-1, +1\\}yi∈{−1,+1}, the **hard-margin SVM** finds the maximum-margin hyperplane:

min⁡w,b12∥w∥2subject toyi(w⊤xi+b)≥1∀i\min_{\mathbf{w}, b} \frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{w}\|^2 \quad \text{subject to} \quad y_i(\mathbf{w}^\top \mathbf{x}_i + b) \geq 1 \quad \forall iw,bmin21∥w∥2subject toyi(w⊤xi+b)≥1∀i

In plain language: find the smallest-norm weight vector $\mathbf{w}$ such that all examples are correctly classified with a margin of at least 1 (scaled by $\|\mathbf{w}\|$). The margin is $2/\|\mathbf{w}\|$, so minimizing $\|\mathbf{w}\|^2$ maximizes the margin.

The examples that lie exactly on the margin boundary (yi(w⊤xi+b)=1y_i(\mathbf{w}^\top \mathbf{x}_i + b) = 1yi(w⊤xi+b)=1) are called **support vectors** — they are the only training examples that determine the decision boundary. Move a non-support-vector, and the boundary doesn't change. This is a dramatic contrast to logistic regression, where all training examples affect the decision boundary.

**Geometric interpretation:** The margin hyperplanes $\mathbf{w}^\top \mathbf{x} + b = +1$ and $\mathbf{w}^\top \mathbf{x} + b = -1$ define a "channel" of width $2/\|\mathbf{w}\|$. The SVM finds the widest possible channel that separates the classes.

### 11.2 Soft-Margin SVM: Handling Non-Separable Data

Real data is almost never perfectly linearly separable. Some examples may be outliers or mislabeled. The hard-margin SVM would fail to find any solution.

#### Definition: Soft-Margin SVM

The **soft-margin SVM** allows some examples to violate the margin (or even be misclassified) by introducing **slack variables** $\xi_i \geq 0$:

min⁡w,b,ξ12∥w∥2+C∑i=1mξi\min_{\mathbf{w}, b, \boldsymbol{\xi}} \frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{w}\|^2 + C\sum_{i=1}^m \xi_iw,b,ξmin21∥w∥2+Ci=1∑mξi subject $\text{subject to} \quad y_i(\mathbf{w}^\top \mathbf{x}_i + b) \geq 1 - \xi_i, \quad \xi_i \geq 0 \quad \forall isubject$

The parameter C>0C > 0C>0 controls the tradeoff:

* **Large $C$**: Heavily penalize violations → small margin, few violations (closer to hard-margin)
* **Small $C$**: Allow more violations in exchange for a wider margin (more regularized)

The connection to regularization from Chapter 6: small $C$ corresponds to large λ\lambdaλ in Ridge regression — more tolerance for training errors in exchange for smaller weights and better generalization.

The soft-margin SVM can be rewritten (its "hinge loss" formulation):

min⁡w12∥w∥2+C∑i=1mmax⁡(0,1−yi(w⊤xi+b))\min_\mathbf{w} \frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{w}\|^2 + C\sum_{i=1}^m \max(0, 1 - y_i(\mathbf{w}^\top \mathbf{x}_i + b))wmin21∥w∥2+Ci=1∑mmax(0,1−yi(w⊤xi+b))

This is a regularized loss with the **hinge loss** ℓ(z)=max⁡(0,1−z)\ell(z) = \max(0, 1-z)ℓ(z)=max(0,1−z), which is zero when the example is correctly classified with margin ≥ 1.

#### Common Misconception

Students often think SVMs always outperform logistic regression because SVMs maximize the margin. In practice, on most real datasets, the difference is small after proper regularization. The SVM's real advantage is the kernel trick (next section), which gives access to non-linear boundaries efficiently.

### 11.3 The Kernel Trick: Infinite Feature Spaces

The SVM objective can be rewritten using only **dot products** between data points. This is the key observation that enables the kernel trick.

The dual formulation of the SVM (via Lagrange multipliers) involves:

max⁡α∑iαi−12∑i,jαiαjyiyjxi⊤xj\max_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}} \sum_i \alpha_i - \frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j} \alpha_i \alpha_j y_i y_j \mathbf{x}_i^\top \mathbf{x}_jαmaxi∑αi−21i,j∑αiαjyiyjxi⊤xj

The data appears only through dot products $\mathbf{x}_i^\top \mathbf{x}_j$.

#### Definition: Kernel Function

A **kernel** k(x,x′)k(\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{x}')k(x,x′) implicitly computes the dot product in a (possibly infinite-dimensional) feature space $\phi(\mathbf{x})$:

k(x,x′)=⟨ϕ(x),ϕ(x′)⟩k(\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{x}') = \langle \phi(\mathbf{x}), \phi(\mathbf{x}') \ranglek(x,x′)=⟨ϕ(x),ϕ(x′)⟩

By replacing every $\mathbf{x}_i^\top \mathbf{x}_j$ in the SVM dual with k(xi,xj)k(\mathbf{x}_i, \mathbf{x}_j)k(xi,xj), we obtain an SVM in a much richer feature space — without ever computing $\phi(\mathbf{x})$ explicitly.

**Common kernels:**

**Linear kernel:** k(x,x′)=x⊤x′k(\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{x}') = \mathbf{x}^\top \mathbf{x}'k(x,x′)=x⊤x′ — standard dot product; equivalent to linear SVM.

**Polynomial kernel:** k(x,x′)=(x⊤x′+c)dk(\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{x}') = (\mathbf{x}^\top \mathbf{x}' + c)^dk(x,x′)=(x⊤x′+c)d — corresponds to all polynomial features up to degree $d$. With d=2d=2d=2 and 2D inputs, the feature space includes: [1,x1,x2,x12,x22,x1x2][1, x_1, x_2, x_1^2, x_2^2, x_1 x_2][1,x1,x2,x12,x22,x1x2] — all without computing 6 features explicitly.

**RBF (Radial Basis Function) kernel:** k(x,x′)=exp⁡(−γ∥x−x′∥$\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{x}') = \exp(-\gamma\|\mathbf{x} - \mathbf{x}'\|^$x,x′)=exp(−γ∥x−x′∥2) — corresponds to an infinite-dimensional feature space. It measures similarity based on Euclidean distance: examples nearby in input space get high kernel value, distant examples near zero. The parameter γ\gammaγ controls how quickly similarity drops with distance.

#### Why the Kernel Trick Is Brilliant

Consider the RBF kernel. Its corresponding feature space $\phi(\mathbf{x})$ is infinite-dimensional — explicitly computing it would require infinite computation. But k(x,x′)k(\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{x}')k(x,x′) is just e−γ∥x−x′∥2e^{-\gamma\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}'\|^2}e−γ∥x−x′∥2 — a simple computation. The SVM dual only needs kernel values, never the actual features. We get the power of infinite features with the cost of finite computation.

This is one of the most elegant ideas in all of machine learning: working implicitly in an infinite-dimensional space by computing only inner products.

**The kernel matrix (Gram matrix):** All pairwise kernel values Kij=k(xi,xj)K_{ij} = k(\mathbf{x}_i, \mathbf{x}_j)Kij=k(xi,xj) form the **kernel matrix** K∈Rm×mK \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times m}K∈Rm×m. This matrix must be positive semi-definite for the optimization to be well-posed — which is guaranteed for any valid kernel function (Mercer's theorem).

### 11.4 Making Predictions

After training, predictions use:

y^(x)=sign ⁣(∑i=1mαiyik(xi,x)+b)\hat{y}(\mathbf{x}) = \text{sign}\\!\left(\sum_{i=1}^m \alpha_i y_i k(\mathbf{x}_i, \mathbf{x}) + b\right)y^(x)=sign(i=1∑mαiyik(xi,x)+b)

Only support vectors have $\alpha_i > 0$ — others have $\alpha_i = 0$ and don't contribute. This sparsity makes SVM prediction fast: only support vectors matter.

### 11.5 SVM for Regression: Support Vector Regression

SVMs can also be used for regression. **Support Vector Regression (SVR)** fits a function that has at most ϵ\epsilonϵ deviation from the true values on training data — errors smaller than ϵ\epsilonϵ are ignored, larger ones are penalized:

min⁡w,b12∥w∥2+C∑imax⁡(0,∣yi−y^i∣−ϵ)\min_{\mathbf{w}, b} \frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{w}\|^2 + C\sum_i \max(0, |y_i - \hat{y}_i| - \epsilon)w,bmin21∥w∥2+Ci∑max(0,∣yi−y^i∣−ϵ)

This creates a "tube" of width 2ϵ2\epsilon2ϵ around the prediction; only points outside the tube are support vectors.

### 11.6 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** In a hard-margin SVM with 1,000 training examples, the solution has 15 support vectors. If you remove one non-support-vector from the training set, how does the decision boundary change?

**Answer:** The decision boundary does **not change at all**. By definition, the SVM solution depends only on the support vectors. Non-support-vectors ($\alpha_i = 0$) don't contribute to the dual objective or to predictions. Removing a non-support-vector is completely irrelevant to the trained model.

This is a useful property in practice: once trained, you can discard all non-support-vectors and still make identical predictions. The model "compresses" the training data to its essential structure.

**Q2.** What does it mean for the kernel matrix $K$ to be positive semi-definite? Why is this required?

**Answer:** A matrix $K$ is positive semi-definite (PSD) if $\mathbf{v}^\top K \mathbf{v} \geq 0$ for all vectors $\mathbf{v}$. For the kernel matrix, this means: the quadratic form $\sum_{i,j} \alpha_i \alpha_j k(\mathbf{x}_i, \mathbf{x}_j)$ is always non-negative.

This is required because the SVM dual is a quadratic maximization problem: max⁡α∑iαi−12α⊤Kyα\max_\alpha \sum_i \alpha_i - \frac{1}{2} \boldsymbol{\alpha}^\top K_y \boldsymbol{\alpha}maxα∑iαi−21α⊤Kyα where KyK_yKy has (Ky)ij=yiyjk(xi,xj)(K_y)_{ij} = y_i y_j k(\mathbf{x}_i, \mathbf{x}_j)(Ky)ij=yiyjk(xi,xj). For this to be a convex optimization problem (with a well-defined maximum), KyK_yKy must be PSD — which follows from $K$ being PSD. If the kernel matrix is not PSD, the optimization can be unbounded or have no unique solution.

Mercer's theorem guarantees that any function of the form k(x,x′)=⟨ϕ(x),ϕ(x′)⟩k(\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{x}') = \langle \phi(\mathbf{x}), \phi(\mathbf{x}') \ranglek(x,x′)=⟨ϕ(x),ϕ(x′)⟩ produces a PSD kernel matrix — so "valid" kernels are exactly those that correspond to some inner product in some feature space.

**Q3.** An RBF kernel SVM has $\gamma = 100$. For two points $\mathbf{x}$ and x′\mathbf{x}'x′ with $\|\mathbf{x} - \mathbf{x}'\|^2 = 0.01$, compute k(x,x′)k(\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{x}')k(x,x′). For $\|\mathbf{x} - \mathbf{x}'\|^2 = 1.0$?

**Answer:**

k(x,x′)=e−100⋅0.01=e−1≈$\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{x}') = e^{-100 \cdot 0.01} = e^{-1} \approx$x,x′)=e−100⋅0.01=e−1≈0.368

k(x,x′)=e−100⋅1.0=e−100≈3.7×10−44≈$\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{x}') = e^{-100 \cdot 1.0} = e^{-100} \approx 3.7 \times 10^{-44} \approx$x,x′)=e−100⋅1.0=e−100≈3.7×10−44≈0

With $\gamma = 100$, only very nearby points ($\|\mathbf{x} - \mathbf{x}'\| < 0.1$) have appreciable similarity. Points at distance 1 are essentially invisible to each other. This corresponds to a very "local" model — each prediction is influenced only by the nearest training examples. Very large γ\gammaγ leads to overfitting (each training example is its own isolated island); very small γ\gammaγ leads to underfitting (all points look similar). The right γ\gammaγ is tuned by cross-validation.

**Q4.** Compare the maximum-margin criterion with logistic regression's cross-entropy criterion. When would you expect the SVM to outperform logistic regression, and vice versa?

**Answer:** Key differences:

**SVM advantages:** (1) Kernel trick gives efficient access to non-linear boundaries. (2) Sparsity (support vectors) makes prediction fast for large $m$. (3) Margin maximization has good theoretical generalization bounds (VC theory).

**Logistic regression advantages:** (1) Produces calibrated probabilities (SVM outputs are scores, not probabilities). (2) More naturally handles multi-class problems. (3) Better with very high-dimensional data (text) where the regularization effect of L2 LR is similar to SVM. (4) Computationally simpler for large $m$ (gradient descent scales better than the O(m2)O(m^2)O(m2) or O(m3)O(m^3)O(m3) kernel matrix).

**In practice:** With kernel trick and appropriate $C, \gamma$: SVM can win on medium-sized datasets with clear non-linear structure. Without kernel: logistic regression and linear SVM give similar results. For very high-dimensional data (text), both are similar; logistic regression is more commonly used today. For tabular data, gradient boosted trees (Chapter 10) typically outperform both.

#### Application Problems

**Q1.** You're training an RBF kernel SVM with C=1C = 1C=1 and $\gamma = 0.01$ on a training set. You observe: training accuracy = 65%, test accuracy = 63%. Then you try C=1000C = 1000C=1000 and $\gamma = 100$: training accuracy = 100%, test accuracy = 55%. Describe the bias-variance situation for each setting and propose a better approach.

Hint

The first setting has low training accuracy; the second has a large gap between training and test accuracy.

**Answer:**

**First setting ( C=1,γ=0.01C = 1, \gamma = 0.01C=1,γ=0.01): High bias, low variance.** Small $C$ means the model is heavily regularized (wide margin, many violations tolerated). Small γ\gammaγ means similarity is nearly uniform across all pairs. The model is too simple — it's a nearly linear classifier that can't capture the data's non-linear structure. Both training and test accuracy are 65% and 63% — nearly the same (small generalization gap), confirming high bias rather than overfitting.

**Second setting ( C=1000,γ=100C = $\gamma = 100C=$γ=100): Low bias, high variance.** Large $C$ is nearly hard-margin (no violations). Large γ\gammaγ means each training point is a nearly isolated island. The model memorizes every training example (100% training accuracy) but fails to generalize (55% test accuracy — worse than the first setting!). This is severe overfitting.

**Better approach:** Use 5-fold cross-validation to search a grid of (C,γ)(C, \gamma)(C,γ) values. Typical grid: C∈{0.1,1,10,100,1000}C \in \\{0.1, 1, 10, 100, 1000\\}C∈{0.1,1,10,100,1000} and $\gamma \in \\{0.001, 0.01, 0.1, 1, 10\\}$. The optimal point might be around C=10,γ=0.1C = $\gamma = 0.1C=$γ=0.1 — enough flexibility to capture non-linear patterns without memorizing noise. Standardize features before training (RBF kernel is sensitive to feature scale).

**Q2.** Explain why SVMs with RBF kernels can theoretically separate _any_ dataset (with all unique inputs) perfectly. Does this mean they will always generalize perfectly?

**Answer:** With large enough γ\gammaγ, the RBF kernel assigns near-zero similarity between any two distinct points. In the limit, each training example occupies an isolated "island" in feature space with its own support vector. The SVM can then assign any label to any example independently — perfect training accuracy.

No, this absolutely does not mean perfect generalization. On new test points, the SVM predicts based on similarity to support vectors. If γ\gammaγ is enormous, every test point is "too far" from all training examples, so the prediction is just the bias term $b$. The model has memorized the training set but has no information to generalize.

This illustrates the overfitting risk: 100% training accuracy with a kernel model is a warning sign, not a goal. The model must balance fitting training data (controlled by $C$) with generalizing to new points (controlled by γ\gammaγ). Both hyperparameters need careful tuning.

#### Think Deeper

**Q1.** The SVM solution depends only on the support vectors. This suggests that the margin is determined by the "hardest" (most borderline) examples. In contrast, logistic regression is influenced by all training examples, with examples far from the boundary having less influence but still contributing. What are the implications of this difference for outliers, noisy labels, and real-world deployment?

**Discussion:**

**Outliers:** SVMs are more robust to outliers _not near the boundary_ (they don't become support vectors and thus have zero influence). But an outlier _near the boundary_ can become a support vector and significantly distort the margin — potentially worse than logistic regression, which averages over all examples. The soft-margin SVM (with appropriate $C$) limits each example's influence via slack variables.

**Noisy labels:** If a mislabeled example lies on the wrong side of the margin, it becomes a support vector and pulls the boundary toward it. With small $C$, the soft margin absorbs this (high slack, small influence). With large $C$, it significantly distorts the boundary. Logistic regression distributes label noise across all examples, diluting its effect.

**Real-world deployment:** The sparsity of SVMs (only support vectors matter) is both a strength (fast prediction, compact model) and a weakness (the model can be very sensitive to the specific examples that happen to become support vectors). A slightly different training set might produce entirely different support vectors and a very different boundary. This is the high-variance property of SVMs near their support vectors. Cross-validation and appropriate $C$ selection mitigate this.

**Q2.** SVMs often generalize well even when the number of features $p$ far exceeds the number of training examples $m$ — the so-called p≫mp \gg mp≫m regime where classical statistical wisdom says generalization should be impossible. Why does this happen? Connect your answer to VC dimension theory and the role of margins.

**Discussion:** Classical intuition says that a model with more parameters than training examples can always fit the training data perfectly (and therefore overfit). For most model families, having p≫mp \gg mp≫m means the VC dimension exceeds the number of examples, and the classical generalization bound — which scales as O(dVC/m)O(\sqrt{d_{\text{VC}} / m})O(dVC/m) — becomes vacuous (greater than 1, providing no guarantee).

**Why SVMs are different: the margin rescues generalization.** The key insight from SVM theory is that the relevant complexity measure is not the number of dimensions but the **margin**. The fat-shattering dimension (and the closely related concept of the margin-based VC bound) depends on the margin γ\gammaγ and the norm of the weight vector $\|\mathbf{w}\|$, not on the ambient dimensionality $p$.

Formally, for a linear classifier with margin γ\gammaγ on data with $\|\mathbf{x}_i\| \leq R$, the generalization bound takes the form:

Test error≤Train error+O ⁣(R2/γ2m)\text{Test error} \leq \text{Train error} + O\\!\left(\sqrt{\frac{R^2 / \gamma^2}{m}}\right)Test error≤Train error+O(mR2/γ2)

The quantity R2/γ2R^2/\gamma^2R2/γ2 is the relevant complexity measure — it is the square of the ratio of data radius to margin. Crucially, $p$ does not appear. A wide-margin classifier in a million-dimensional space can generalize as well as a wide-margin classifier in two dimensions, if the margin is equally large.

**Why SVMs find wide-margin solutions in high dimensions.** In p≫mp \gg mp≫m settings, there are infinitely many hyperplanes that separate the training data with zero error. The SVM's max-margin objective specifically selects the hyperplane with the largest margin — the one that minimizes $\|\mathbf{w}\|^2$ (equivalently, maximizes $\gamma = 1/\|\mathbf{w}\|$). Among all zero-training-error classifiers, it chooses the one with the best generalization bound. This is why the SVM is not just a classifier but a principled generalization method: its objective directly optimizes the quantity that controls the generalization bound.

**The kernel connection.** With the RBF kernel, the implicit feature space is infinite-dimensional — yet the margin bound still applies, because what matters is the norm of the weight vector in that feature space (which the kernel SVM controls via the regularization parameter $C$), not the dimensionality. This is why kernel SVMs with appropriate $C$ generalize well on text classification problems with hundreds of thousands of sparse features and relatively few training examples, and why they were state-of-the-art in many p≫mp \gg mp≫m domains before deep learning.

### Chapter Summary

Support Vector Machines find the maximum-margin hyperplane between classes, with strong theoretical generalization guarantees (VC theory).

Key ideas:

* **Hard-margin SVM** : maximum margin for separable data; solution is determined by support vectors only
* **Soft-margin SVM** : allows margin violations controlled by $C$; equivalent to regularized hinge loss minimization
* **Kernel trick** : replace dot products with kernel evaluations to work implicitly in a (possibly infinite-dimensional) feature space
* **RBF kernel** : similarity based on distance; γ\gammaγ controls locality
* SVMs are excellent for medium-sized datasets with clear non-linear structure; gradient boosted trees (Chapter 10) often outperform them on tabular data; deep learning (Chapters 14–21) on image and text data

In Chapter 12, we shift to unsupervised learning: what can we discover when we have no labels at all?

---

## Chapter 12: Unsupervised Learning — Finding Structure Without Labels

All the models we've built so far required labeled data: house prices paired with features, reviews paired with sentiment labels. But labeled data is expensive — it requires human annotation, domain expertise, and time. Unlabeled data is cheap and abundant.

Unsupervised learning asks: what can we discover from data alone, without any labels? The answer turns out to be a lot. Clustering groups similar examples together. Anomaly detection flags unusual examples. Dimensionality reduction finds compact representations. Together, these techniques unlock the vast majority of real-world data that exists in unlabeled form.

### 12.1 Clustering: Grouping Similar Examples

#### Definition: Clustering

**Clustering** assigns each example to one of $K$ groups (clusters) such that examples within a cluster are more similar to each other than to examples in other clusters, with no predefined cluster labels.

The central challenge: "similar" is not defined by the problem statement — we must define it, and different similarity measures lead to different clusterings.

### 12.2 K-Means Clustering

K-means is the most widely used clustering algorithm. It is fast, simple, and often effective.

#### Definition: K-Means Algorithm

**Initialization:** Choose $K$ cluster centers $\boldsymbol{\mu}_1, \ldots, \boldsymbol{\mu}_K$ (randomly or using K-means++).

**Repeat until convergence:**

  1. **Assignment step:** Assign each example xi\mathbf{x}_ixi to its nearest cluster center:

zi=arg⁡min⁡k∥xi−μk∥2z_i = \arg\min_k \|\mathbf{x}_i - \boldsymbol{\mu}_k\|^2zi=argkmin∥xi−μk∥2

  2. **Update step:** Recompute each cluster center as the mean of assigned examples:

μk=1∣{i:zi=k}∣∑i:zi=kxi\boldsymbol{\mu}_k = \frac{1}{|\\{i : z_i = k\\}|}\sum_{i : z_i = k} \mathbf{x}_iμk=∣{i:zi=k}∣1i:zi=k∑xi

K-means minimizes the **within-cluster sum of squares (WCSS)** :

J=∑k=1K∑i:zi=k∥xi−μk∥2J = \sum_{k=1}^K \sum_{i : z_i = k} \|\mathbf{x}_i - \boldsymbol{\mu}_k\|^2J=k=1∑Ki:zi=k∑∥xi−μk∥2

**Convergence:** WCSS decreases at every step and reaches a local minimum. K-means always converges but may converge to a poor local minimum.

#### K-Means++: Better Initialization

Standard random initialization can lead to poor local minima. K-means++ spreads initial centers intelligently:

  1. Choose the first center uniformly at random
  2. For each subsequent center: choose the next center with probability proportional to its squared distance from the nearest existing center
  3. Points far from all existing centers are more likely to be chosen

K-means++ dramatically improves both the quality of the final clustering and convergence speed.

#### Choosing K: The Elbow Method

K-means requires you to specify $K$ in advance. How do you choose?

Plot WCSS vs. $K$. As $K$ increases, WCSS decreases (more clusters = smaller within-cluster variance). The **elbow** — the point where WCSS starts decreasing more slowly — often indicates the "natural" number of clusters.

Caution: the elbow is often unclear in real data. Alternatives include the **silhouette score** (measures how much better each point fits its own cluster than the next-best cluster) and domain knowledge.

#### Common Misconception

K-means assumes clusters are roughly spherical and of similar size. It will produce poor results on clusters that are elongated, non-convex, or very different in size. The "clusters" found by K-means are always convex Voronoi regions — if the true clusters are crescent-shaped or nested rings, K-means will fail entirely.

### 12.3 Hierarchical Clustering

Instead of pre-specifying $K$, **hierarchical clustering** builds a nested hierarchy of clusters, represented as a **dendrogram** (tree diagram).

#### Agglomerative (Bottom-Up) Clustering

  1. Start with each example as its own cluster ($m$ clusters)
  2. Repeatedly merge the two closest clusters until all examples are in one cluster
  3. The dendrogram records which clusters merged at each step

Different **linkage criteria** define "closest" between clusters:

* **Single linkage** : distance between the two closest points in the clusters (tends to produce elongated, "chained" clusters)
* **Complete linkage** : distance between the two farthest points (tends to produce compact, roughly spherical clusters)
* **Average linkage** : average pairwise distance (a good compromise)
* **Ward's method** : merge clusters that minimize the increase in WCSS (often best for compact, spherical clusters)

**Reading the dendrogram:** The height of each merge indicates the distance at which the two clusters merged. Cut the dendrogram at a horizontal level to obtain any number of clusters $K$ — the dendrogram shows all possible values of $K$ simultaneously.

### 12.4 DBSCAN: Density-Based Clustering

K-means and hierarchical clustering struggle with non-convex cluster shapes and noise. **DBSCAN (Density-Based Spatial Clustering of Applications with Noise)** overcomes these limitations.

#### Definition: DBSCAN

DBSCAN defines clusters as dense regions separated by low-density regions. Parameters: radius ϵ\epsilonϵ and minimum points minPts\text{minPts}minPts.

* **Core point:** A point with at least minPts\text{minPts}minPts neighbors within radius ϵ\epsilonϵ
* **Border point:** Not a core point, but within ϵ\epsilonϵ of a core point
* **Noise point (outlier):** Neither core nor border

A cluster is a maximal set of core points mutually reachable (connected through chains of core points within ϵ\epsilonϵ), plus the border points attached to them.

**Key advantages over K-means:**

  1. Discovers clusters of arbitrary shape (not just spherical)
  2. Automatically finds the number of clusters
  3. Labels outliers explicitly (noise points)
  4. Robust to initialization (deterministic, given ϵ\epsilonϵ and minPts\text{minPts}minPts)

**Limitation:** DBSCAN struggles with clusters of varying density (the same ϵ\epsilonϵ works for some clusters but not others) and degrades in very high dimensions.

### 12.5 Anomaly Detection

**Anomaly detection** identifies examples that are unusually different from the rest — outliers, novelties, or anomalies. Unlike classification, you typically have few or no labeled anomalies to learn from.

#### Statistical Approach

Fit a distribution to the normal data (e.g., a Gaussian). Assign anomaly scores based on the negative log-likelihood:

score(x)=−log⁡P(x)\text{score}(\mathbf{x}) = -\log P(\mathbf{x})score(x)=−logP(x)

High score = low probability = likely anomaly. Set a threshold on the score to flag anomalies.

For multivariate data: fit a multivariate Gaussian $\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mu}, \Sigma)$ to the training data. The anomaly score is the **Mahalanobis distance** :

d(x)=(x−μ)⊤Σ−1(x−μ)d(\mathbf{x}) = \sqrt{(\mathbf{x} - \boldsymbol{\mu})^\top \Sigma^{-1} (\mathbf{x} - \boldsymbol{\mu})}d(x)=(x−μ)⊤Σ−1(x−μ)

#### Isolation Forest

**Isolation Forest** works by a different principle: anomalies are easier to isolate. Build random decision trees on random feature subsets and random thresholds. The number of splits needed to isolate a point is the **isolation depth**. Anomalies (unusual points) are isolated quickly (shallow depth); normal points require many splits.

Isolation Forest is computationally efficient, handles high-dimensional data well, and requires no assumption about the distribution shape.

### 12.6 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** Run K-means with K=2K = 2K=2 manually on the points: {1,2,8,9}\\{1, 2, 8, 9\\}{1,2,8,9} (1D). Start with initial centers $\mu_1 = 1, \mu_2 = 5$. Show two iterations.

**Answer:**

**Iteration 1 — Assignment:**

* Point 1: ∣1−1∣=0|1-1| = 0∣1−1∣=0, ∣1−5∣=4|1-5| = 4∣1−5∣=4 → cluster 1
* Point 2: ∣2−1∣=1|2-1| = 1∣2−1∣=1, ∣2−5∣=3|2-5| = 3∣2−5∣=3 → cluster 1
* Point 8: ∣8−1∣=7|8-1| = 7∣8−1∣=7, ∣8−5∣=3|8-5| = 3∣8−5∣=3 → cluster 2
* Point 9: ∣9−1∣=8|9-1| = 8∣9−1∣=8, ∣9−5∣=4|9-5| = 4∣9−5∣=4 → cluster 2

Cluster 1: {1,2}\\{1, 2\\}{1,2}, Cluster 2: {8,9}\\{8, 9\\}{8,9}

**Iteration 1 — Update:** $\mu_1 = (1+2)/2 = 1.5$, $\mu_2 = (8+9)/2 = 8.5$

**Iteration 2 — Assignment:**

* Point 1: ∣1−1.5∣=0.5|1-1.5| = 0.5∣1−1.5∣=0.5, ∣1−8.5∣=7.5|1-8.5| = 7.5∣1−8.5∣=7.5 → cluster 1
* Point 2: ∣2−1.5∣=0.5|2-1.5| = 0.5∣2−1.5∣=0.5, ∣2−8.5∣=6.5|2-8.5| = 6.5∣2−8.5∣=6.5 → cluster 1
* Point 8: ∣8−1.5∣=6.5|8-1.5| = 6.5∣8−1.5∣=6.5, ∣8−8.5∣=0.5|8-8.5| = 0.5∣8−8.5∣=0.5 → cluster 2
* Point 9: ∣9−1.5∣=7.5|9-1.5| = 7.5∣9−1.5∣=7.5, ∣9−8.5∣=0.5|9-8.5| = 0.5∣9−8.5∣=0.5 → cluster 2

Same assignments → **converged**. Final clusters: {1,2}\\{1, 2\\}{1,2} and {8,9}\\{8, 9\\}{8,9} with centers 1.5 and 8.5.

**Q2.** K-means assigns each point to the nearest centroid using Euclidean distance. What happens if you have features on very different scales (e.g., income in thousands of dollars and age in years)?

**Answer:** Features with large scale dominate the Euclidean distance. If income ranges from 20,000 to 200,000 (range: 180,000) and age ranges from 20 to 80 (range: 60), income differences dominate the clustering. Two people with very different ages but similar incomes will be placed in the same cluster; two people with very similar ages but different incomes will be placed in different clusters — regardless of which differences are more meaningful.

**Fix:** Standardize all features to zero mean and unit variance before running K-means. After standardization, each feature contributes equally to distance calculations. This is essential preprocessing for any distance-based method (K-means, KNN, DBSCAN, SVM with RBF kernel).

**Q3.** Give one advantage DBSCAN has over K-means, and one situation where DBSCAN would fail.

**Answer:** **Advantage:** DBSCAN can find clusters of arbitrary shape. K-means only finds convex (Voronoi) clusters. For data with ring-shaped or crescent-shaped clusters, DBSCAN correctly identifies the clusters while K-means splits them incorrectly.

**DBSCAN failure:** When clusters have very different densities (e.g., one dense cluster of 10,000 points and one sparse cluster of 100 points). With any fixed ϵ\epsilonϵ, either the sparse cluster's points are treated as noise (if ϵ\epsilonϵ is set for the dense cluster) or the dense cluster merges with everything (if ϵ\epsilonϵ is set for the sparse cluster). HDBSCAN (hierarchical DBSCAN) addresses this by varying the density threshold.

**Q4.** K-means minimizes the within-cluster sum of squares (WCSS). Suppose you run K-means with K=3K = 3K=3 and obtain WCSS = 120. Then you run it with K=4K = 4K=4 and obtain WCSS = 95. Does it necessarily follow that K=4K = 4K=4 is a better clustering? Explain.

**Answer:** Not necessarily. WCSS always decreases (or stays the same) as $K$ increases — adding more clusters trivially allows each cluster to be smaller and more compact. In the extreme case, K=mK = mK=m (one cluster per point) gives WCSS = 0. But this is not a useful clustering.

The right criterion is not "which $K$ has lowest WCSS" but rather "which $K$ captures the natural structure while avoiding overfitting." A better $K$ should show a disproportionate drop in WCSS (the "elbow") rather than the gradual improvement expected from just adding clusters. If WCSS drops from 200 at K=2K=2K=2 to 120 at K=3K=3K=3 (large drop) but only from 120 to 95 at K=4K=4K=4 (smaller drop), the elbow suggests K=3K=3K=3 better captures the natural structure. Supplementing WCSS with silhouette score and domain knowledge gives a more complete picture.

**Q5.** What is the Mahalanobis distance, and how does it differ from Euclidean distance for anomaly detection?

**Answer:** The Euclidean distance treats all dimensions equally: dE(x,μ)=∥x−μ∥d_E(\mathbf{x}, \boldsymbol{\mu}) = \|\mathbf{x} - \boldsymbol{\mu}\|dE(x,μ)=∥x−μ∥. This ignores the scale and correlations among features — a point at distance 5 in the "income" direction might be very typical, while a point at distance 5 in the "rare transaction count" direction might be extreme.

The **Mahalanobis distance** accounts for both the scale and the correlation structure of the data:

dM(x)=(x−μ)⊤Σ−1(x−μ)d_M(\mathbf{x}) = \sqrt{(\mathbf{x} - \boldsymbol{\mu})^\top \Sigma^{-1} (\mathbf{x} - \boldsymbol{\mu})}dM(x)=(x−μ)⊤Σ−1(x−μ)

where Σ\SigmaΣ is the covariance matrix. Intuitively, $\Sigma^{-1}$ rescales the space so that directions of high variance are "compressed" and directions of low variance are "stretched" — a point that is unusual relative to the data's own spread is flagged as an anomaly, regardless of which feature it deviates in.

For anomaly detection: a point is anomalous if its Mahalanobis distance from the center μ\boldsymbol{\mu}μ exceeds a threshold. Under a multivariate Gaussian, dM2d_M^2dM2 follows a chi-squared distribution with $n$ degrees of freedom, providing a principled threshold.

#### Application Problems

**Q1.** An e-commerce company wants to segment customers into groups for targeted marketing. They have purchase history (200 product categories) and demographic data (age, location, income). Describe a complete clustering pipeline — including preprocessing, algorithm choice, and evaluation.

Hint

Think about the high dimensionality of the purchase data, the different scales of different features, and how to evaluate cluster quality without ground-truth labels.

**Answer:**

**Step 1 — Preprocessing:**

* Purchase history: 200-dimensional binary vectors (bought/didn't buy). Normalize by number of purchases per customer (TF-IDF analog for items). These are already in [0,1] range.
* Demographics: standardize age and income to zero mean, unit variance. One-hot encode categorical location.

**Step 2 — Dimensionality reduction:** 200 purchase dimensions is high. Apply PCA (Chapter 13) to reduce to 20–50 principal components that capture ~90% of variance. This removes noise dimensions and makes clustering more meaningful.

**Step 3 — Algorithm choice:** K-means with K-means++ initialization. For customer segmentation, we expect roughly spherical clusters (groups of similar-behavior customers). K-means is fast, scalable, and interpretable. Try K = 4 to 10.

**Step 4 — Choosing K:** Elbow method on WCSS; supplemented by silhouette scores. Also: business constraint — 5–8 segments are actionable; 50 segments are not.

**Step 5 — Evaluation without labels:**

* **Silhouette score:** measures cluster cohesion vs. separation (higher is better, range -1 to 1)
* **Interpretation:** Examine the centroid of each cluster — what are the top-purchased items? What demographics are typical? Does each cluster "tell a story"?
* **Business validation:** Do the segments align with known customer archetypes? Do different segments respond differently to marketing?

**Step 6 — Iterate:** Clustering is exploratory. Try different $K$, different features, different algorithms. The "right" clustering is the one most actionable for the business.

**Q2.** You apply K-means with K=5K = 5K=5 to a dataset and inspect the resulting clusters. One cluster contains 4,500 of your 5,000 examples; the other four clusters contain roughly 125 examples each. What does this suggest, and what would you do next?

Hint

Think about whether the algorithm has done something sensible or whether something has gone wrong with initialization or data scaling.

**Answer:** A single cluster containing 90% of the data while four clusters each have ~2.5% suggests one of two problems:

**Problem 1 — Poor initialization:** One or more initial centroids may have been placed in a sparse region, causing them to "attract" almost no points. The dominant cluster absorbed nearly everything because its centroid happened to be well-centered in the main data mass. Fix: use K-means++ initialization (which spreads initial centers intelligently) and run multiple restarts, keeping the result with the lowest WCSS.

**Problem 2 — Genuine structure:** Perhaps the data really does have one large natural group and several small, tight sub-groups. In this case, the clustering may be correct but K=5K = 5K=5 is the wrong choice. Inspect the four small clusters: do they correspond to meaningful sub-populations (outlier types, rare categories)? If not, try smaller $K$.

Next steps: (1) re-run with K-means++ and 10+ random restarts; (2) plot the elbow curve for K=2K = 2K=2 to K=10K = 10K=10 to see where structure naturally breaks; (3) check whether features need standardization (a dominant feature with large variance can make all distances point toward one cluster).

#### Think Deeper

**Q1.** Clustering is sometimes called "ill-posed" because there's no unique correct answer. A set of data points can be validly clustered in many different ways depending on the algorithm, the number of clusters, the distance metric, and the scale of features. Does this make unsupervised learning less useful than supervised learning? How do practitioners handle this ambiguity?

**Discussion:** The "ill-posedness" of clustering is real and important to acknowledge. Unlike supervised learning, where we can measure accuracy against ground-truth labels, clustering has no single correct answer. Different algorithms answer different implicit questions: K-means finds compact spherical groups; hierarchical clustering reveals nested structure; DBSCAN finds density-based regions.

This doesn't make clustering less useful — it makes it differently useful. Practitioners handle the ambiguity by:

  1. **Defining the goal first:** Clustering for customer segmentation asks "what groups best enable targeted marketing?" — the criterion is business utility, not mathematical correctness. Clustering for anomaly detection asks "what is typical?" — the criterion is statistical.

  2. **Using multiple algorithms:** Running K-means, DBSCAN, and hierarchical clustering and looking for structure that appears consistently across methods. Stable structure is more trustworthy.

  3. **External validation:** If even a few labels are available, they can validate cluster quality (adjusted Rand index, normalized mutual information). A clustering that correctly groups the labeled examples is more likely to be meaningful.

  4. **Feature engineering as a decision:** The choice of features and their weights encodes implicit assumptions about what matters. Including location in customer clustering says "location is relevant"; excluding it says it's not. The clusterer should make these choices consciously.

Ultimately, the output of clustering is not ground truth — it's a structured summary of the data that may or may not align with meaningful real-world categories. Treat it as a hypothesis generator, not a conclusion.

### Chapter Summary

Unsupervised learning finds structure in unlabeled data. Key methods:

* **K-means** : fast, simple clustering into $K$ spherical groups; sensitive to initialization (use K-means++) and feature scaling; requires pre-specifying $K$
* **Hierarchical clustering** : builds a dendrogram; shows all $K$ simultaneously; Ward's linkage usually best for compact clusters
* **DBSCAN** : density-based; finds arbitrary-shape clusters; automatically identifies outliers; requires ϵ\epsilonϵ and minPts\text{minPts}minPts tuning
* **Anomaly detection** : isolation forest and Mahalanobis distance; identifies unusual examples without requiring labeled anomalies

In Chapter 13, we turn to dimensionality reduction — finding low-dimensional representations of high-dimensional data. This both aids visualization and serves as preprocessing for clustering and supervised learning.

---

## Chapter 13: Dimensionality Reduction — PCA and t-SNE

A single image in a standard dataset might be $224 \times 224$ pixels × 3 color channels = 150,528 dimensions. A bag-of-words document is 10,000–100,000 dimensions. Working in such high-dimensional spaces creates problems: clustering distances become meaningless (the "curse of dimensionality"), visualizing data is impossible, and many algorithms slow to a crawl.

Dimensionality reduction finds a low-dimensional representation that preserves the structure most relevant to our task. Two methods dominate: PCA, which finds the directions of maximum variance through linear projections, and t-SNE, which preserves local neighborhoods for visualization. Both reveal structure in data that is invisible in the raw high-dimensional form.

### 13.1 The Curse of Dimensionality

As dimensionality $d$ increases, some deeply counterintuitive things happen:

**Distances become meaningless:** In high dimensions, almost all pairs of points are roughly the same distance from each other. The ratio of the maximum to minimum pairwise distance approaches 1. Nearest-neighbor search becomes useless — "nearest" doesn't distinguish close from far.

**Exponential data requirements:** To uniformly cover a $d$-dimensional unit cube at resolution ϵ\epsilonϵ, you need $\epsilon^{-d}$ points. For $\epsilon = 0.1$ and d=100d = 100d=100, that's 1010010^{100}10100 points — more than atoms in the universe.

**Most volume is near the surface:** In a $d$-dimensional sphere, the fraction of volume within distance ϵ\epsilonϵ of the surface is 1−(1−ϵ)d1 - (1-\epsilon)^d1−(1−ϵ)d, which approaches 1 as $d$ grows. With d=100d = 100d=100 and $\epsilon = 0.1$: 1−0.9100≈0.$99971 - 0.9^{100} \approx 0.999$71−0.9100≈0.9999997 — essentially all points are near the surface.

These properties explain why high-dimensional ML problems are hard, why we need dimensionality reduction, and why deep learning (which learns feature representations) has been so successful.

### 13.2 Principal Component Analysis (PCA)

PCA is the most widely used dimensionality reduction technique. It finds the directions of maximum variance in the data.

**The question PCA answers:** If I must project my $n$-dimensional data onto a $k$-dimensional subspace (k<nk < nk<n), which subspace should I choose to preserve the most information (variance)?

**The answer:** The subspace spanned by the top $k$ eigenvectors of the data covariance matrix.

#### Definition: Principal Component Analysis

Given data matrix $X \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$ (centered: mean of each feature is 0), the **principal components** are the eigenvectors of the sample covariance matrix $\hat{\Sigma} = X^\top X / (m-1)$.

The $k$ principal components $\mathbf{v}_1, \ldots, \mathbf{v}_k$ (corresponding to the $k$ largest eigenvalues $\lambda_1 \geq \ldots \geq \lambda_k$) define the optimal $k$-dimensional subspace.

The **projected (reduced-dimension) data** is:

Z=XVkZ = XV_kZ=XVk

where Vk=[v1∣⋯∣vk]∈Rn×kV_k = [\mathbf{v}_1 | \cdots | \mathbf{v}_k] \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times k}Vk=[v1∣⋯∣vk]∈Rn×k. Each column of $Z$ is one principal component score.

#### Computing PCA via SVD

In practice, PCA is computed via the **Singular Value Decomposition (SVD)** of the centered data matrix:

X=UΣV⊤X = U \Sigma V^\topX=UΣV⊤

where U∈Rm×mU \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times m}U∈Rm×m and V∈Rn×nV \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}V∈Rn×n are orthogonal matrices, and $\Sigma \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$ is diagonal with non-negative entries (singular values) $\sigma_1 \geq \sigma_2 \geq \ldots \geq 0$.

The principal components are the columns of $V$. The singular values relate to eigenvalues by $\lambda_i = \sigma_i^2 / (m-1)$.

SVD is numerically stable and preferred over directly computing the covariance matrix (which can lose precision when $n$ is large).

#### Explained Variance

The **explained variance ratio** of principal component $k$ is $\lambda_k / \sum_j \lambda_j$ — the fraction of total variance captured by that component. Plot these as a **scree plot** (eigenvalues vs. component number) and choose $k$ to retain 90–95% of total variance.

#### Geometric Interpretation

PCA rotates the coordinate system so that the new axes (principal components) align with the directions of maximum data variance. No information is lost in the rotation — it's the _projection_ (keeping only the top $k$ components) that discards the least-variant dimensions.

**Example — 2D to 1D:** Data is a cloud elongated along the diagonal (x1≈x2x_1 \approx x_2x1≈x2). PCA finds that the first principal component is the diagonal direction (direction of maximum variance). Projecting onto this 1D line captures most of the data's structure while halving the dimensionality.

#### Common Misconception

PCA finds the directions of maximum _variance_ , not the directions most predictive of the label. PCA is unsupervised — it doesn't know the labels. A direction with high variance might be completely uninformative for classification (e.g., lighting variation in photos), while a direction with low variance might be crucial (e.g., the specific shape of an eye). For supervised dimensionality reduction (where labels matter), use methods like Linear Discriminant Analysis (LDA) or learn representations jointly with the classifier.

### 13.3 Whitening and Other PCA Applications

**Whitening (PCA whitening):** After PCA projection, divide each component by its standard deviation (λk\sqrt{\lambda_k}λk). The result has unit variance in every principal direction — isotropic features. Whitening is useful as preprocessing before algorithms that assume uncorrelated, unit-variance features.

**Noise reduction:** Keep only the top $k$ principal components (capturing the signal) and discard the remaining dimensions (dominated by noise). Reconstructing the data from the $k$ components produces a "denoised" version.

**Data compression:** Store the $k$ principal component scores instead of the full $n$-dimensional features. For n=1000n = 1000n=1000 and k=50k = 50k=50, this is a 20× compression.

**Face recognition (eigenfaces):** One of PCA's famous applications. Images of faces form a high-dimensional space, but actual faces cluster in a low-dimensional "face subspace." PCA finds this subspace, and the principal components are called "eigenfaces" — they represent the dominant modes of facial variation (e.g., lighting, age, gender expression).

### 13.4 t-SNE: Visualizing High-Dimensional Data

PCA's linear projection is interpretable but may not preserve the local structure that makes high-dimensional data interesting. **t-SNE (t-distributed Stochastic Neighbor Embedding)** is designed specifically for 2D or 3D visualization.

#### The t-SNE Idea

**Step 1:** For each pair of points (i,j)(i, j)(i,j) in the original space, compute a similarity (probability) that point $i$ would pick point $j$ as its neighbor, using a Gaussian kernel:

pj∣i=exp⁡(−∥xi−xj∥2/2σi2)∑k≠iexp⁡(−∥xi−xk∥2/2σi2)p_{j|i} = \frac{$\mathbf{x}_i - \mathbf{x}_j\|^2 / 2\sigma_i^2)}{\sum_{k \neq i}$\mathbf{x}_i - \mathbf{x}_k\|^2 / 2\sigma_i^2)}pj∣i=∑k=iexp(−∥xi−xk∥2/2σi2)exp(−∥xi−xj∥2/2σi2)

Symmetrize: pij=(pj∣i+pi∣j)/(2m)p_{ij} = (p_{j|i} + p_{i|j}) / (2m)pij=(pj∣i+pi∣j)/(2m)

**Step 2:** In the 2D embedding space, define a similar similarity using a **Student's t-distribution** (heavier tails than Gaussian):

qij=(1+∥yi−yj∥2)−1∑k≠l(1+∥yk−yl∥2)−1q_{ij} = \frac{(1 + \|\mathbf{y}_i - \mathbf{y}_j\|^2)^{-1}}{\sum_{k \neq l}(1 + \|\mathbf{y}_k - \mathbf{y}_l\|^2)^{-1}}qij=∑k=l(1+∥yk−yl∥2)−1(1+∥yi−yj∥2)−1

**Step 3:** Find the 2D embedding {yi}\\{y_i\\}{yi} that minimizes the KL divergence between the high-dimensional similarities $P$ and low-dimensional similarities $Q$:

KL(P∥Q)=∑i,jpijlog⁡pijqij\text{KL}(P \| Q) = \sum_{i,j} p$\log \frac{p_{ij}}{q$}KL(P∥Q)=i,j∑pijlogqijpij

The t-distribution in the embedding space has heavier tails than the Gaussian used for the input space. This creates a "crowding problem" solution: nearby points in high-D get pushed far enough apart in 2D (moderate distances in 2D → small q_ij, large p_ij → large KL gradient), so they don't all pile up in the center.

#### Interpreting t-SNE Plots

t-SNE preserves local structure (nearby points in high-D stay nearby in 2D) but **not global structure** (distances between clusters are not meaningful). The key interpretations:

* Clusters in a t-SNE plot reflect genuine local structure in high-D
* The distances _between_ clusters in a t-SNE plot are not meaningful
* Cluster sizes and shapes in t-SNE are not meaningful
* **Different random initializations produce different plots** — always check that structure is consistent across runs

The **perplexity** hyperparameter (typically 5–50) controls the effective number of neighbors considered for each point. High perplexity → more global structure preserved; low perplexity → more local.

### 13.5 UMAP: A Modern Alternative

**UMAP (Uniform Manifold Approximation and Projection)** is a newer dimensionality reduction method that:

* Is faster than t-SNE (especially for large datasets)
* Preserves more global structure (meaningful distances between clusters)
* Can be used as a preprocessing step for ML (unlike t-SNE, which is only for visualization)
* Has a principled mathematical foundation in Riemannian geometry

For visualization: t-SNE and UMAP produce qualitatively similar results; UMAP is faster. For preprocessing: UMAP is preferred because global structure is preserved and it can project new data.

### 13.6 Connection to SVMs and Embeddings

Recall from Chapter 11 that the SVM kernel trick projects data into an implicit high-dimensional feature space. PCA projects in the opposite direction: from high-dimensional to low-dimensional. Together, they illustrate a key principle: the "right" dimensionality for a problem depends on the structure of the data, not on the number of original features.

Word embeddings (Chapter 19) will take this further: instead of an unsupervised linear projection, they _learn_ a dense low-dimensional representation that is maximally predictive of linguistic context. The connection between PCA (unsupervised linear representation learning) and word embeddings (supervised non-linear representation learning) is deep — both are trying to find the low-dimensional structure in high-dimensional data.

### 13.7 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** A dataset has 100 features, and PCA gives eigenvalues $\lambda_1 = 50, \lambda_2 = 30, \lambda_3 = 10$, with the remaining 97 eigenvalues summing to 10. How much variance do the first 3 components explain?

**Answer:**

Total variance = 50+30+10+10=10050 + 30 + 10 + 10 = 10050+30+10+10=100.

Variance explained by top 3 components = (50+30+10)/100=90%(50 + 30 + 10) / 100 = 90\%(50+30+10)/100=90%.

We can reduce from 100 dimensions to 3 while retaining 90% of variance — a 97% reduction in dimensionality. This is common in practice: real-world high-dimensional data often has most of its variance concentrated in a small number of directions.

**Q2.** Why does PCA center the data (subtract the mean) before computing the covariance matrix?

**Answer:** Centering ensures that the covariance matrix X⊤X/(m−1)X^\top X / (m-1)X⊤X/(m−1) measures the _variance_ of the data around its mean — the relevant quantity for PCA. Without centering, the covariance matrix includes the effect of the mean vector, and the first principal component would point toward the mean rather than capturing the direction of maximum spread. The first "principal component" would be trivially the direction of the mean, which is uninformative about the data's structure. After centering, all principal components capture genuine variance around the center.

**Q3.** A t-SNE plot of a dataset shows 5 distinct clusters. Can you conclude that: (a) the original data has 5 natural groups? (b) the clusters in the plot are the same "size" in original space? (c) the spatial arrangement of clusters in the plot reflects their relative positions in original space?

**Answer:** **(a) Yes, with caution:** t-SNE is designed to reveal cluster structure, and well-separated clusters in a t-SNE plot typically reflect genuine local structure in the original data. This conclusion is reasonable, though you should verify with other methods.

**(b) No:** t-SNE rescales and distorts distances. A small cluster in the t-SNE plot might be a large, dense group in original space; a large cluster might be a small, sparse group. Cluster sizes in t-SNE plots are not meaningful.

**(c) No:** This is the critical caveat for t-SNE. The algorithm preserves local neighborhoods but not global distances. Two clusters that are nearby in the t-SNE plot might actually be far apart in original space, or vice versa. Never interpret the spatial arrangement of clusters in a t-SNE plot as reflecting their relative distances in original space.

**Q4.** Compare PCA and feature normalization (standardization). Both modify the feature representation before applying another algorithm. What does each one do, and when would you use each?

**Answer:** **Feature normalization/standardization** rescales each feature independently to zero mean and unit variance. It does not change the number of features or rotate the coordinate system. It prevents features with large numerical scales from dominating distance-based algorithms. Used as preprocessing for almost any ML algorithm.

**PCA** rotates the coordinate system to align with the directions of maximum variance, then reduces dimensionality by keeping only the top-$k$ components. It decorrelates features (principal components are orthogonal), reduces dimensionality, and can remove noise.

Use standardization: always, before distance-based methods (K-means, SVM, KNN) and when features are on different scales.

Use PCA: when the data is high-dimensional and you suspect most variance is in a low-dimensional subspace; for visualization (reduce to 2–3 components); for noise reduction; before K-means when many features are correlated.

Often both: standardize first, then apply PCA. Standardization ensures each feature contributes equally to the PCA; otherwise features with large raw variance dominate the first component.

#### Application Problems

**Q1.** You have a medical imaging dataset: 5,000 MRI scans, each with $256 \times 256 = 65,536$ voxels. You want to cluster patients by brain structure similarity. Using raw voxels as features would be infeasible. Describe how you would use PCA to make clustering tractable, and discuss how many principal components to keep.

Hint

Think about the computational cost of clustering in 65,536 dimensions vs. 100 dimensions. Also consider: what fraction of variance is typically captured by the top principal components for images?

**Answer:**

**Step 1 — Flatten and center:** Represent each MRI as a 65,536-dimensional vector; subtract the mean MRI across the dataset.

**Step 2 — PCA:** Compute the SVD of the centered data matrix X∈R5000×65536X \in \mathbb{R}^{5000 \times 65536}X∈R5000×65536. Note: computing the full $65,536 \times 65,536$ covariance matrix is infeasible. Instead, compute the SVD of $X$ directly (or use the $5000 \times 5000$ matrix $XX^\top$, since m<nm < nm<n, and extract right singular vectors from the top left singular vectors).

**Number of components to keep:** MRI data is typically highly structured — brain anatomy occupies a relatively small subspace of all possible $256 \times 256$ images. A common heuristic: keep enough components for 90–95% explained variance. For medical images, this often requires 100–500 principal components (vs. 65,536 raw voxels). Plot the scree plot and choose the "elbow," or retain components explaining >90% of variance.

**Step 3 — Cluster in reduced space:** Apply K-means or agglomerative clustering on the 200-dimensional (say) PCA representation. Clustering in 200D is tractable; in 65,536D it would suffer from the curse of dimensionality and be computationally prohibitive.

**Caveat:** PCA is a linear projection. Brain structure differences that appear in non-linear subspaces (e.g., topological connectivity differences) might be lost. For large datasets, consider UMAP or autoencoders (non-linear generalization of PCA using neural networks, covered in Chapter 24).

**Q2.** You apply PCA to a dataset and find that the first two principal components explain 45% and 30% of total variance respectively. A colleague proposes using both components as features for a downstream logistic regression model. You notice that the raw data has 50 features. Describe two reasons this dimensionality reduction might help the logistic regression, and one scenario where it could hurt.

**Answer:**

**Reason 1 — Reduced overfitting (variance reduction):** With 50 features and a limited training set, logistic regression may overfit by finding spurious correlations between noise features and the label. By compressing to 2 principal components, we drastically reduce the number of parameters from 50 to 2, greatly lowering variance. The model is forced to focus on the two dominant directions of variation in the data.

**Reason 2 — Removes multicollinearity:** Real-world datasets often have highly correlated features (e.g., multiple demographic variables that move together). Logistic regression is unstable when features are nearly collinear — weights blow up. PCA produces orthogonal components, so multicollinearity is eliminated by construction.

**Scenario where it hurts:** If the discriminative signal lives in lower-variance directions (as discussed in the Common Misconception in Section 13.2), PCA may discard the most predictive dimensions. For example, if the label is determined by a combination of features that only explains 5% of total variance, neither PC1 (45%) nor PC2 (30%) will capture it. The compressed 2-component model may perform worse than the full 50-feature model. This is why supervised dimensionality reduction (LDA, or learning the representation jointly with the classifier) is often superior to PCA for classification tasks.

#### Think Deeper

**Q1.** PCA and SVMs with linear kernels are both linear methods. The SVM finds a direction (the maximum-margin hyperplane normal) that best _separates_ two classes. PCA finds a direction that maximizes _variance_. These objectives can disagree — a direction of maximum variance might not be the most discriminative, and vice versa. Can you construct a toy example where the PCA direction is useless for classification, and explain why this can happen?

**Discussion:** Classic example: data in 2D where class 1 is at (0,y)(0, y)(0,y) for y∈[−10,$\in [-10,$∈[−10,10] (a vertical stripe) and class 0 is at (1,y)(1, y)(1,y) for y∈[−10,$\in [-10,$∈[−10,10] (another vertical stripe, shifted right by 1). The $x$-axis separates the classes perfectly (a vertical line at x=0.5x = 0.5x=0.5). The variance in the $y$ direction is enormous (range 20). The variance in the $x$ direction is tiny (range 1, actually just a bimodal cluster at 0 and 1).

PCA finds the first component is the $y$-axis (maximum variance). Projecting onto this axis mixes both classes completely — useless for classification. The $x$-axis (the discriminative direction) has low variance and is the _second_ PCA component.

This happens whenever the direction of maximum variance is orthogonal to or irrelevant for the class boundary. It's common in real data: in face images, lighting variation (not diagnostic) often dominates variance; in medical images, patient positioning variation dominates; in financial data, broad market movements dominate even if the predictive signal is in idiosyncratic movements.

**Lesson:** For supervised tasks, supervised dimensionality reduction methods (Linear Discriminant Analysis, which maximizes between-class vs. within-class variance ratio; or learned neural network embeddings) are often better than PCA. PCA is best used as a preprocessing step for unsupervised tasks or when you want to remove noise/redundancy rather than optimize for discrimination.

**Q2.** t-SNE is widely used for visualization, but it has a critical property that makes it unsuitable for preprocessing features for a classifier. Explain this limitation, and contrast it with UMAP's behavior in the same regard.

**Discussion:** The core limitation of t-SNE for preprocessing is that it **cannot project new data points** into the existing embedding. The t-SNE algorithm jointly optimizes the positions of all training points simultaneously — it produces a fixed mapping specific to the training set, but there is no closed-form function f:Rn→Rkf: \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}^kf:Rn→Rk that can be applied to a new point at inference time. If you train a classifier on t-SNE embeddings of the training set, you have no principled way to embed a new test point — you would need to re-run t-SNE including the new point, which changes all the existing embeddings.

UMAP, by contrast, learns an explicit parametric mapping (based on a fuzzy simplicial set construction) that _can_ be applied to new data. After fitting UMAP on training data, new points can be projected using the learned mapping, making UMAP usable as a genuine preprocessing step for downstream classifiers.

Additionally, t-SNE's non-determinism (different random seeds produce different embeddings) and its distortion of global distances make it unreliable for anything beyond exploratory visualization. For production feature engineering requiring dimensionality reduction, PCA (for linear structure) or UMAP (for non-linear structure) are the appropriate choices.

### Chapter Summary

Dimensionality reduction transforms high-dimensional data into lower-dimensional representations that preserve the most important structure.

Key methods:

* **PCA** : finds the orthogonal directions of maximum variance via eigendecomposition of the covariance matrix (or equivalently, SVD of the data matrix); preserves global linear structure; provides interpretable components and explained variance ratio
* **t-SNE** : preserves local neighborhoods for 2D/3D visualization; uses a t-distribution in the embedding space to prevent crowding; cluster structure in t-SNE is meaningful, but distances between clusters are not
* **UMAP** : faster than t-SNE, preserves more global structure, usable as preprocessing

**Connection to earlier chapters:** PCA is the projection idea from Chapter 2 applied to the empirical covariance matrix. The kernel SVM (Chapter 11) goes in the opposite direction — projecting to higher dimensions. Word embeddings (Chapter 19) will learn non-linear low-dimensional representations using neural networks, building directly on these ideas.

In Chapter 14, we begin the neural network section of the book, starting with the simplest neural network: the perceptron.

---

## Chapter 14: The Perceptron and Neural Network Foundations

In 1943, Warren McCulloch and Walter Pitts proposed that neurons in the brain could be modeled as simple binary threshold units: they fire (output 1) if the sum of their inputs exceeds a threshold, and don't fire (output 0) otherwise. This biological inspiration gave birth to the first model of artificial intelligence: the **artificial neuron**.

We've been building toward this for several chapters. Logistic regression (Chapter 7) is already a single artificial neuron with a sigmoid activation — a continuous, probabilistic version of the threshold unit.
