Building responsible ML systems requires not just knowing how to train a model, but understanding who it affects, how it can fail, and how to make its decisions explainable and correctable.

### 25.1 The Real Cost of "Neutral" Algorithms

A persistent myth in ML is that algorithms are neutral — that replacing human judgment with statistical models eliminates bias. This is false. ML models learn from historical data generated by humans and human institutions, which encode historical biases. A model trained on biased data learns biased patterns.

**Three entry points for bias:**

**1\. Training data bias:** Facial recognition systems trained predominantly on lighter-skinned faces performed significantly worse on darker-skinned faces (Buolamwini and Gebru, 2018). The model was not maliciously designed to discriminate — it was trained on data that underrepresented darker-skinned individuals, and its accuracy on this group was simply never measured. This reflects the **representation gap** problem.

**2\. Historical bias:** A recidivism prediction model (COMPAS) trained to predict reoffending learned from historical arrest data that reflected racially biased policing. Black defendants were assigned higher risk scores at approximately twice the rate of white defendants for the same level of actual reoffending. The bias was not in the algorithm — it was in the target variable itself (arrests, not true reoffending).

**3\. Feedback loops:** A model that predicts who to audit for tax fraud is trained on past audit outcomes. If audits are more likely in low-income neighborhoods (due to lower audit costs, not higher fraud rates), the model learns to predict fraud there, causing more audits there, generating more "evidence" of fraud there — a self-reinforcing cycle.

### 25.2 Definitions of Fairness

Before fixing bias, we need to define fairness mathematically. This turns out to be harder than it seems — many natural definitions are mutually incompatible.

Let Y^\hat{Y}Y^ be the model's prediction, $Y$ the true label, and $A$ the protected attribute (e.g., race, gender).

#### Definition: Demographic Parity

P(Y^$=1)P(\hat{Y} = 1 | A = 0) = P(\hat{Y} = 1 | A = 1)P(Y^$=1)

Equal probability of positive prediction across groups. If a hiring model grants interviews to 30% of applicants, it should grant interviews to 30% of each demographic group.

_Problem:_ Demographic parity doesn't account for actual qualification differences. If Group A has 40% qualified applicants and Group B has 20%, demographic parity requires interviewing the same fraction from each group — by definition selecting less-qualified Group A candidates or more-qualified Group B candidates for different reasons.

#### Definition: Equalized Odds

P(Y^$=1)P(\hat{Y} = 1 | Y = 1, A = 0) = P(\hat{Y} = 1 | Y = 1, A = 1)P(Y^$=1) P(Y^$=1)P(\hat{Y} = 1 | Y = 0, A = 0) = P(\hat{Y} = 1 | Y = 0, A = 1)P(Y^$=1)

Equal true positive rates AND equal false positive rates across groups. A credit model satisfies equalized odds if: (1) among truly creditworthy applicants, it approves equal fractions across groups; (2) among truly non-creditworthy applicants, it incorrectly approves equal fractions across groups.

_Problem:_ Satisfying equalized odds requires knowing the true label $Y$ — but in lending, we only observe repayment for those who were given loans. For those denied, we never observe the counterfactual.

#### Definition: Calibration

P(Y=1∣P^=p,A=a)=p for all p,aP(Y = 1 | \hat{P} = p, A = a) = p \text{ for all } p, aP(Y=1∣P^=p,A=a)=p for all p,a

The model's probability estimates should be equally well-calibrated for all groups. If the model says 70% probability of loan default for both Group A and Group B, approximately 70% of those individuals should actually default.

#### The Impossibility Theorem

**Chouldechova (2017) and Kleinberg et al. (2017)** proved independently that **no classifier can simultaneously satisfy calibration, equal false positive rates, and equal false negative rates unless base rates are equal across groups.** In the criminal justice context: if recidivism rates differ between demographic groups, any well-calibrated predictor must necessarily have different false positive rates across groups.

This is not a failure of algorithm design — it is a mathematical impossibility. Which fairness definition to prioritize is a **value judgment** , not a technical question. Different stakeholders will legitimately disagree on the right criterion.

### 25.3 Measuring and Mitigating Bias

#### Measuring Bias

  1. **Disaggregated evaluation:** Always evaluate accuracy metrics separately for each protected group. Overall accuracy can be high while performance on minority groups is poor (the "accuracy paradox").

  2. **Counterfactual fairness:** Would the prediction change if the individual's protected attribute changed, all else equal? For a name-based model, would "Emily" get a different loan decision than "Latasha" for the same financial profile?

  3. **Intersectionality:** Bias compounds at intersections. A model might be fair for women overall and fair for Black people overall, but biased against Black women specifically.

#### Mitigating Bias

**Pre-processing** (fix the training data):

* **Reweighting:** Assign higher loss weight to underrepresented groups
* **Resampling:** Oversample minority groups (e.g., SMOTE, Chapter 22)
* **Counterfactual data augmentation:** Generate counterfactual examples (change protected attribute, keep everything else constant)

**In-processing** (modify the learning objective):

* **Fairness constraints:** Add Lagrange multiplier constraints to the optimization to enforce demographic parity or equalized odds during training
* **Adversarial debiasing:** Train a classifier to predict $Y$ while an adversarial network tries to predict $A$ from the representations — force the model to learn representations that don't encode $A$

**Post-processing** (calibrate the output):

* **Threshold adjustment:** Use different decision thresholds for different groups to equalize false positive/negative rates. This is the simplest approach: no retraining needed.
* **Calibration:** Platt scaling or isotonic regression separately for each group

**Warning:** Bias mitigation does not guarantee fairness. Each technique optimizes for a specific definition; check multiple definitions after mitigation. Also: removing bias along one dimension can introduce bias along another.

### 25.4 Interpretability and Explainability

High-stakes ML decisions require justification. A loan denial must be explainable; a medical diagnosis recommendation should be interpretable; a fraud detection flag should be auditable.

#### Global vs. Local Explanations

**Global interpretability:** Understand the model's overall behavior. Which features matter most? What are the general patterns?

**Local interpretability:** Understand a specific prediction. Why did this particular individual get denied?

#### Inherently Interpretable Models

Some model classes are interpretable by design:

* **Linear models:** The coefficient wjw_jwj directly measures the effect of feature $j$ on the prediction
* **Decision trees (shallow):** The decision path from root to leaf is a logical rule
* **Rule-based systems:** Explicitly represented as if-then-else rules

The interpretability-accuracy tradeoff is often invoked to justify using black-box models. Rudin (2019) argues persuasively that this tradeoff is often overstated: in many domains, well-designed interpretable models match the accuracy of black-box models, and the claimed accuracy gains from black boxes do not justify the loss of transparency.

#### SHAP: SHapley Additive exPlanations

**SHAP** (Lundberg & Lee, 2017) provides theoretically grounded feature attributions for any model, based on Shapley values from cooperative game theory.

**Shapley value** for feature $j$ in prediction for input $\mathbf{x}$:

ϕj=∑S⊆F∖{j}∣S∣!(∣F∣−∣S∣−1)!∣F∣![fS(x)−fS∖{j}(x)]\phi_j = \sum$\subseteq F \setminus \\{j\\}} \frac{|S|!(|F|-|S|-1)!}{|F|!}\left[f_S(\mathbf{x}) - f$ \setminus \\{j\\}}(\mathbf{x})\right]ϕj=S⊆F∖{j}∑∣F∣!∣S∣!(∣F∣−∣S∣−1)![fS(x)−fS∖{j}(x)]

where $F$ is the set of all features, and fS(x)f_S(\mathbf{x})fS(x) is the model's prediction using only features in $S$.

In plain language: the Shapley value for feature $j$ is the average marginal contribution of feature $j$ across all possible subsets of features — how much does adding feature $j$ to any group of other features improve the prediction? It satisfies desirable axioms: efficiency (attributions sum to prediction), symmetry (features with identical contributions get equal attribution), dummy (unused features get zero attribution), and linearity.

**TreeSHAP** computes exact Shapley values for tree-based models in O(TLD2)O(TLD^2)O(TLD2) time (where $T$ is number of trees, $L$ leaves, $D$ depth) — tractable even for large forests.

**SHAP applications:**

* **Feature importance (global):** Average ∣ϕj∣|\phi_j|∣ϕj∣ across all training examples ranks features by global importance
* **Individual explanation:** The SHAP values for a single prediction show which features pushed the prediction up or down relative to the average

#### LIME: Local Interpretable Model-Agnostic Explanations

**LIME** (Ribeiro et al., 2016) generates local explanations by fitting a simple interpretable model (linear or decision tree) in the neighborhood of a specific prediction:

  1. Perturb the input $\mathbf{x}$ randomly: generate $N$ nearby samples {$\\{\mathbf{x}'_1, \ldots, \mathbf{x}'_N\\}{$
  2. Query the black-box model: get predictions {$\\{f(\mathbf{x}'_1), \ldots, f(\mathbf{x}'_N)\\}{$
  3. Weight samples by proximity to $\mathbf{x}$
  4. Fit a linear model on the weighted samples: g(x′)=∑jwjxj′g(\mathbf{x}') = \sum_j w_j x'_jg(x′)=∑jwjxj′
  5. The coefficients {wj}\\{w_j\\}{wj} are the local explanation

**Limitation:** LIME explanations depend on the neighborhood sampling strategy and are not guaranteed to be consistent across multiple runs. SHAP is generally preferred when exact computation is feasible.

#### Attention Is Not Explanation

Transformers (Chapter 20) produce attention weights that have been widely used as "explanations" — the model attended to these words, so they are important. This is largely misleading. Jain & Wallace (2019) showed that attention weights can be arbitrarily changed without affecting the model's output (because the output depends on the attended values, not just the weights). Attention shows which representations are combined, not which inputs causally determine the output.

### 25.5 Privacy and Data Ethics

#### Data Consent and Usage

ML models are trained on data collected from humans. This raises questions that are often legally, not just ethically, regulated:

* Were users informed their data would be used to train AI systems?
* Can users opt out, or have their data deleted from trained models?
* Do the data's terms of service permit model training?

Copyright and consent issues have become central to large-scale foundation model training (web scraping without explicit consent, training on artists' work without compensation).

#### Differential Privacy

**Differential privacy (DP)** provides a formal, mathematically rigorous guarantee that a model's output reveals very little about any individual training example.

**Definition (Dwork, 2006):** A randomized mechanism $M$ is (ε,δ)(\varepsilon, \delta)(ε,δ)-differentially private if for any two datasets $D$ and D′D'D′ differing in a single record, and any output set $S$:

$P(M(D) \in S) \leq e^\varepsilon \cdot P(M(D') \in S) + \delta$

In plain language: an adversary who sees the model's output cannot determine (with confidence better than eεe^\varepsiloneε) whether any specific individual was in the training data.

**DP-SGD:** Implements differential privacy in deep learning by:

  1. Clipping per-example gradients to a maximum norm $C$
  2. Adding Gaussian noise $\mathcal{N}(0, \sigma^2 C^2 \mathbf{I})$ to each batch's gradient

**Privacy-accuracy tradeoff:** Stronger privacy (smaller ε\varepsilonε) requires more noise, degrading model accuracy. The practical implications: with $\varepsilon = 1$ (strong privacy), accuracy drops significantly. With $\varepsilon = 10$ (weaker privacy), accuracy is near-normal. Finding the right tradeoff depends on the sensitivity of the data and the harm from privacy violations.

#### Membership Inference Attacks

Without formal privacy guarantees, models can leak information about their training data. In a **membership inference attack** , an adversary queries the model and determines whether a specific individual was in the training set — a privacy violation if the training set is sensitive (e.g., medical records).

The mechanism: models often exhibit higher confidence on training examples than test examples. The attack trains a binary classifier on (model confidence, correct/incorrect) features to distinguish members from non-members.

### 25.6 Robustness and Distribution Shift

#### Distribution Shift

A model trained on distribution Ptrain(x,y)P_\text{train}(\mathbf{x}, y)Ptrain(x,y) and deployed on distribution Pdeploy(x,y)≠PtrainP_\text{deploy}(\mathbf{x}, y) \neq P_\text{train}Pdeploy(x,y)=Ptrain may fail silently.

**Types of shift:**

* **Covariate shift:** P(x)P(\mathbf{x})P(x) changes, $P(y|\mathbf{x})$ is the same (input distribution shifts)
* **Label shift:** $P(y)$ changes, $P(\mathbf{x}|y)$ is the same (class proportions change)
* **Concept drift:** $P(y|\mathbf{x})$ changes (the relationship between inputs and outputs changes over time)

**Real-world examples:**

* COVID-19 caused concept drift for every model trained on pre-pandemic health data: symptom-diagnosis relationships changed, hospital utilization patterns changed
* An ML fraud detection model trained before a new payment type was introduced may classify all new-type transactions as anomalous
* A hiring model trained on 2010 resumes and deployed in 2025 may penalize skills that didn't exist in 2010 (cloud computing, ML frameworks)

#### Adversarial Robustness

**Adversarial examples** (Szegedy et al., 2013; Goodfellow et al., 2014): small, often imperceptible perturbations to an input that cause a model to make incorrect predictions with high confidence.

Example: adding carefully crafted pixel-level noise to an image of a panda causes ResNet to classify it as a gibbon with 99% confidence, while humans see only a panda.

**Why this matters:** Adversarial examples are not academic toys. Self-driving car systems using computer vision can be fooled by stickers placed on stop signs. Medical imaging classifiers can be attacked by perturbing the imaging process. Physical adversarial patches can fool object detectors in the real world.

**Defenses:**

* **Adversarial training:** Include adversarial examples in training data. Effective but computationally expensive (must generate adversarial examples at each training step).
* **Certified robustness:** Use mathematical proofs to guarantee that no perturbation within a radius ϵ\epsilonϵ can change the prediction. Achievable with randomized smoothing (add Gaussian noise to the input, take majority vote of perturbed predictions) but at significant accuracy cost.

### 25.7 Responsible ML in Practice

#### Model Cards

**Model cards** (Mitchell et al., 2019) are standardized documentation for ML models, analogous to nutrition labels for food:

* Intended use and out-of-scope uses
* Performance metrics disaggregated by subgroup
* Limitations and known failure modes
* Evaluation data and training data description

Model cards are now standard at Google, Hugging Face, and many organizations; required by some regulatory frameworks.

#### Datasheets for Datasets

Similarly, **datasheets for datasets** (Gebru et al., 2021) document the provenance, collection process, intended uses, and known limitations of training datasets. A model can only be responsibly deployed if its training data is understood.

#### The ML Deployment Checklist

Before deploying a model that affects people:

  1. **Who is affected?** Map all stakeholders, including those not in the training data.
  2. **What could go wrong?** Failure mode analysis, adversarial use cases, edge cases.
  3. **Disaggregate evaluation.** Measure performance on every relevant subgroup.
  4. **Audit for bias.** Apply fairness metrics; mitigate identified biases.
  5. **Establish a feedback loop.** How will errors be detected and corrected post-deployment?
  6. **Set performance thresholds.** Below what accuracy/fairness level should the model not be deployed?
  7. **Plan for distribution shift.** How will the model be monitored and retrained?
  8. **Determine accountability.** Who is responsible for harms caused by model failures?

### 25.8 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** A recidivism prediction model has 70% accuracy for both Black and White defendants. A critic argues the model is still biased. What additional statistics would you need to evaluate, and what bias might they reveal?

**Answer:** Overall accuracy being equal across groups is insufficient to establish fairness. We need:

**False positive rate (FPR):** Among defendants who do NOT reoffend, what fraction does the model incorrectly predict as high-risk? If FPR(Black) > FPR(White), then the model incorrectly labels more innocent Black defendants as risky — they are denied bail or given harsher sentences even though they would not reoffend. This is exactly what the ProPublica analysis found in COMPAS.

**False negative rate (FNR):** Among defendants who DO reoffend, what fraction does the model miss? If FNR(White) > FNR(Black), the model under-predicts risk for White defendants who do reoffend.

**Precision by group:** What fraction of defendants labeled high-risk actually reoffend? Lower precision for one group means more unjustified interventions for that group.

Equal accuracy is consistent with systematically higher false positive rates for one group. The accuracy paradox: if one group has a lower base rate of reoffending, a classifier that predicts "low risk" for everyone in that group achieves high accuracy by construction.

**Q2.** A facial recognition system achieves 99% accuracy overall. A journalist reports that for women with darker skin tones, the false positive rate is 35% (incorrectly identifying the person as someone else), versus 1% for lighter-skinned men. The company responds: "Our overall accuracy is 99%, well above the industry standard." Explain the flaw in this response, and identify which fairness metric the journalist is pointing to.

**Answer:** The company's response is an example of the **accuracy paradox** — high aggregate accuracy can coexist with severe disparate error rates across subgroups. If the test population contains mostly lighter-skinned men, overall accuracy can be 99% even while the system fails severely for women with darker skin tones.

The journalist is pointing to **equalized odds** (specifically, equal false positive rates across demographic groups). A false positive in facial recognition means incorrectly matching a person to someone else's identity — in law enforcement or security contexts, this can lead to wrongful arrest or denial of access. A 35% false positive rate for one demographic versus 1% for another means women with darker skin tones are 35× more likely to be misidentified. This is a serious, quantifiable harm that aggregate accuracy completely obscures.

The appropriate response is **disaggregated evaluation** : report accuracy, false positive rate, and false negative rate separately for each demographic group. If equalized false positive rates are required, the model should be audited and either retrained with more representative data, post-processed with group-specific thresholds, or not deployed in high-stakes settings until the disparity is resolved.

**Q3.** You compute SHAP values for a loan model and find that "zip code" has a high positive SHAP value for some predictions. Should you remove zip code as a feature? Discuss the tradeoffs.

**Answer:** The decision is not straightforward:

**Arguments for removal:** Zip code is a proxy for race and neighborhood demographics due to historical redlining and residential segregation. A model using zip code may achieve equal accuracy statistics across race while still using a feature that correlates with race. In jurisdictions where lending discrimination by proxy characteristics is illegal (US Equal Credit Opportunity Act), zip code may be a legally impermissible feature even if it predicts well.

**Arguments against removal:** Zip code may capture legitimate credit-relevant variation (local economic conditions, property values) that is not captured by individual financial features. Removing it reduces accuracy and may actually harm fairness if it leads to more false positives overall.

**Resolution:** The right test is **counterfactual** : if two applicants are identical in every financial characteristic but differ only in zip code, do they receive different predictions? If yes, and if zip code is a proxy for race, this may constitute disparate impact. A better approach than removing zip code: test for disparate impact directly by measuring prediction differences across race after controlling for all legitimate financial features. If zip code creates disparate impact beyond what is justified by legitimate credit risk, remove it.

**Q4.** Explain the difference between covariate shift and concept drift. Give one real-world example of each and describe how you would detect each type of shift in a deployed model.

**Answer:**

**Covariate shift:** The input distribution P(x)P(\mathbf{x})P(x) changes at deployment time, but the relationship between inputs and outputs $P(y|\mathbf{x})$ stays the same. Example: a fraud detection model trained on 2020 transaction data is deployed in 2025; transaction amounts and merchant categories have shifted (people shop differently) but the definition of what makes a transaction fraudulent is the same.

**Detection:** Monitor the input feature distribution over time. Use statistical tests (KS test for numeric features, chi-squared for categorical) to compare the live input distribution to the training distribution. Significant drift triggers a data collection effort to retrain on more recent data.

**Concept drift:** The relationship $P(y|\mathbf{x})$ changes — the same inputs now map to different outputs. Example: a model predicting hospital length-of-stay was trained pre-COVID; after March 2020, the same symptoms, age, and comorbidities predict dramatically longer stays due to new treatment protocols. The features haven't changed, but the label-generating process has.

**Detection:** Monitor prediction accuracy on a labeled holdout stream. If accuracy degrades despite stable input features, concept drift is likely. Requires ongoing ground truth collection (observing actual outcomes) to compare against predictions.

**Q5.** Differential privacy (DP) for machine learning involves clipping per-example gradients and adding Gaussian noise. Explain why gradient clipping is a necessary first step before adding noise, and what would happen if noise were added to unclipped gradients.

**Answer:** Gradient clipping bounds the **sensitivity** of the gradient — the maximum amount the gradient can change if a single training example is added or removed. Without clipping, gradients have unbounded sensitivity: a single outlier example could contribute an arbitrarily large gradient, and the amount of noise required to mask its contribution would also need to be arbitrarily large.

The DP guarantee requires that the noise scale σ\sigmaσ be proportional to sensitivity $C$ (the clip norm): adding $\mathcal{N}(0, \sigma^2 C^2 \mathbf{I})$ noise provides (ε,δ)(\varepsilon, \delta)(ε,δ)-DP because each example's contribution is bounded to norm $C$, and the noise overwhelms any individual's specific gradient signal.

Without clipping: sensitivity is unbounded, so no finite amount of noise provides DP. Equivalently, the added noise would need to be so large (to cover the worst-case gradient magnitude) that it would completely destroy the learning signal — the model could not be trained usefully. Clipping is what makes the privacy-accuracy tradeoff manageable.

#### Application Problems

**Q1.** You're building a hiring model for a tech company. The historical training data shows that male applicants were 3× more likely to be hired than female applicants at the same qualification level (due to past bias in hiring managers). Propose a complete fairness-aware ML pipeline from data collection to deployment.

**Answer:**

**Data collection and preprocessing:**

* Audit the training labels: identify whether historical hire/no-hire decisions reflect qualification differences or hiring manager bias. If possible, have HR re-evaluate a sample of past rejections blindly.
* Collect new evaluation rubrics: replace binary hire/no-hire labels with standardized technical assessment scores (coding test, structured interview rubric) that reduce individual evaluator bias.
* Document the bias: create a datasheet noting the 3× disparity and its probable causes.

**Feature engineering:**

* Exclude name, gender, pronouns, and any direct gender proxies from features
* Audit indirect proxies: certain colleges, sports, or extracurricular activities may correlate with gender — evaluate and consider excluding or controlling for these
* Focus on assessable skills: coding test scores, portfolio projects, relevant experience

**Model training:**

* Use equalized odds as the fairness constraint (equal TPR and FPR across gender groups)
* Apply adversarial debiasing: jointly train the classifier and a gender predictor; penalize the classifier for making predictions from which gender can be inferred
* If using historical data at all: reweight examples to reduce the influence of historically biased hiring decisions

**Evaluation:**

* Evaluate accuracy, FPR, FNR, precision separately for each gender group
* Measure intersectional performance: women of color, women with non-traditional backgrounds
* Conduct A/B testing: compare outcomes under the new model vs. human reviewers

**Deployment and monitoring:**

* Require human review for all model recommendations (model assists, doesn't decide)
* Track hiring rates by demographic group quarterly; set alert thresholds for disparities
* Establish an appeal process for rejected candidates
* Annual audit by an independent third party

#### Think Deeper

**Q1.** "Fairness through unawareness" — removing protected attributes (race, gender) from the model — is a naive fairness intervention. Explain why it typically fails, using the concept of proxy variables. Then discuss whether the alternative (using protected attributes explicitly for fairness adjustments) raises its own ethical concerns.

**Discussion:**

**Why unawareness fails:** Modern ML models are excellent at recovering predictive signals from correlated features. Even after removing race and gender from the feature set, many remaining features are correlated with these attributes due to systemic inequalities:

* Zip code → race (residential segregation)
* College name → socioeconomic status and race
* Employment gaps → often pregnancy/caregiving leave → gender
* Credit history depth → age and immigrant status

The model learns these correlations and effectively uses the protected attribute through proxies. Studies have shown that models nominally blind to race can be nearly as predictive of race from remaining features as the explicit attribute itself.

**Using protected attributes for fairness:** The alternative — explicitly include the protected attribute and use it in post-processing (threshold adjustment) or in-processing (fairness constraints) — is more effective at achieving fairness by most definitions, but raises concerns:

  1. **Disparate treatment:** In many legal systems, explicitly considering race or gender in decisions (even to benefit the disadvantaged group) constitutes disparate treatment — which may be illegal even if it reduces disparate impact. This creates a legal tension.

  2. **Paternalism and stigma:** Separate treatment, even when beneficial, can stigmatize groups or be perceived as patronizing.

  3. **Which definition?** Explicitly optimizing for one fairness definition (demographic parity) may worsen others (equalized odds). The choice requires value judgments.

  4. **Gaming:** If candidates know the model adjusts for their group membership, behavior may change in ways that undermine the adjustment.

**Conclusion:** There is no purely technical solution to bias. "Fairness through unawareness" is inadequate. Explicit fairness interventions are more effective but involve legal and ethical tradeoffs that require human judgment, stakeholder input, and societal debate — not just algorithmic tuning.

**Q2.** A colleague proposes defending a medical imaging classifier against adversarial examples by using adversarial training: generate adversarial examples at each training step and include them in the mini-batch. Discuss (a) why this works as a defense, (b) one significant practical drawback, and (c) an alternative defense that provides stronger formal guarantees.

**Discussion:**

**(a) Why adversarial training works:** The model is explicitly exposed to the types of perturbations an adversary would use during training, forcing it to learn decision boundaries that are robust to small input perturbations. Intuitively, the loss landscape is "smoothed" around training examples so that moving slightly in any direction does not suddenly cross a decision boundary. Goodfellow et al.'s FGSM adversarial training showed that a single gradient-based perturbation step (Fast Gradient Sign Method) at each batch is enough to substantially improve robustness.

**(b) Practical drawback:** Adversarial training is computationally expensive — generating adversarial examples at every training step roughly doubles or triples the compute budget, since each step requires an additional forward and backward pass to compute the adversarial perturbation. More importantly, adversarial training with one attack type (e.g., ℓ∞ FGSM) does not generalize to all attack types: a model adversarially trained against ℓ∞ perturbations may still be vulnerable to ℓ2 or ℓ1 attacks. It is an empirical defense, not a formal guarantee — stronger, adaptive attacks may still succeed.

**(c) Certified robustness:** Randomized smoothing (Cohen et al., 2019) provides a formal guarantee: given a base classifier $f$ and a smoothed classifier g(x)=argmaxcP(f(x+ϵ)=c)g(\mathbf{x}) = \text{argmax}_c P(f(\mathbf{x} + \epsilon) = c)g(x)=argmaxcP(f(x+ϵ)=c) where $\epsilon \sim \mathcal{N}(0, \sigma^2 \mathbf{I})$, if the top class probability exceeds a threshold, the prediction is certified to be stable for all perturbations within a provable ℓ2 radius. No adversarial example within that radius can change the prediction. The tradeoff: adding Gaussian noise during inference lowers accuracy on clean inputs, and the certified radius may be smaller than the perturbations encountered in practice.

### Chapter Summary

Building responsible ML systems requires addressing technical and ethical dimensions together.

Key ideas:

* **Bias sources** : representation gaps in training data, historical bias in labels, feedback loops that amplify existing inequalities
* **Fairness definitions** : demographic parity, equalized odds, calibration — mutually incompatible under different base rates (impossibility theorem)
* **Mitigation** : pre-processing (reweighting/augmentation), in-processing (fairness constraints, adversarial debiasing), post-processing (threshold adjustment)
* **Interpretability** : SHAP (Shapley values, axiomatic attribution), LIME (local linear approximation), model cards
* **Privacy** : differential privacy provides formal guarantees; membership inference attacks exploit model memorization
* **Robustness** : distribution shift degrades performance silently; adversarial examples exploit input sensitivity
* **Deployment responsibility** : disaggregated evaluation, model cards, datasheets, feedback loops, accountability

**Connection forward:** Chapter 26 looks ahead to where ML is heading — foundation models, multimodal AI, AI safety, and the open questions that remain at the frontier of the field.

---

## Chapter 26: The Future — Foundation Models, Multimodal AI, and Open Questions

We have traveled a long distance. We began with the question "can a machine learn from data?" and answered it with linear regression, gradient descent, and the squared loss. We built upward through decision trees, neural networks, backpropagation, convolutional networks, recurrent networks, transformers, and language models. Along the way, two concrete projects — predicting house prices and classifying movie sentiment — grounded abstract theory in measurable results, growing from 73% R² and 85% accuracy to 87% R² and 96% accuracy as our tools became more powerful.

This final chapter steps back from individual algorithms to survey the landscape: where are we, what changed everything, what remains unsolved, and what kind of practitioners and thinkers the field needs.

### 26.1 The Paradigm Shift: From Task-Specific to Foundation Models

For most of machine learning's history, the workflow was clear: define a task, collect labeled data for that task, train a model on that data, evaluate on a held-out test set. One task, one model, one dataset. The CIFAR-10 image classifier knows nothing about text. The IMDb sentiment model knows nothing about images.

#### What Changed

Three things converged:

  1. **Scale** : models with hundreds of billions of parameters trained on trillions of tokens of text
  2. **Self-supervised pretraining** : eliminating the need for task-specific labeled data
  3. **Emergent capabilities** : behaviors that appear qualitatively at scale, not present in smaller models

The result: **foundation models** (Bommasani et al., 2021) — large models pretrained on broad data that can be adapted (fine-tuned, prompted, or used zero-shot) for a vast range of downstream tasks.

GPT-4, Claude, Gemini, and their successors are not task-specific systems. They can write code, explain scientific concepts, translate languages, analyze images, reason through logic puzzles, and draft legal documents — from a single set of weights. This is categorically different from anything that came before.

#### Why Foundation Models Are Powerful

**Transfer learning at scale:** Every concept learned during pretraining is available for every downstream task. The model doesn't start from scratch on any new problem — it starts from a rich, compressed representation of all text (and increasingly, all modalities) it has seen.

**In-context learning:** Large language models can learn new tasks from examples in the prompt, without any gradient updates. Provide three examples of a translation task; the model generalizes to new examples. The mechanism is not fully understood — it appears the model is performing implicit Bayesian inference over programs, using the examples to infer the task and apply it.

**Emergent capabilities:** Abilities like chain-of-thought reasoning, multi-step code debugging, and analogy completion appear at scale but not in smaller models. The emergence is not merely quantitative improvement — qualitatively new capabilities appear as model size crosses certain thresholds.

### 26.2 Multimodal AI

Language is just one modality. The world consists of images, audio, video, 3D structures, time series, and the interactions between them.

#### Vision-Language Models

**CLIP (Contrastive Language-Image Pretraining, OpenAI 2021)** jointly trains a vision encoder and a text encoder to align images and their descriptions in a shared embedding space. The training objective: given a batch of (image, text) pairs, maximize the cosine similarity of matching pairs and minimize it for non-matching pairs.

CLIP learns visual representations that are semantically meaningful in the language sense — the image embedding of "a dog on a surfboard" is near the text embedding of that phrase. This enables zero-shot classification: classify an image by comparing its embedding to the embeddings of class descriptions.

**GPT-4V, Claude, Gemini** extend language models to accept images as input. The image is encoded by a vision encoder (CLIP-style), the resulting visual tokens are prepended to the text context, and the autoregressive language model processes both together. These models can describe images, answer questions about them, read text in images, and reason about visual relationships.

#### Beyond Images

**Audio:** Whisper (OpenAI) achieves near-human speech recognition across 99 languages by training on 680,000 hours of web audio. AudioLM (Google) models raw audio as a sequence prediction task, generating coherent speech and music.

**Video:** Sora (OpenAI, 2024) generates videos from text prompts using a diffusion transformer architecture. Understanding and generating video requires spatial and temporal consistency across frames — a qualitative step beyond image generation.

**Protein structure:** AlphaFold2 (DeepMind, 2021) predicts protein 3D structure from amino acid sequence with near-experimental accuracy. By framing structure prediction as a sequence-to-structure translation problem and leveraging attention over multiple sequence alignments, AlphaFold2 effectively solved a 50-year-old grand challenge in biology. ESM3 (2024) goes further, generating novel protein sequences with desired structural properties — using language model pretraining on protein sequences.

**Molecules and materials:** Graph neural networks represent molecular structures as graphs; transformers over SMILES strings predict properties or generate candidate drug molecules. Foundation models for chemistry may accelerate drug discovery timescales from decades to years.

### 26.3 AI Safety and Alignment

As AI systems become more capable, questions about their alignment with human values become more urgent.

#### The Alignment Problem

A system that maximizes a reward function defined by humans will maximize that reward — including in ways that violate human intent if the reward function is even slightly misspecified. The challenge of ensuring that AI systems reliably do what humans want is called the **alignment problem**.

**Near-term alignment (RLHF):** As we saw in Chapter 21, reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) trains language models to produce outputs that human raters prefer. This improves alignment significantly but introduces new issues:

* Reward hacking: models learn to produce plausible-sounding outputs, not accurate ones
* Evaluator limitations: human raters can't verify technical claims in specialized domains
* Distributional shift: RLHF improves alignment on the training distribution; novel situations may still produce misaligned behavior

**Constitutional AI (Anthropic, 2022):** Instead of (or in addition to) human feedback, specify a set of principles (a "constitution") and train the model to critique and revise its own outputs against those principles. Reduces reliance on human labeling at scale; makes the alignment criteria explicit and auditable.

**Scalable oversight:** How do we supervise systems that are more capable than their supervisors? If GPT-5 can produce arguments that even experts cannot evaluate for correctness, how do human raters provide useful feedback? Approaches include:

* **Debate:** Two AI systems debate; a human judges who makes the better argument. Assumes good arguments are easier to evaluate than to generate.
* **Recursive reward modeling:** Humans evaluate AI assistants on subtasks that are individually evaluable; the AI learns to decompose hard problems into evaluable subtasks.
* **Interpretability tools:** If we can understand what a model "knows" and "intends," we can verify alignment without relying solely on behavioral tests.

#### Interpretability Research

**Mechanistic interpretability** (Anthropic, DeepMind, others) aims to reverse-engineer neural networks: identify which circuits implement which computations, which neurons represent which concepts.

**Superposition hypothesis:** Models represent more features than they have dimensions by encoding multiple features in the same neurons in a non-orthogonal fashion. "Polysemantic neurons" respond to multiple semantically unrelated concepts. This makes identifying what a neuron "does" difficult.

**Circuits:** Specific collections of neurons and attention heads implement specific algorithms. Induction heads (identified in small transformers) implement a general "copy the token that followed this context last time" algorithm — a primitive used in in-context learning. Identifying these circuits provides a path toward verifying model behavior.

The goal of mechanistic interpretability is ambitious: a complete understanding of how a large language model works, neuron by neuron. We are far from that goal, but the work is advancing rapidly.

### 26.4 Open Problems

Despite remarkable progress, fundamental questions remain open.

#### The Sample Efficiency Gap

Humans learn to recognize cats from seeing perhaps a dozen cats. GPT-4 trained on trillions of tokens. This gap is orders of magnitude. Deep learning is extraordinarily **sample-inefficient** compared to biological learning. We do not know why biological learning is so efficient or how to replicate it.

Partial approaches: meta-learning ("learn to learn"), neural program synthesis, compositional generalization (learning rules that compose rather than memorizing examples). None has closed the gap.

#### Reasoning and Compositionality

Language models exhibit impressive but brittle reasoning. They can solve novel math problems but fail on slight variations. They can generate syntactically plausible code that contains semantic errors. The question of whether current transformers can perform genuine **systematic generalization** — applying rules compositionally to new combinations of familiar concepts — remains debated.

**SCAN, COGS, and compositional benchmarks** have repeatedly shown that standard transformers fail to generalize compositionally (applying learned rules to new compositions). Chain-of-thought prompting substantially improves reasoning on many tasks, but the mechanism is unclear: is the model doing computation in the chain-of-thought, or is it pattern-matching to chain-of-thought reasoning patterns in the training data?

#### World Models and Causal Reasoning

Language models are trained to predict tokens. Does predicting tokens require learning a causal model of the world, or is it sufficient to learn surface statistical patterns?

**Evidence for world models:** LLMs perform well on causal reasoning questions, physical intuition tasks, and counterfactual scenarios — suggesting some internal world model.

**Evidence against:** LLMs fail systematically on out-of-distribution causal queries, confuse correlation and causation, and show susceptibility to simple rephrasing of identical causal problems.

**The bitter lesson** (Sutton, 2019): historically, approaches that rely on human-designed structure (feature engineering, knowledge graphs, causal graphs) have been surpassed by scale + compute. This suggests learning causal representations directly from data may be more effective than engineering them in — but we don't yet know how to guarantee it.

#### Hallucination and Reliability

Large language models hallucinate: they produce fluent, confident assertions that are factually wrong. This is a fundamental consequence of the training objective: the model is trained to produce plausible continuations, not verified facts. Reducing hallucination requires either grounding (retrieval-augmented generation — fetch relevant facts before generating), verifiability (chain-of-thought with explicit steps that can be checked), or uncertainty quantification (the model should express appropriate confidence in its claims).

None of these is solved. Hallucination rates have decreased with scale and RLHF, but not to zero — and for high-stakes domains (medicine, law, safety-critical engineering), even rare hallucinations are unacceptable.

### 26.5 What Does This Mean for You?

#### The Practitioner's Perspective

The tools described in this book are now accessible to anyone. Pre-trained models on Hugging Face, fine-tuned with a few hundred examples, can solve NLP tasks that required custom research models five years ago. XGBoost achieves competitive results on tabular data with minimal feature engineering. Stable Diffusion generates photorealistic images on a laptop.

The barrier has shifted from "can this be done?" to "is this the right thing to build?" and "how do we deploy it responsibly?" Technical expertise in architecture design and training is still valuable, but the highest-leverage skills increasingly include:

* Domain expertise to formulate the right problem
* Evaluation design to measure what actually matters
* Ethical reasoning to anticipate and mitigate harms
* Systems thinking to understand deployment context

#### The Researcher's Perspective

The open problems outlined above — sample efficiency, compositionality, causal reasoning, alignment, interpretability — are genuinely hard and genuinely important. Progress on them will require new ideas, not just scaling current architectures.

The most important result in the history of machine learning is probably the **universal approximation theorem** \+ **gradient descent** working better than anyone expected — empirical success driving theoretical understanding, not the reverse. This pattern will likely continue: insights come from taking systems seriously, measuring them carefully, and being willing to revise foundational assumptions.

#### The Human Perspective

AI systems are tools made by humans, trained on human knowledge, deployed in human contexts, affecting human lives. The decisions about what to build, how to train it, what constraints to impose, and who bears the costs of its failures are human decisions. They are not made by algorithms.

The most important skill for anyone working with AI is the willingness to ask: who does this help? Who does this harm? What values does this encode? Is this worth building?

### 26.6 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** CLIP learns a shared embedding space for images and text. Explain how zero-shot image classification works using CLIP, without any labeled training examples for the target classes.

**Answer:** CLIP has a vision encoder and a text encoder trained so that (image, caption) pairs have high cosine similarity and (image, unrelated caption) pairs have low cosine similarity.

For zero-shot classification with classes {$\\{c_1, \ldots, c_K\\}{$:

  1. Embed each class label as text: tk=TextEncoder("a photo of a ck")\mathbf{t}_k = \text{TextEncoder}(\text{"a photo of a } c_k\text{"})tk=TextEncoder("a photo of a ck")
  2. Embed the query image: $\mathbf{v} = \text{VisionEncoder}(\mathbf{x})$
  3. Predict: y^=arg⁡max⁡kcos⁡(v,tk)\hat{y} = \arg\max_k \cos(\mathbf{v}, \mathbf{t}_k)y^=argmaxkcos(v,tk)

No labeled examples are needed because the joint training on 400M image-caption pairs created a shared space where visual and linguistic representations of the same concept are aligned. "a photo of a dog" in text space is near an image of a dog in vision space — this alignment was learned from captioned web images, not from class labels.

**Q2.** Explain why hallucination is a structural consequence of the language model training objective, not a fixable bug.

**Answer:** Language models are trained to maximize P(xt+1∣x1,…,xt)P(x_{t+1} | x_1, \ldots, x_t)P(xt+1∣x1,…,xt) — predict the next token given the context. The optimal strategy under this objective is to produce the most plausible continuation, given all patterns seen in training.

"Most plausible" ≠ "factually correct." For a given context, many factually false continuations are highly plausible because they match patterns of how text is written, regardless of their truth. "The Eiffel Tower was built in [year]" in text tends to be followed by a specific year, and the model will produce _a_ year — it has no mechanism for distinguishing "I know the year" from "a year sounds right here."

The model is trained on human-generated text, which sometimes contains errors. It has no access to a ground truth oracle. It has no internal representation of "I don't know this" separate from "this sounds unlikely." Hallucination is not a bug in the sense of a correctable implementation error — it is the behavior predicted by the training objective in knowledge-limited scenarios.

Partial mitigations: retrieval augmentation (ground generation in retrieved facts), uncertainty quantification (calibrate confidence), and training with explicit reward for accuracy (RLHF toward correctness). These reduce but do not eliminate hallucination.

**Q3.** AlphaFold2 predicts protein 3D structure from amino acid sequence with near-experimental accuracy, solving a problem that had resisted 50 years of effort. Identify which architectural components from this textbook contributed to AlphaFold2's design, and explain what specific challenge each component addresses in the protein structure prediction problem.

**Answer:** AlphaFold2 draws on several deep learning components covered in this book:

**Transformer / attention (Chapter 20):** The Evoformer module uses multi-head attention over both the amino acid sequence and a multiple sequence alignment (MSA) of related proteins across species. Self-attention allows every residue to directly attend to every other residue, regardless of distance in the sequence — critical because residues far apart in the chain may be physically adjacent in the folded structure (long-range contacts). Standard RNNs could not model these long-range dependencies efficiently.

**Residual connections (Chapter 18):** AlphaFold2 stacks 48 Evoformer blocks, and like ResNets, each block uses residual connections. Without them, gradients would vanish across 48 layers and training would fail. Residual connections allow the deep architecture to train stably.

**Equivariant structure modules:** The final structure module produces 3D coordinates that are equivariant to rotation and translation — applying a rotation to the input should rotate the output coordinates accordingly. This is a domain-specific architectural constraint (not covered in depth in this book) that encodes physical symmetry of protein structure.

**Transfer learning principles (Chapter 21):** AlphaFold2 is pre-trained on the entire Protein Data Bank (~200,000 structures) and uses evolutionary information from billions of protein sequences. This is transfer learning at scale: knowledge from distantly related proteins transfers to improve prediction of the target protein.

The overall lesson: AlphaFold2's success came from combining domain-specific biological knowledge (evolutionary co-variation, structural constraints) with general-purpose deep learning components (attention, residual connections, large-scale pre-training) — consistent with the book's theme that good inductive biases drive progress.

**Q4.** The chapter describes "emergent capabilities" in large language models — qualitative new abilities that appear suddenly at scale. Give two examples of emergent capabilities and explain why their sudden appearance at certain scales is surprising and scientifically important.

**Answer:**

**Example 1 — Multi-step arithmetic:** Small language models (< 1B parameters) are essentially unable to correctly compute multi-digit multiplication even with standard prompting. Above roughly 10B parameters, models begin to reliably solve these problems. This is surprising because the training objective (next-token prediction on text) contains very few examples of explicit multi-step arithmetic — the capability appears to emerge from general pattern learning at scale, not from arithmetic-specific training.

**Example 2 — Chain-of-thought reasoning:** Below ~100B parameters, prompting a model with "Let's think step by step" produces no improvement on reasoning tasks. Above this scale, the same prompt triggers coherent multi-step reasoning that dramatically improves accuracy. The sudden usefulness of a simple phrase shift suggests a qualitative change in how the model internally represents and processes problems.

**Why scientifically important:** The scaling laws (Chapter 21) predict smooth, predictable improvement in loss as scale increases. Emergent capabilities violate this prediction: performance on specific tasks can be near-zero up to a threshold, then jump rapidly. This means:

  1. Evaluation benchmarks can give misleading signals — a model may appear to have no capability until scale suddenly enables it.
  2. Emergent capabilities cannot be predicted from small-scale experiments, complicating safety research (you cannot test for dangerous capabilities at small scale if they only emerge at large scale).
  3. The mechanism of emergence is not understood — it is an open scientific question whether emergence reflects genuine phase transitions in model internals or is an artifact of how capabilities are measured.

#### Think Deeper

**Q1.** The "bitter lesson" argues that methods leveraging computation and learning beat methods leveraging human-designed structure. But modern LLMs incorporate enormous amounts of human-designed structure: transformer architecture, tokenization, positional encoding, RLHF, system prompts. How do you reconcile this with the bitter lesson?

**Discussion:**

The bitter lesson's actual claim is more precise: methods that are designed to exploit computation (by learning from data) outperform methods that require specifying problem-specific knowledge in advance. The lesson is about the source of structure, not its presence.

Modern LLMs use human-designed **architectural primitives** (attention, residual connections, layer normalization) but these are general-purpose tools that make computation efficient, not domain-specific knowledge. By contrast, the approaches the bitter lesson critiques — hand-crafted chess evaluation functions, expert knowledge bases for NLP, feature engineering — encode domain expertise that only applies to that domain.

The reconciliation: the bitter lesson applies to the _content_ of representations (learn from data, not hand-craft) but not necessarily to the _mechanism_ of learning (architectural innovations that improve how data is processed are legitimate). Transformers, residual connections, and attention are architectural innovations that improve learning efficiency; they don't encode facts about language or images.

Where the bitter lesson's limits may lie: architecture design is itself a form of inductive bias. The choice of attention mechanism encodes assumptions about what kinds of relationships matter (pairwise, position-independent similarities). RLHF encodes human values through reward signals. As systems become more capable, the inductive biases embedded in architecture choices may themselves become limiting — and we may find that the most important progress comes from learning architecture structures themselves (neural architecture search, hypernetworks, self-organizing systems).

**Q2.** Reflect on the two threads that ran through this book: the house price prediction thread and the sentiment analysis thread. Both improved dramatically as we applied more powerful models. But what costs came with those improvements, and what questions does this raise about when "better" models are actually better in practice?

**Discussion:**

**Accuracy vs. other values:**

The house price model improved from RMSE $34,500 (linear regression) to $21,200 (feature-engineered XGBoost), a 38% error reduction. But along the way:

* The linear model's coefficients were interpretable (warea=127w_\text{area} = 127warea=127: each additional square foot adds 127 USD to price). The XGBoost model's predictions are explainable only post-hoc with SHAP — not by direct inspection.
* The feature engineering in Chapter 22 required domain expertise (why log-transform distance? why interaction features?). Deploying this in production requires documentation of these choices.
* XGBoost trained on 2020 data may fail for properties in 2030 if neighborhood dynamics change — concept drift.

The sentiment model improved from 85% (logistic regression + BoW) to 96% (BERT fine-tuning). But:

* BERT has 110M parameters vs. the logistic regression's ~50K. The computational cost, energy consumption, and latency are dramatically higher.
* BERT's predictions are harder to explain and audit for bias. The simple LR model makes it obvious if "great" correlates with positive — BERT's attribution is far less transparent.
* For a sentiment classification task where 85% is adequate, is the additional 11 pp worth the computational and interpretability costs? This is context-dependent: for a high-volume production system, 11 pp matters; for a researcher's prototype, it may not.

**The "better" model question:**

"Better" in the benchmark sense (higher accuracy on held-out test data from the same distribution) is not the same as "better" in the deployment sense. A "better" model that:

* Consumes 1000× more compute
* Is opaque to auditors
* Fails on underrepresented populations disproportionately
* Requires expensive infrastructure

...may be worse in practice for many use cases. The sophistication to distinguish these senses — and to resist the reflex of always deploying the highest-accuracy model — is one of the most important things a machine learning practitioner can develop.

### Chapter Summary

The field has shifted from task-specific models to foundation models that adapt broadly.

Key ideas:

* **Foundation models** : large-scale self-supervised pretraining + adaptation; in-context learning; emergent capabilities at scale
* **Multimodal AI** : CLIP aligns vision and language; Sora generates video; AlphaFold solves protein structure — the frontier is across modalities
* **AI safety and alignment** : RLHF improves alignment; scalable oversight, constitutional AI, and mechanistic interpretability are active research directions
* **Open problems** : sample efficiency gap, systematic compositionality, hallucination, causal reasoning, interpretability
* **The practitioner's challenge** : technical expertise is necessary but not sufficient; domain expertise, ethical reasoning, and deployment judgment are equally important

* * *

### Epilogue: The Two Threads, Completed

**Thread A — House Price Prediction** began with a 500-house dataset and a simple linear model (Chapter 5). Regularization (Chapter 6) reduced overfitting. Decision trees captured nonlinear patterns (Chapter 9). Ensemble methods aggregated many weak learners (Chapter 10). Neural networks learned complex feature interactions (Chapter 15). Feature engineering revealed which hand-crafted representations mattered most (Chapter 22).

| Model | RMSE | R² |
|---|---|---|
| Linear Regression | 34,500 | 0.73 |
| Decision Tree | 31,200 | 0.78 |
| Random Forest | 27,100 | 0.83 |
| XGBoost | 23,800 | 0.87 |
| MLP (neural network) | 26,200 | 0.84 |
| XGBoost + feature engineering | 21,200 | 0.89 |
  
**Thread B — Sentiment Analysis** began with bag-of-words and logistic regression (Chapter 7), achieving 85%. Word embeddings improved feature quality (Chapter 19). LSTMs modeled sequential dependencies (Chapter 17). The Transformer's self-attention captured global context (Chapter 20). BERT fine-tuning transferred pretraining knowledge from hundreds of millions of sentences (Chapter 21).

Model | Accuracy  
---|---  
BoW + Logistic Regression | 85%  
GloVe avg. + Logistic Regression | 88%  
LSTM (random embeddings) | 91%  
LSTM (GloVe embeddings) | 93%  
Transformer | 94%  
BERT fine-tuning | 96.1%  
  
Every improvement in both threads came from the same source: a better inductive bias — a structural assumption about the problem that matched reality more closely. Linear regression assumes linearity. Decision trees assume axis-aligned splits matter. CNNs assume spatial locality and translation invariance. Transformers assume that any position can directly attend to any other. BERT assumes that the meaning of a sentence can be recovered from its context.

Each assumption was a hypothesis about how the world works. Each experiment was a test of that hypothesis. This is science, applied to function approximation. It is what machine learning has always been, from Rosenblatt's perceptron to GPT-4: a disciplined search for the right structural assumptions, validated by prediction.

The search continues.
